On the number of quadratic polynomials with a given portrait
Ho Chung Siu

Abstract
. Let F F be a number field. Given a quadratic polynomial f c ​ ( z ) = z 2 + c ∈ F ⁡ [ z ] f_{c}(z)=z^{2}+c\in F[z] , we can construct a directed graph Preper ⁡ ( f c , F ) \mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F) (also called a portrait), whose vertices are F F -rational preperiodic points for f c f_{c} , with an edge α → β \alpha\to\beta if and only if f c ​ ( α ) = β f_{c}(\alpha)=\beta . Poonen [ 45 ] and Faber [ 21 ] classified the portraits that occur for infinitely many c c ’s. Given a portrait P P , we prove an asymptotic formula for counting the number of c ∈ F c\in F ’s by height, such that Preper ⁡ ( f c , F ) ≅ P \mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\cong P . We also prove an asymptotic formula for the analogous counting problem, where Preper ⁡ ( f c , K ) ≅ P \mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P for some quadratic extension K / F K/F . These results are conditioned on Morton-Silverman conjecture [ 39 ] .



1.
Introduction

Let
F
be a number field. For
c\in F
, the quadratic polynomial

f_{c}(z)=z^{2}+c

is an endomorphism of the affine line
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F}
. Via iteration, we may view it as a dynamical system on
\mathbb{A}^{1}(F)=F
. The set of
F
-rational preperiodic points - those with finite forward orbit under
f_{c}
, will be denoted by
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)
. We equip this set with the structure of a directed graph (called the
preperiodic portrait
or just
portrait
) by drawing an arrow from
x\to f_{c}(x)
for each
x\in\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)
.

In this setup, a special case of Morton-Silverman conjecture
[
39
]
is as follows.

Conjecture 1.1
.

Fix a number field
F
. For a given integer
d\geq 1
, there is a uniform bound
N=N(F,d)
on the size of preperiodic points

\#\,\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\leq N

across all number fields
K/F
of degree
d
, and polynomials
f_{c}(z)=z^{2}+c\in K[z]
.

A consequence of Morton-Silverman conjecture is that there are only finitely many possible portraits
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)
across all number fields
K/F
of degree
d
.

Although the conjecture asserts uniformity over all number fields
K/F
of the same degree
d
, Conjecture
1.1

is not known even for the simplest case of
d=1
and
F=\mathbb{Q}
. However, we do know what portraits
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},\mathbb{Q})
can show up for infinitely many
c\in\mathbb{Q}
’s, as recorded in the following theorem.

Figure 1.
Portraits of
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},\mathbb{Q})
occurring infinitely often, excluding the empty portrait (Picture from
[
21
]
).

Theorem 1.2

(Poonen
[
45
]
, Faber
[
21
]
, Doyle
[
17
, Proposition 4.2]
)
.

The portraits that occur as
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},\mathbb{Q})
for infinitely many
c\in\mathbb{Q}
’s are exactly

\emptyset,4(1,1),4(2),6(1,1),6(2),6(3),8(2,1,1).

See Figure
1
for pictures of these graphs.

More generally, fix a number field
F
. If there are infinitely many
c\in F
’s such that
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\cong P
, then
P
is isomorphic to one of the following graphs in
\mathscr{P}_{1}:=\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}
, where

\Gamma_{0}:=\{\emptyset,4(1,1),4(2),6(1,1),6(2),6(3),8(2,1,1)\},

\Gamma_{1}:=\{8(1,1)a,8(1,1)b,8(2)a,8(2)b,10(2,1,1)a,10(2,1,1)b\}.

See Appendix
A
for pictures of these graphs.

The purpose of this paper is to understand how often each portrait occurs. To make things precise, we will use the Weil height: if
c=\frac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{Q}
where
a,b
are coprime integers, we define the (absolute, multiplicative) Weil height
H(c)
to be

H(c)=\max\{|a|,|b|\}

This can be extended to
H:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to[1,\infty)
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Fix a number field
F
and let
H:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute multiplicative Weil height. Consider the portraits
\mathscr{P}_{1}
defined in Theorem
1.2
.

For each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
, define

S_{F,1}(P,B):=\#\{c\in F:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\cong P,H(c)\leq B\}

Assume the Morton-Silverman conjecture for the number field
F
. Then for each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
, we have an asymptotic formula of the form

S_{F,1}(P,B)\sim c_{F,1}(P)B^{a_{F,1}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,1}(P)}

as
B\to\infty
.

(The constants
a_{F,1},b_{F,1},c_{F,1}
are defined in Definition
5.1
. The theorem with an explicit error term is restated and proved at Theorem
5.2
.)

An example of this theorem for the portrait
8(2,1,1)
can be found at Section
4.1
.

Remark
.

The shape of the main term and the error term in the asymptotic formula would depend on the geometry (e.g. genus) of the dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(P)
. Hence we defer the precise result to Definition
5.1
and Theorem
5.2
.

In particular, we can give an interpretation of
a_{F,1},b_{F,1},c_{F,1}
in terms of the geometry/number theory of
X_{1}(P)
and the natural forgetful map
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

By the definition of
a_{F,1}
, it is immediate that
a_{F,1}(\emptyset)>a_{F,1}(P)
for any
P\neq\emptyset
. We hence obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 1.4
.

Fix a number field
F/\mathbb{Q}
, and assume the Morton-Silverman conjecture for the number field
F
.

When ordering
c\in F
by height, 100% of quadratic polynomials
f_{c}(z)=z^{2}+c
has no
F
-rational preperiodic point.

This corollary is already known unconditionally by Sadek
[
46
, Theorem 4.8]
and Le Boudec-Mavraki
[
6
, Theorem 1.1]
. Our results here can be seen as a refinement of their results, since we can compare the frequency of occurrence for portraits of density 0 (although we need to assume Morton-Silverman conjecture). We also remark that Olechnowicz computed
c_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P)
for
P=4(1,1),4(2)
in a related line of investigation
[
41
, Theorem 8.3]
, using methods similar to those in Section 4.

Remark
.

The analogous problem for elliptic curves: counting by height the number of elliptic curves with a prescribed rational torsion group, was studied extensively in recent years. We refer readers to
[
26
]
,
[
12
, Corollary 1.3.6]
for results over
\mathbb{Q}
, and
[
7
]

[
31
]

[
43
]
for results over general number fields. See also
[
20
]
for the general setup and conjectures when counting points on stacks by height.

Our situation is nicer: there is an analogue of modular curves in our situation (dynamical modular curves), which are smooth projective curves instead of stacks. Existing results in counting rational points on smooth projective curves by height would apply. As a result, we can give a geometric interpretation of the leading coefficient
c_{F,1}(P)
in the asymptotics formula.

For a fixed degree
d\geq 1
, Morton-Silverman’s conjecture is uniform across all number fields
K/F
of degree
d
. Hence when counting points by height, it is natural to consider all number fields
K/F
of degree
d
altogether. We ask

Question 1.5
.

Fix a number field
F
, an integer
d\geq 1
, and a portrait
P
. When is

\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $d$ containing $c$}\}

an infinite set? When this set is infinite, how quickly does

\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $d$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

grow, as
B\to\infty
?

The case of
d=1
was answered by Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
.

For
d=2
, the analogue of Theorem
1.2
was recently obtained by Doyle-Krumm
[
18
]
.

Theorem 1.6

(Doyle-Krumm,
[
18
, Theorem 1.5]
)
.

Fix a number field
F
. If there are infinitely many
c\in\overline{F}
’s such that

\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P

for some
K/F
of degree 2 containing
c
, then
P
is isomorphic to one of the following graphs in
\mathscr{P}_{2}:=\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
.

\Gamma_{0}:=\{\emptyset,4(1,1),4(2),6(1,1),6(2),6(3),8(2,1,1)\}

\Gamma_{1}:=\{8(1,1)a,8(1,1)b,8(2)a,8(2)b,10(2,1,1)a,10(2,1,1)b\}

\Gamma_{2}:=\{8(3),8(4),10(3,1,1),10(3,2)\}

See Appendix
A
for pictures of these graphs.

Remark
.

Although the theorem was only stated for
\mathbb{Q}
in
[
18
, Theorem 1.5]
, the proof there works verbatim for any number field
F
.

Remark
.

\Gamma_{g}
are exactly the portraits
P
whose dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(P)
has genus
g
. In particular, a consequence of the theorem is that if
X_{1}(P)
has genus
\geq 3
, then
X_{1}(P)
has finitely many quadratic points over
F
.

Conditional on Morton-Silverman conjecture, our second theorem counts these portraits for
d=2
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Fix a number field
F/\mathbb{Q}
and let
H:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute multiplicative Weil height. Consider the portraits
\mathscr{P}_{2}
defined in Theorem
1.6
.

For each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
, define

S_{F,2}(P,B):=\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}.

Assume the Morton-Silverman conjecture for ground field
F
and
d=2
. Then for each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
, we have an asymptotic formula of the form

S_{F,2}(P,B)\sim c_{F,2}(P)B^{a_{F,2}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,2}(P)}

as
B\to\infty
.

(The constants
a_{F,2},b_{F,2},c_{F,2}
are defined in Definition
6.1
. The theorem with an explicit error term is restated and proved at Theorem
6.2
.)

An example of this theorem for the portrait
8(2,1,1)
can be found at Section
4.2
.

Remark
.

The shape of the main term and the error term in the asymptotic formula would depend on the geometry (e.g. genus) of the dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(P)
. Hence we defer the precise result to Definition
6.1
and Theorem
6.2
.

In particular, we can give an interpretation of
a_{F,2},b_{F,2},c_{F,2}
in terms of the geometry/number theory of
X_{1}(P)
and the natural forgetful map
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

1.1.
Overview of the proof

The proofs of the main theorems rely on the notion of dynamical modular curve. For a portrait
P
, we can define a smooth projective curve
X_{1}(P)
that parametrizes the pair
(c,(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}))
, where
c\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and
(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
transforms under
f_{c}
as prescribed by
P
, without degeneracy. We call
X_{1}(P)
a dynamical modular curve. It comes with a natural forgetful map

\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

that only "remembers"
c
and forgets the prescribed points.

Remark
.

The situation is entirely analogous to classical modular curves
X_{1}^{ell}(N)
that parametrizes elliptic curves with a
N
-torsion point. It comes with a forgetful map
X_{1}^{ell}(N)\to X_{1}^{ell}(1)
, which remembers the elliptic curve but forgets the torsion point.

Rational points on
X_{1}(P)
encodes
f_{c}
’s whose preperiodic portrait
contains

P
. Assuming Morton-Silverman conjecture, there are only finitely many portraits
P^{\prime}
containing
P
where
X_{1}(P^{\prime})
has a rational point. By the principle of inclusion-exclusion, our theorems amount to understanding:

•

(Case of
d=1
) For a fixed number field
F
, the growth rate of

N_{F,1}(P,B):=\#\{x\in X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\}

as
B\to\infty
.

•

(Case of
d=2
) For a fixed number field
F
, the growth rate of

N_{F,2}(P,B):=\#\{x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(x):F]\leq 2,H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\}

as
B\to\infty
.

In the case of
d=1
, Faltings’ theorem tells us that if genus of
X_{1}(P)
is at least 2, the number of
F
-rational points is finite, hence bounded independent of
B
. We thus focus our attention to the cases where
X_{1}(P)
has genus 0 or 1. In both cases
N_{F,1}(P,B)
can be computed: for genus 0, this is a known case of Batyrev-Manin-Peyre Conjectures for
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, proved by Franke-Manin-Tschinkel
[
24
]
; for genus 1, this is due to Néron
[
40
]
.

In the case of
d=2
, we use a similar strategy on the symmetric product
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)
. Faltings’ theorem has an analogue in this situation
[
23
]
, and one can show that if genus of
X_{1}(P)
is at least 3, the number of quadratic points is finite, hence bounded independent of
B

[
18
, Theorem 1.5]
. We thus focus on the portraits
P
where
X_{1}(P)
has genus
\leq 2
, and we are tasked to count rational points on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)
by height. Again we can compute
N_{F,2}(P,B)
: our tools are Franke-Manin-Tschinkel’s theorem
[
24
]
applied to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, some calculus on arithmetic surfaces, and Néron’s theorem
[
40
]
applied to abelian surfaces.

1.2.
Outline of the paper

In Section 2, we recall basic facts of dynamical modular curve and set up our notations.

In Section 3, we recall the basic theory of heights via metrized line bundles, and set up our notations/conventions around absolute values and heights. We then discuss the Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures
[
3
]
[
42
]
, which will allow us to interpret the leading constants
c_{F,i}
in our asymptotic formulas, in terms of the degree of the forgetful map
\pi_{P}
and a Tamagawa measure on
X_{1}(P)
. Finally, we document two known results of counting rational points by height: Franke-Manin-Tschinkel
[
24
]
and Néron
[
40
]
.

In Section 4, we illustrate Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.7
for the portrait
8(2,1,1)
over
\mathbb{Q}
. We will also prove Theorem
1.3
directly for this portrait. This makes it clear how general point counting result comes in, as well as where we would need to assume Morton-Silverman conjecture. Readers are suggested to read Section 4 first before reading the rest of the paper.

In Section 5, we prove Theorem
1.3
(see Theorem
5.2
). The main input is an asymptotic formula for
N_{F,1}(P,B)
as
B\to\infty
(Theorem
5.5
), using Franke-Manin-Tschinkel
[
24
]
and Néron
[
40
]
.

In Section 6, we prove Theorem
1.7
(see Theorem
6.2
). The main input is an asymptotic formula for
N_{F,2}(P,B)
as
B\to\infty
(Theorem
6.7
). Specifically for the case of symmetric square of genus 1 curves, we will need some calculus on arithmetic surfaces; the full details in that case would be in Appendix
B
.

1.3.
Notations

We will use
f=O_{X,Y,Z}(g)
or
f\ll_{X,Y,Z}g
to mean that

|f|\leq C_{X,Y,Z}|g|

for some constant
C_{X,Y,Z}>0
depending on
X,Y,Z
.

We will use
f\asymp_{X,Y,Z}g
to mean
f\ll_{X,Y,Z}g
and
g\ll_{X,Y,Z}f
.

1.4.
Acknowledgements

We thank Trevor Hyde and Simon Rubinstein-Salzedo for feedback on the draft of this paper. Special thanks to Trevor Hyde for introducing the author to arithmetic dynamics and various discussions.

2.
Preliminaries on dynamical modular curves

We recollect the definition of dynamical modular curves and their basic properties. This sets up our notations for dynamical modular curves and their forgetful maps. Our main reference is
[
16
]
.

2.1.
Dynatomic modular curves

Let
F
be a number field, and
N
be a positive integer. Let
f_{c}(z)\in F[z]
be defined by

f_{c}(z)=z^{2}+c.

Suppose
c,x\in F
are such that
x
has period
N
for
f_{c}(z)
, i.e.

f_{c}^{N}(x)=x

and for all
k<N
,
f_{c}^{k}(x)\neq x
. To pick out the
x
’s with exactly period
N
, we define the
N
-th dynatomic polynomial
to be

\Phi_{N}(c,z):=\prod_{n|N}\left(f_{c}^{n}(z)-z\right)^{\mu(N/n)}.

That
\Phi_{N}
is actually a polynomial is shown in
[
47
, Theorem 4.5]
. There is a natural factorization,

f_{c}^{N}(z)-z=\prod_{n|N}\Phi_{N}(c,z).

More generally, let
M,N\geq 1
be integers, and suppose
c,x\in F
are such that
x
has preperiod
M
and eventual period
N
for
f_{c}(z)
, i.e.

f_{c}^{M+N}(x)=f_{c}^{M}(x)

and for
1\leq k\leq M,1\leq l\leq N
,

f_{c}^{k+l}(x)\neq f_{c}^{k}(x),

unless
k=M
and
l=N
. Note that
(c,x)
satisfies

\Phi_{N}(c,f_{c}^{M}(x))=0,

but points with preperiod
\leq M
and eventual period
N
also satisfy the same equation. To pick out the
z
’s with preperiod exactly
M
and eventual period exactly
N
, we define the
generalized dynatomic polynomial
to be

\Phi_{M,N}(c,z):=\frac{\Phi_{N}(c,f_{c}^{M}(z))}{\Phi_{N}(c,f_{c}^{M-1}(z))}.

That
\Phi_{M,N}
is actually a polynomial is shown in
[
29
]
.

The vanishing locus of
\Phi_{N}
(resp.
\Phi_{M,N}
) defines an affine curve
Y_{1}(N)
(resp.
Y_{1}(M,N)
), which we refer to as a dynatomic modular curve. We denote
X_{1}(\cdot)
to be the smooth projective model of the curve
Y_{1}(\cdot)
, and we also refer to
X_{1}(\cdot)
as a dynatomic modular curve. These modular curves are the key examples of dynamical modular curves, that we will introduce in the next section.

2.2.
Dynamical modular curve associated to directed graphs

Let
G
be a finite directed graph. Fix an ordering of vertices
V(G)
, denoted as
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}
. We can then define
the dynamical modular curve associated to
G
, denoted
Y_{1}(G)
, as follows. This is the affine curve that parametrizes the tuple
(c,z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})
, where
c\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
(z_{1},\cdots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{A}^{n}
, and that
f_{c}(z_{i})=z_{j}
if and only if
v_{i}
has an edge towards to
v_{j}
, and
z_{i}
’s do not "degenerate"; see
[
16
, Section 2.4]
for a precise definition of
Y_{1}(G)
. We denote
X_{1}(G)
to be the smooth projective model of the curve
Y_{1}(G)
.

For
Y_{1}(G)
to be non-empty, one needs to impose restrictions on
G
.

(1)

Each vertex has at most one outward edge, since
f_{c}(x)
is well-defined for any given
x
; with our focus on preperiodic points, it does not hurt to assume each vertex has exactly one outward edge.

(2)

Each vertex has at most two inward edges:
f_{c}(z)
is quadratic in
z
, so for any given
c,a
,
f_{c}(z)=a
has at most two solutions.

Generically, we should expect each vertex to have either no inward edge or exactly two inward edges, as long as no double roots occur.

(3)

For any
N\geq 1
, the number of cycles of length
N
is bounded. Indeed, any period
N
point for
f_{c}
is a root of
\Phi_{N}(c,z)=0
; moreover, if
z
is a period
N
point, so is
f_{c}^{k}(z)
for
0<k<N
.
N
-cycles either coincide or do not overlap at all. Hence if we set

D(N):=\mathrm{deg}_{z}\Phi_{N}(c,z)=\sum_{n|N}\mu(N/n)2^{n},

and

R(N):=\frac{D(N)}{N},

then there are
\leq R(N)
cycles of length
N
.

(4)

There are at most 2 fixed points (vertex with an edge to itself). This is because
f_{c}(z)
is quadratic in
z
, so for any given
c
,
f_{c}(z)=z
has at most two solutions.

Generically, we should expect either no or exactly two fixed points, as long as no double roots occur.

With these restrictions in mind, we define

Definition 2.1
.

A finite directed graph is called
strongly admissible
if it satisfies the following properties:

(a)

Each vertex of
G
has out-degree 1 and in-degree either 0 or 2.

(b)

For each
N\geq 2
,
G
contains at most
R(N)

N
-cycles.

(c)

G
contains either 0 or 2 fixed points.

Remark
.

The definition of strong admissibility is taken from
[
16
, Definition 2.3]
. We sometimes quote results from
[
18
]
, where "generic quadratic" is used synonymously as "strongly admissible".

Remark
.

Given any directed graph
G_{0}
where each vertex has out-degree 1, there is a unique, minimal, strongly admissible graph
G
that contains
G_{0}
, with a canonical isomorphism
X_{1}(G)\cong X_{1}(G_{0})
. Hence it does not hurt to only focus on strongly admissible graphs; see
[
16
]
for more details.

Even if a directed graph
G_{0}
is not strongly admissible, we may abuse notation and refer to dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(G_{0})
, to really mean
X_{1}(G)
.

Remark
.

We only need dynamical modular curve associated to quadratic polynomials in this paper, whose theory was developed in
[
16
]
. More generally one can define dynamical moduli space associated to morphisms on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
; see
[
19
]
.

Example 2.2
.

The cyclic graph
C_{4}
of four vertices is not strongly admissible, because the in-degree of each vertex is 1. The minimal, strongly admissible graph
C_{4}^{\prime}
that contains
C_{4}
, can be obtained by adding vertices and edges, so that the original four vertices of
C_{4}
have in-degree 2. These graphs are depicted in Figure
2
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 2.
Left:
C_{4}
, the cyclic graph of 4 vertices. Right:
C_{4}
’, the minimal strongly admissible graph containing
C_{4}
.

As in previous remark,
X_{1}(C_{4}^{\prime})\cong X_{1}(C_{4})\cong X_{1}(4)
, which recovers the dynatomic modular curve defined by
\Phi_{4}(c,z)
.

More generally,

•

X_{1}(N)
is the dynamical modular curve associated to the directed cyclic graph
C_{N}
of
N
vertices.

•

X_{1}(M,N)
is the dynamical modular curve associated to the directed graph
C_{M,N}
with an
N
-cycle and a tail of length
M
. We show an example of
C_{2,4}
in Figure
3
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 3.
The graph
C_{2,4}
, with a 4-cycle and a tail of length 2.

We summarize properties of dynamical modular curves in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
G
be a strongly admissible directed graph.

(a)

[
16
, Theorem 1.7]

X_{1}(G)
is geometrically irreducible over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(b)

[
16
, Proposition 3.3]
If
H
is another strongly admissible directed graph contained in
G
, then there is a canonical finite map
\pi_{G,H}:X_{1}(G)\to X_{1}(H)
of degree
\geq 2
.

In particular, if
H
is the empty graph,
X_{1}(\emptyset)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and we have the canonical forgetful map

\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

that only "remembers"
c
and forgets the prescribed points.

We will also encounter the automorphisms of
\pi_{G}
in our theorems.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
G
be a strongly admissible directed graph, and
\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the forgetful map. We define the automorphism group of
\pi_{G}
to be

Aut(\pi_{G}):=\{\sigma:X_{1}(G)\to X_{1}(G):\text{$\sigma$ invertible morphism of curves / $\mathbb{Q}$, and }\pi_{G}\circ\sigma=\sigma\}.

2.3.
When
X_{1}(G)
has small genus

Proposition 2.5
.

[
17
, Proposition 4.2]

Let
G
be a strongly admissible directed graph. Then
X_{1}(G)
has genus
\leq 2
if and only if
G
is isomorphic to one of the following graphs in
\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
:

\Gamma_{0}:=\{\emptyset,4(1,1),4(2),6(1,1),6(2),6(3),8(2,1,1)\}

\Gamma_{1}:=\{8(1,1)a,8(1,1)b,8(2)a,8(2)b,10(2,1,1)a,10(2,1,1)b\}

\Gamma_{2}:=\{8(3),8(4),10(3,1,1),10(3,2)\}

Here
\Gamma_{g}
is the set of strongly admissible graphs
G
, where
X_{1}(G)
has genus
g
.

Furthermore,

(a)

If
G\in\Gamma_{0}
, then
X_{1}(G)
is rational over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(b)

If
G\in\Gamma_{1}
, then
X_{1}(G)
is an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(c)

If
G\in\Gamma_{2}
, then
X_{1}(G)
is a smooth projective genus 2 curve over
\mathbb{Q}
.

For each of the graphs above, see Appendix
A
for a model of
X_{1}(G)
, their Cremona label/LMFDB label, a presentation of the forgetful map
\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and the automorphism group
Aut(\pi_{G})
.

3.
Preliminaries on counting rational points by height

3.1.
Weil heights

We recollect some basic facts about Weil heights, and record the convention around absolute values and heights in this paper. More details can be found in
[
5
, Chapter 1]
.

By Ostrowski’s theorem, the only non-trivial absolute values (up to equivalence) on
\mathbb{Q}
are:

•

|\cdot|_{p}
, where
p
is a prime. This is defined as: if
x=p^{k}\frac{a}{b}\in\mathbb{Q}
for coprime integers
a,b
satisfying
gcd(p,ab)=1
, we define

|x|_{p}=p^{-k}.

•

|\cdot|_{\infty}
, the usual absolute value on
\mathbb{R}
restricted to
\mathbb{Q}
.

We denote the set of equivalence classes of non-trivial absolute values on
\mathbb{Q}
, also called places of
\mathbb{Q}
, as
M_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Now let
F
be a number field over
\mathbb{Q}
of degree
d=[F:\mathbb{Q}]
. A place
v
on
F
is an absolute value
F\to[0,\infty)
whose restriction to
\mathbb{Q}
is a power of either
|\cdot|_{p}
or
|\cdot|_{\infty}
. Denote
F_{v}
as the completion of
F
with respect to
v
. An absolute value is normalized if

|x|_{v}=|N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(x)|_{p},

if
v
lies above
|\cdot|_{p}
. These normalized absolute values satisfy the product formula: for
x\in F^{\times}
,

\prod_{v\in M_{F}}|x|_{v}=1,

where we denote
M_{F}
as the set of places on
F
. In this paper, we may use "place" and "normalized absolute value" synonymously. We use
M_{F,finite}
to denote the non-archimedean places lying over
|\cdot|_{p}
, and use
M_{F,\infty}
to denote the archimedean places lying over
|\cdot|_{\infty}
.

Let
v
be a place, and
F_{v}
be the completion of
F
with respect to
v
. Sometimes we need to extend
|\cdot|_{v}
to
\overline{F_{v}}
, defined as follows. For
y\in\overline{F_{v}}
, define

|y|_{v}:=|N_{F_{v}(y)/F_{v}}(y)|_{v}^{1/[F_{v}(y):F_{v}]}.

When restricted to a finite extension of
F_{v}
, this gives an unnormalized extension of the absolute value
|\cdot|_{v}
.

A height on
\mathbb{P}^{n}(F)
can be defined by

H_{F/\mathbb{Q}}([x_{0},\cdots,x_{n}])=\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\max\{|x_{0}|_{v},\cdots,|x_{n}|_{v}\}^{1/[F:\mathbb{Q}]}.

One can check that if
F^{\prime}/F
is a finite extension of number fields, and
x\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(F)\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}(F^{\prime})
, then

H_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(x)=H_{F^{\prime}/\mathbb{Q}}(x).

Hence
H_{\cdot/\mathbb{Q}}
glues to the absolute multiplicative Weil height
H:\mathbb{P}^{n}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\to[1,\infty)
.

3.2.
Haar measures

Our choice of Haar measure is consistent with that in Peyre
[
42
]
.

Let
F
be a number field, and
v\in M_{F}
be a place. Let
F_{v}
be the completion of
F
at
v
,
O_{F_{v}}
be the ring of integers of
F_{v}
, and
O_{F_{v}}^{\times}
be the units of
O_{F_{v}}
.

•

The additive Haar measure
dx_{v}
on
F_{v}
satisfies

dx_{v}(aU)=|a|_{v}dx_{v}(U),

for a measurable set
U
and
a\in F_{v}
. The measure is normalized such that

–

If
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{R}
,
dx_{v}
is the usual Lebesgue measure.

–

If
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{C}
,
dx_{v}
is twice the Lebesgue measure.

–

If
v
is non-archimedean,

dx_{v}(O_{F_{v}})=1.

•

The multiplicative Haar measure
d^{\times}x_{v}
on
F_{v}^{\times}
is a multiple of
\dfrac{dx_{v}}{|x_{v}|_{v}}
. We normalize it such that

–

If
v
is archimedean,

d^{\times}x_{v}=\frac{dx_{v}}{|x_{v}|_{v}}.

–

If
v
is non-archimedean,

d^{\times}x_{v}(O_{F_{v}}^{\times})=1.

3.3.
Heights and metrized line bundles

We recall the definition of metrized line bundles for a projective variety over a number field
F
, and recall how heights can be defined via metrized line bundles. We generally follow the exposition of
[
9
]
; more details can be found in
[
5
, Chapter 2.7]
.

Let
F
be a number field. Let
V
be a projective variety over
F
, and let
L
be a line bundle on
V
.

Definition 3.1

(
v
-adic metric on
L
)
.

Fix a place
v\in M_{F}
. A
v
-adic metric
on
L
is a choice of
v
-adic norms
\|\cdot\|_{v}
on each fiber
L(x)
that varies continuously over
x\in V(\overline{F_{v}})
, i.e. for each
x\in V(\overline{F_{v}})
,
s_{x}\in L(x)
and
\lambda\in\overline{F_{v}}
, we have

\|\lambda s_{x}\|_{v}=|\lambda|_{v}\|s_{x}\|_{v}.

Moreover, if
U\subset V
is an open subset and
s\in\Gamma(U,L)
is any section of
L
over
U
, we require

x\to\|s(x)\|_{v}

to be a continuous function on
U(\overline{F_{v}})
.

Definition 3.2

(Adelic metric on
L
)
.

An
adelic metric
on
L
is a family of compatible
v
-adic metrics for each
v\in M_{F}
.

Compatibility is defined as follows: let
U\subset V
be an affine open subset, and pick any identification of
U
as a Zariski-closed subset of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
over
F
. For each non-archimedean place
v\in M_{F}
, we can consider the integral points
U(O_{F_{v}})=U(F)\cap O_{F_{v}}^{n}
. One can check that for any two identifications of
U
as Zariski closed subsets of
\mathbb{A}^{n}
,
U(O_{F_{v}})
is the same up to finitely many exceptions of
v
’s.

The adelic compatibility condition we need is then: for any affine open
U
, any non-vanishing section
s\in\Gamma(U,L)
, we have for all but finitely many
v\in M_{F}
,

\|s(x)\|_{v}\equiv 1

for all
x\in U(O_{F_{v}})
.

Definition 3.3

(Metrized line bundles)
.

A
metrized line bundle

\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
on a projective variety
V
over
F
, is a line bundle
L
with a specified adelic metric
\|\cdot\|
.

\mathcal{L}
is said to be
ample
if the underlying line bundle
L
is ample.

Definition 3.4

(Height on
V(F)
induced by metrized line bundle)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
be a metrized line bundle on a projective variety
V
over
F
.

For any
x\in V(F)
, take any non-vanishing section
s\in\Gamma(U,L)
over some open subset
U
containing
x
. We define the height (relative to
F
) to be

H_{\mathcal{L},F,s}(x):=\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\|s(x)\|_{v}^{-1}.

We record the properties of heights in the following proposition.

Proposition 3.5
.

(
[
9
, Section 2.4]
)
The height induced by metrized line bundles satisfies:

(a)

Height is well-defined: it does not depend on the open subset
U
and the section
s\in\Gamma(U,L)
chosen. We hence use
H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)
to denote this relative height.

(b)

If
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are two metrized line bundles on
V
over
F
, with the same underlying line bundle, then there exists constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that for all
x\in V(F)
,

c_{1}H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq H_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime},F}(x)\leq c_{2}H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x).

(c)

(Northcott property) Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
be an ample metrized line bundle on
V
over
F
. The set

\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq B\}

is finite, for any given
B
.

(d)

(Product) If
\mathcal{L}_{1}=(L_{1},\|\cdot\|_{1})
,
\mathcal{L}_{2}=(L_{2},\|\cdot\|_{2})
are two metrized line bundles on
V
over
F
, we can form their tensor product of the metrized line bundle as follows.

On the line bundle
L=L_{1}\otimes L_{2}
, there is a unique adelic metric
\|\cdot\|
such that: for any
v\in M_{F}
, any open
U\subset V
, any
x\in U(F_{v})
, and sections
s_{1}\in\Gamma(U,L_{1})
,
s_{2}\in\Gamma(U,L_{2})
, we have

\|(s_{1}\otimes s_{2})(x)\|_{v}=\|s_{1}(x)\|_{1,v}\|s_{2}(x)\|_{2,v}.

Moreover, the induced height satisfies:

H_{\mathcal{L}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{2},F}(x)=H_{\mathcal{L}_{1},F}(x)H_{\mathcal{L}_{2},F}(x)

for any
x\in V(F)
.

(e)

(Pullback) Let
V^{\prime}
be another variety over
F
, and
f:V^{\prime}\to V
be a morphism over
F
. Let
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|_{V})
be a metrized line bundle on
V
.

We can define the metrized line bundle
f^{*}L
on
V^{\prime}
as follows. On the pullback line bundle
f^{*}L
, there is a unique adelic metric
\|\cdot\|_{V^{\prime}}
such that: for any
v\in M_{F}
, any open
U\subset V
, any
x\in f^{-1}(U)(F_{v})
, and section
s\in\Gamma(U,L)
, we have

\|f^{*}s(x)\|_{V^{\prime},v}=\|s(f(x))\|_{V,v}.

Moreover, the induced height satisfies:

H_{f^{*}\mathcal{L},F}(x)=H_{\mathcal{L},F}(f(x))

for any
x\in V^{\prime}(F)
.

(f)

If
F^{\prime}/F
is a finite extension of number fields, one may consider the base change
{\mathcal{L}_{F^{\prime}}=(L_{F^{\prime}},\|\cdot\|_{L,F^{\prime}})}
. The
\|\cdot\|_{L,F^{\prime}}
is defined as follows: if
w\in M_{F^{\prime}}
lying over
v\in M_{F}
, let
d_{w|v}:=[F^{\prime}_{w}:F_{v}]
, and define

\|\cdot\|_{L,F^{\prime},w}:=\|\cdot\|_{v}^{d_{w|v}}.

For
x\in V(F)\subset V(F^{\prime})
, the base change satisfies

H_{\mathcal{L}_{F^{\prime}},F^{\prime}}(x)=H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)^{[F^{\prime}:F]}.

Hence we can define the
absolute multiplicative height
for
x\in V(\overline{F})
by

H_{\mathcal{L}}(x):=H_{\mathcal{L}_{F^{\prime}},F^{\prime}}(x)^{1/[F^{\prime}:\mathbb{Q}]}

if
F^{\prime}/F
is any number field such that
x\in V(F^{\prime})
.

Example 3.6

(Standard height on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
)
.

Let
F
be a number field, and consider
\mathbb{P}^{n}
over
F
with the line bundle
O(1)
. Let
s_{0},\cdots,s_{n}
be the standard basis of global sections
\Gamma(\mathbb{P}^{n},O(1))
: on open set
U_{j}:=\{x_{j}\neq 0\}
, the section
s_{i}
is defined by

s_{i}(x):=\frac{x_{i}}{x_{j}}.

One can define an adelic metric on
O(1)
, such that for any
x\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(F)
, and
s\in\Gamma(\mathbb{P}^{n},O(1))
,

\|s(x)\|_{v}=\frac{|s(x)|_{v}}{\max\{|s_{0}(x)|_{v},\cdots,|s_{n}(x)|_{v}\}}.

We will call this the standard metric on
O(1)
, and denote the metrized line bundle as
\mathbf{O(1)}
.

One can check that the absolute height induced from this metric is exactly the absolute multiplicaive Weil height: for
x\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(\overline{F})
,

H_{\mathbf{O(1)}}(x)=\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\max\{|x_{0}|_{v},\cdots,|x_{n}|_{v}\}^{1/[F:\mathbb{Q}]}.

Abusing notation, we may omit
\mathbf{O(1)}
and just use
H(x)
to denote the absolute Weil height on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, as before.

Example 3.7

(Standard height on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
)
.

We first start off with some properties of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. A reference for this example is le Rudulier’s thesis
[
33
, Section 4 and Section 6]
.

Let
F
be a number field, and consider
\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
.
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
acts on
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
by swapping coordinates. There is a natural quotient map,

\pi:(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}.

On the other hand, there is a map
\sigma:(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
that sends

\sigma([a_{1},b_{1}],[a_{2},b_{2}])=[a_{1}a_{2},a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1},b_{1}b_{2}].

Lemma 3.8

(Properties of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
)
.

[
33
, Section 4]

(a)

There is a unique isomorphism

\epsilon:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}

defined over
F
, such that
\sigma=\epsilon\circ\pi
.

(b)

Let
p_{i}:(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the i-th coordinate projection map, and let

O(1,1)=p_{1}^{*}O(1)\otimes p_{2}^{*}O(1)

be the line bundle on
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
. We denote
O(1,1)
metrized with the pullback/product metric as
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
.

There is a unique metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that

\pi^{*}\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{O(1,1)},

with the underlying line bundle
L=\epsilon^{*}O_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1)
. Moreover, the induced height satisfies

H_{(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2},\mathbf{O(1,1)}}\left(([a_{1},b_{1}],[a_{2},b_{2}])\right)=H_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbf{O(1)}}([a_{1},b_{1}])H_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbf{O(1)}}([a_{2},b_{2}]).

The line bundle (resp. metric, height) in the lemma would be called the
standard line bundle (resp. metric, height)
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
; by abuse of notation, we may denote this line bundle on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
also as
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
. When we consider the pushforward of this line bundle to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we will call the line bundle (resp. metric, height) the
product line bundle (resp. metric, height)
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

To make things concrete, we write down how the product metric on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is defined in a special case
[
33
, Section 6]
. Let
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}
be the coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Let
U_{0}:=\{x_{0}\neq 0\}
, and
s_{0}=\frac{x_{0}}{x_{0}}
be the section
\equiv 1
on
U_{0}
.

For a place
v
and a point
x\in U_{0}(F)
, the product metric is

\|s_{0}(x)\|_{v}=\prod_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\in\overline{F_{v}}}}{{x_{0}a^{2}-x_{1}a+x_{2}=0}}}\max\{1,|a|_{v}\}^{-1}.

Example 3.9

(Heights on
X_{1}(G)
)
.

Let
F
be a number field. Let
G
be a strongly admissible directed graph, and
X_{1}(G)
be the associated dynamical modular curve defined over
\mathbb{Q}
(hence
F
).

Recall from Proposition
2.3
that we have a forgetful map

\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}.

Consider the line bundle
\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}/F
with standard metric, and pull this back to the metrized line bundle
\pi_{G}^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
on
X_{1}(G)
. This induces a height on
X_{1}(G)(\overline{F})
:

H_{\pi_{G}^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}(x)=H_{\mathbf{O(1)}}(\pi_{G}(x))=H(\pi_{G}(x)),

for any
x\in X_{1}(G)(\overline{F})
, where
H
is the absolute multiplicative Weil height on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Example 3.10

(Heights on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(G)
)
.

Let
F
be a number field. Let
G
be a strongly admissible directed graph, and
X_{1}(G)
be the associated dynamical modular curve.

From Proposition
2.3
, we have a forgetful map:

\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}.

This induces a corresponding map on the symmetric square:

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{G}:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(G)\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}.

Consider the standard metrized line bundle
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
from Example
3.7
, and pulls it back to
\pi_{G}^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(G)
. This induces a height on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(G)(\overline{F})
.

Concretely, if
P=\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(G)(\overline{F})
corresponds to the degree 2 divisor
[Q_{1}]+[Q_{2}]
on
X_{1}(G)
with
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in X_{1}(G)(\overline{F})
, then

H_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(G),\pi_{G}^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}}(P)=H_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbf{O(1)}}(\pi_{G}(Q_{1}))H_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathbf{O(1)}}(\pi_{G}(Q_{2})).

3.4.
Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures

Let
F
be a number field of degree
d
over
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
V
be a smooth projective variety over number field
F
, and
\mathcal{L}
an ample, metrized line bundle on
V
. This induces the relative height
H_{\mathcal{L},F}
on
V(F)
; Northcott property implies that

\#\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq B\}

is finite, for any given
B
. Let

N(V,\mathcal{L},B):=\#\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq B\}.

When
V
has many
F
-rational points, it is natural to understand how quickly
N(V,\mathcal{L},B)
grows as
B\to\infty
.

For smooth projective Fano varieties
V
over
F
, Batyrev-Manin’s conjectures
[
3
]
(refined by Peyre
[
42
]
for anticanonical line bundle and Batyrev-Tschinkel
[
4
]
for more general
\mathcal{L}
’s) predict that

N(V,\mathcal{L},B)=c_{V}(\mathcal{L})B^{\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})}(\log B)^{\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})-1}(1+o(1))

for
B\to\infty
, for some constants
\alpha,\beta,c
depending only on
(V,\mathcal{L})
. We will review their conjectures below; see surveys
[
50
]
and
[
9
]
for more information.

Remark
.

Since the absolute height satisfies

H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)=H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)^{1/d}

for any
x\in V(F)
, we have

\displaystyle\#\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\,

\displaystyle\#\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq B^{d}\}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{V}(\mathcal{L})B^{d\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})}(d\log B)^{\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})-1}(1+o(1)).

The Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures is known for many kinds of varieties, in particular for
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with any metrized line bundle
[
24
]
. For non-Fano varieties, asymptotic result of this type is sometimes available as well; an example is Néron’s theorem for abelian varieties
[
40
]
.

In the rest of this section, we define the constants
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})
,
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})
and
c_{V}(\mathcal{L})
that shows up in the conjectural asymptotic formula. We will use them to intrepret the constants that show up in Theorem
1.3
and
1.7
. In the next section, we will document known results of Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures and Néron’s theorem. These results will be used to deduce asymptotic formulas for
N_{F,1}(P,B)
and
N_{F,2}(P,B)
mentioned in the introduction.

3.4.1.
Constants
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})
and
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})
.

Let
V
be a smooth projective variety over number field
F
, and
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
an ample metrized line bundle on
V
.

Define:

•

\mathrm{Pic}(V)_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{Pic}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
.

•

\Lambda_{eff}\subset\mathrm{Pic}(V)_{\mathbb{R}}
to be the effective cone, i.e. the closed cone in
\mathrm{Pic}(V)_{\mathbb{R}}
generated by effective divisors on
V
.

•

K_{V}
to be the canonical line bundle on
V
.

Then we can define

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L}):=\mathrm{inf}\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:\alpha L+K_{V}\in\Lambda_{eff}\},

and
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})
be the co-dimension of the minimal face of
\Lambda_{eff}
containing
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}
.

Remark
.

Note that
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})
and
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})
only depends on
L
, and does not depend on the chosen metric on
L
.

Example 3.11
.

For
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
, it is well-known that

Pic(V)\cong\mathbb{Z},

generated by the line bundle
O(1)
. Hence
Pic(V)_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R}
; under this isomorphism
O(1)
corresponds to 1, and
\Lambda_{eff}
corresponds to
[0,\infty)
.

Moreover,
K_{V}\cong O(-n-1)
. Hence for
\mathcal{L}=O(1)
with any metric, we see that

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{inf}\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:\alpha\cdot 1-(n+1)\geq 0\}=n+1.

Since
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}=0
, the minimal face of
\Lambda_{eff}
containing
0
is
0
itself. Thus the co-dimension
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
.

Example 3.12
.

Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}

of degree
k
, and consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
in the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Note that the underlying bundle
L\cong O(k)
.

As in last example,
Pic(V)_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R}
, where
O(1)
corresponds to 1, and
\Lambda_{eff}
corresponds to
[0,\infty)
. Moreover,
K_{V}\cong O(-n-1)
. Hence,

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{inf}\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:\alpha\cdot k-(n+1)\geq 0\}=\frac{n+1}{k}.

Again since
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}=0
, the minimal face of
\Lambda_{eff}
containing
0
is
0
itself, and the co-dimension
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
.

Example 3.13
.

Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

of degree
k
. This induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
in the source
V=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Since
Sym^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
O(1,1)
corresponding to
O(1)
, we see that

•

Pic(V)_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R}
with
O(1,1)
corresponds to 1, and
\Lambda_{eff}
corresponds to
[0,\infty)
.

•

The canonical line bundle
K_{V}
corresponds to
-3
(since it is
O(-3)
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
).

•

\mathcal{L}
corresponds to
k
(since
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f
has degree
k
)

Hence,

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{inf}\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:\alpha\cdot k-3\geq 0\}=\frac{3}{k}.

Again since
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}=0
, the minimal face of
\Lambda_{eff}
containing
0
is
0
itself, and the co-dimension
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
.

3.4.2.
Tamagawa measure induced by metrized line bundle

The definition of
c_{V}(\mathcal{L})
will involve the volume of (closure of)
V(F)
in the adelic space
V(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. Hence we need to first define a measure on
V(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. In this section, we would define such Tamagawa measure. More details can be found in
[
4
, Section 3]
and
[
9
, Section 3.3]
.

Definition 3.14

(
\mathcal{L}
-primitive variety)
.

Let
V
be a smooth projective variety over number field
F
, and
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
be an ample metrized line bundle on
V
.

V
is called
\mathcal{L}
-primitive if
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})>0
is rational, and if there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

D:=k\left(\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}\right)

is a
rigid
effective divisor, i.e. an effective divisor satisfying
\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V}(vD))=1
for all
v>0
.

Example 3.15
.

Let
V
be a Fano variety over number field
F
, whose anticanonical bundle
-K_{V}
is ample. Let
\mathcal{L}
be
-K_{V}
, equipped with the pullback metric from
\mathbf{O(1)}
via the anticanonical embedding.

Now

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\alpha_{V}(-K_{V})=1.

Moreover,
L+K_{V}=0
, and for all
v>0
,

\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V}(v\cdot 0))=\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V})=1.

Hence
V
is
\mathcal{L}
-primitive.

Example 3.16
.

Consider a morphism
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
of degree
k
, and consider
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
in the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Note that the underlying bundle
L\cong O(k)
.

We showed in Example
3.12
that
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{n+1}{k}
. Again,

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}=0.

For all
v>0
,

\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V}(v\cdot 0))=\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V})=1.

Hence
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is
\mathcal{L}
-primitive.

Example 3.17
.

Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

of degree
k
. This induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on the source
V=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

We showed in Example
3.13
that
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{3}{k}
. Again,

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}=0.

Again for all
v>0
,

\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V}(v\cdot 0))=\dim H^{0}(V,O_{V})=1.

Hence
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
\mathcal{L}
-primitive.

Local Tamagawa measures.
Let
V
be a
\mathcal{L}
-primitive variety over
F
, and let
v\in M_{F}
be a place. We now define a local measure on
V(F_{v})
.

By definition of
\mathcal{L}
-primitivity, there is
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

D=k(\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V})

is a rigid effective divisor. By scaling up
k
, we can further assume that
k\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})
is also an integer. Choose a
F
-rational section
g\in H^{0}(V,O_{V}(D))
; by rigidity,
g
is unique up to a multiple of
F^{\times}
. Choose local analytic coordinates
dx_{1,v},\cdots,dx_{n,v}
in a neighborhood
U_{x}
of
x\in V(F_{v})
. We can then write
g
as

g=cs^{k\alpha_{V}(L)}\left(dx_{1,v}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n,v}\right)^{k}

where
s\in\Gamma(U_{x},L)
and
c\in\Gamma(U_{x},O_{V}^{\times})
. This defines a local
v
-adic measure

|c|_{v}^{1/k}\|s\|_{v}^{\alpha_{V}(L)}|dx_{1,v}\cdots dx_{n,v}|

on
U_{x}
; here
|dx_{1,v}\cdots dx_{n,v}|
is the additive Haar measure on
U_{x}
. One checks that this measure is well-defined under change of coordinates, hence it glues to a measure
\omega_{V,L,v,g}
on the entire
V(F_{v})
.

Example 3.18
.

Consider
\mathbb{P}^{1}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, and consider the line bundle
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with the standard metric. We will write down a local Tamagawa measure, and compute the total volume of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v})
for each place
v\in M_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

We saw in Example
3.11
that
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
\mathcal{L}
-primitive with
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=2
. Since

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V}=0,

we can take
k=1
,
D=0
, and
g\in H^{0}(V,O_{V})
be the constant section
g\equiv 1
.

Let
x_{0},x_{1}
be the homogeneous coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and let
U_{i}:=\{x_{i}\neq 0\}
. Let
w=\frac{x_{1}}{x_{0}}
be the local coordinate on
U_{0}
and
z=\frac{x_{0}}{x_{1}}
be the coordinate on
U_{1}
. On
U_{0}\cap U_{1}
, the differentials satisfy

dz=-\frac{dw}{w^{2}}.

Consider the section
s
of
O(1)
corresponding to
x_{0}
, i.e.
s(w)\equiv 1
on
U_{0}
, and
s(z)=z
on
U_{1}
. Then the constant global section
1
can be written as

1=\begin{cases}1\otimes dw&\text{on $U_{0}$, }\\
z^{2}\otimes\left(-\frac{1}{z^{2}}dz\right)=-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\left(z^{2}\otimes dz\right)&\text{on $U_{1}$.}\end{cases}

Hence in local coordinates, the local Tamagawa measure is

\displaystyle w_{V,\mathcal{L},v,g}=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\|1\|_{v}^{2}|dw|&\text{on $U_{0}$,}\\
\left|-\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right|_{v}\|z\|_{v}^{2}|dz|=\|1\|_{v}^{2}|dz|&\text{on $U_{1}$.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\max\{|w|_{v},1\}^{2}}|dw|&\text{on $U_{0}$,}\\
\frac{1}{\max\{|z|_{v},1\}^{2}}|dz|&\text{on $U_{1}$.}\end{cases}

For each place
v
, let us calculate the total volume of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with this Tamagawa measure. Since the point
[0:1]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
has measure 0, it suffices to calculate the volume of
U_{0}
.

For
v=\infty
, we have

w_{V,\mathcal{L},\infty,g}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{R}))=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\max\{|w|,1\}^{2}}|dw|=4.

For each finite prime
p
, recall that the Haar measure is normalized such that
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})=1
. Now,

\displaystyle w_{V,\mathcal{L},p,g}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\frac{1}{\max\{|w|_{p},1\}^{2}}|dw|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{|w|_{p}=p^{i}}\frac{1}{\max\{|w|_{p},1\}^{2}}|dw|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i>0}\mathrm{Vol}(|w|_{p}=p^{i})\cdot\frac{1}{p^{2i}}+\sum_{i\leq 0}\mathrm{Vol}(|w|_{p}=p^{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}(|w|_{p}=1)\sum_{i>0}\frac{1}{p^{i}}+\mathrm{Vol}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\cdot\frac{1/p}{1-1/p}+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{p}.

Note that the "adelic volume" of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, i.e. the infinite product

\prod_{v}w_{V,\mathcal{L},v,g}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{v}))=4\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)

diverges. By rewriting it as

4\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)=4\prod_{p}\frac{1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}}{1-\frac{1}{p}}=\lim_{s\to 1}4\frac{\zeta(s)}{\zeta(2s)},

and taking residue at
s=1
, we can assign the value
\frac{4}{\zeta(2)}
to the diverging product.

In a similar way, we will interpret (divergent) adelic volume when we discuss global Tamagawa measures.

Example 3.19
.

Let
F
be a number field. Consider a morphism
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
over
F
and of degree
k
, and consider
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
in the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

If
x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
are the homogeneous coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, let
U_{i}=\{x_{i}\neq 0\}
. As an example, we calculate the measure on
U_{0}
.

Let
u_{i}=\frac{x_{i}}{x_{0}}
, and let
u_{1},\cdots,u_{n}
be the local coordinates on
U_{0}
. Write

f(u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})=[G_{0}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n}),\cdots,G_{n}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})]

where
G_{0},\cdots,G_{n}\in F[x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}]
are relatively prime, homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
, and that
G_{0},\cdots,G_{n}
has empty common vanishing locus.

As in the last example, we can take
D=0
, and take
g\in H^{0}(V,O_{V})
to be the constant section 1. A similar calculation shows that on
U_{0}
,

w_{V,\mathcal{L},v,g}|_{U_{0}}=\max\{|G_{0}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})|_{v},\cdots|G_{n}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})|_{v}\}^{-(n+1)/k}|du_{1}\cdots du_{n}|

For each place
v
, let us calculate the total volume of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with this Tamagawa measure. One checks that the hyperplane
x_{0}=0
has measure 0, so it suffices to compute volume of
U_{0}
. Hence,

w_{V,\mathcal{L},v,g}(\mathbb{P}^{n}(F_{v}))=\int_{F_{v}^{n}}\max\{|G_{0}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})|_{v},\cdots|G_{n}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})|_{v}\}^{-(n+1)/k}|du_{1}\cdots du_{n}|.

Note that for all but finitely many non-archimedean places
v
, we have

\max\{|G_{0}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})|_{v},\cdots,|G_{n}(1,u_{1},\cdots,u_{n})|_{v}\}^{(n+1)/k}=\max\{1,|u_{1}|_{v},\cdots,|u_{n}|_{v}\}^{n+1}.

It follows that for all but finitely many non-archimedean places
v=v_{\mathfrak{p}}
with corresponding prime
\mathfrak{p}
, we have

w_{V,\mathcal{L},v,g}(\mathbb{P}^{n}(F_{v}))=\int_{F_{v}^{n}}\max\{1,|u_{1}|_{v},\cdots,|u_{n}|_{v}\}^{-(n+1)}|du_{1}\cdots du_{n}|=\frac{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-(n+1)}}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}.

Example 3.20
.

Let
F
be a number field. Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

over
F
of degree
k
. This induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
in the source
V=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

As in Example
3.7
, we identify
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Let
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}
be the coordinates of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and let
U_{0}=\{x_{0}\neq 0\}
. Suppose for the place
v
,
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f
takes the form

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f([x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}])=[F_{0}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),F_{1}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),F_{2}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})]

where
F_{0},F_{1},F_{2}\in O_{F_{v}}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]
are relatively prime, homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
, with empty common vanishing locus.

As in previous examples, we can take
D=0
, and
g\in H^{0}(V,O_{V})
to be the constant section 1.
One checks that the local Tamagawa measure on
U_{0}
is:

w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},v,g}|_{U_{0}}=|F_{0}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|_{v}^{-3/k}\prod_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\in\overline{F_{v}}}}{{a^{2}-x_{1}a+x_{2}=0}}}\max\{1,|f(a)|_{v}\}^{-3/k}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|.

(See
[
33
, Section 6]
in the case of
f
being identity.) The integrand can be seen as the
(-3/k)
-th power of the
v
-adic Mahler measure of the polynomial
F_{0}z^{2}-F_{1}z+F_{2}
. We recall the
v
-adic Mahler measure (
[
5
, Section 1.6]
) as follows: if
f(x)\in\overline{F_{v}}[x]
factorizes as

f(x)=a(x-\alpha_{1})\cdots(x-\alpha_{n}),

the
v
-adic Mahler measure of
f
is defined as

M_{v}(f):=|a|_{v}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|_{v}\}.

When
v
is non-archimedean, one can write

w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},v,g}|_{U_{0}}=\max\{|F_{0}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|_{v},|F_{1}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|_{v},|F_{2}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|_{v}\}^{-3/k}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|,

by Gauss lemma
[
5
, Lemma 1.6.3]
. One deduces that for all but finitely many non-archimedean places
v
, we have

w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},v,g}|_{U_{0}}=\max\{1,|x_{1}|_{v},|x_{2}|_{v}\}^{-3}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|.

We now compute the volume of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
using the local Tamagawa measure. The Tamagawa measure on the hyperplane
x_{0}=0
is 0, so it suffices to compute the volume of
U_{0}
, thus

w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},v,g}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))=\int_{F_{v}^{2}}|F_{0}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|_{v}^{-3/k}\prod_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\in\overline{F_{v}}}}{{a^{2}-x_{1}a+x_{2}=0}}}\max\{1,|f(a)|_{v}\}^{-3/k}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|.

Hence for all but finitely many non-archimedean places
v=v_{\mathfrak{p}}
with corresponding prime
\mathfrak{p}
, we have

w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},v,g}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))=\int_{F_{v}^{2}}\max\{1,|x_{1}|_{v},|x_{2}|_{v}\}^{-3}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|=\frac{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-3}}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}.

Global Tamagawa measures.
Given a
\mathcal{L}
-primitive variety
V
, we now want to define the global Tamagawa measure on
V(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. Ideally we can just take the product measure of
V(F_{v})
at all places
v\in M_{F}
. However, as we saw in Example
3.18
, the infinite product diverges.

Nonetheless, we can make sense of the infinite divergent product similar to Example
3.18
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
k\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})
is also an integer and that

D=k(\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})L+K_{V})

is a rigid effective divisor. Think of
D
as a Weil divisor, and let
D_{1},\cdots,D_{l}
be the irreducible components of
D
. Define

\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L}):=\mathrm{Pic}(V-\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}D_{l})

The Galois group
Gal(\overline{F}/F)
acts on
\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})
. Let
S\subset M_{F}
be a finite set of places where the datum
(V,(D_{1},\cdots,D_{l}))
has bad reduction, including the archimedean places
[
4
, Definition 3.3.5]
. Let
L_{v}(s,\mathrm{Pic}(X,\mathcal{L}))
be the local factor for the Artin
L
-function, and let

L_{S}^{*}(1,\mathrm{Pic}(X,\mathcal{L}))=\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\prod_{v\not\in S}L_{v}(s,\mathrm{Pic}(X,\mathcal{L})).

Finally let

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}L_{v}(1,\mathrm{Pic}(X,\mathcal{L}))&\text{if $v\notin S$,}\\
1&\text{if $v\in S$.}\end{cases}.

We can now define the normalized measure
\omega_{V,\mathcal{L}}
on
V(\mathbb{A}_{F})
by

\omega_{V,\mathcal{L}}:=|disc(F)|^{-n/2}L_{S}^{*}(1,\mathrm{Pic}(X,\mathcal{L}))\prod_{v}\lambda_{v}^{-1}\omega_{V,\mathcal{L},v},

where
n=\dim(V)
.

Remark
.

While the local Tamagawa measure depends on the choice of a
F
-rational section
g\in H^{0}(V,O_{V}(D))
, the global Tamagawa measure does not have this dependency. This follows from the product formula of
F
, as
g
is unique up to
F^{\times}
.

We may hence suppress mentioning
g
in the following examples.

Example 3.21
.

Consider
\mathbb{P}^{1}/\mathbb{Q}
, and
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{O(1)}
with the standard metric. We saw in Example
3.11
and Example
3.18
that
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is
\mathcal{L}
-primitive with
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=2
, and we can choose
k=1
,
D=0
.

In this case,
\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. All finite places have good reduction, i.e.
S=\{\infty\}
. For each prime
p
, the local
L
-factor at
p
is exactly
1+\frac{1}{p}
, and

L_{S}^{*}(1,\mathbb{Z})=\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)=\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\frac{\zeta(s)}{\zeta(2s)}=\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}.

The normalized Tamagawa measure on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
is hence

w_{V,\mathcal{L}}=\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}w_{V,\mathcal{L},\infty}\prod_{p}\frac{w_{V,\mathcal{L},v_{p}}}{1+1/p}.

In particular, the total volume of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
is
\frac{4}{\zeta(2)}
.

Example 3.22
.

Let
F
be a number field. Consider a morphism
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
over
F
and of degree
k
, and consider
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
in the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
. We saw in Example
3.12
and Example
3.19
that
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is
\mathcal{L}
-primitive with
\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{n+1}{k}
. Again we can choose
D=0
, and all finite places have good reduction.

It follows from Example
3.19
that

w_{V,\mathcal{L}}=\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}{\zeta_{F}(n+1)}\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\frac{w_{V,\mathcal{L},v}}{\lambda_{v}},

where

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}\frac{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-(n+1)}}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}&\text{if $v<\infty$ corresponds to prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$,}\\
1&\text{if $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{cases}

Example 3.23
.

Let
F
be a number field. Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

over
F
of degree
k
. This induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
in the source
V=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

It follows from Example
3.20
that

w_{V,\mathcal{L}}=\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}{\zeta_{F}(3)}\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\frac{w_{V,\mathcal{L},v}}{\lambda_{v}},

where

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}\frac{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-3}}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}&\text{if $v<\infty$ corresponds to prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$,}\\
1&\text{if $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{cases}

3.4.3.
Constant
c_{V}(\mathcal{L})
.

We will finally define
c_{V}(\mathcal{L})
in this section. More details can be found in
[
4
, Section 3]
.

Consider an
\mathcal{L}
-primitive variety
V
over number field
F
. We defined the relative Picard group
\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})
, and the global Tamagawa measure
\omega_{V,\mathcal{L}}
on
V(\mathbb{A}_{F})
. We further define a few constants:

•

\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L})
. Define

\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}.

There is a natural restriction map

\mathrm{Pic}(V)\to\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L}).

Let
\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})
be the image of the effective cone
\Lambda_{eff}(V)
under this restriction map. We hence have a triple

(\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L}),\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}},\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})).

Consider the dual
\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}},\mathbb{R})
, which comes with a natural pairing

\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}

by
(v,y)\to y(v)
. Let

\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})^{*}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L}),\mathbb{Z})

be the lattice dual to
\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})
under this natural pairing, and let
dy
be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
, normalized by
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})^{*})=1
.

Consider the dual cone to
\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})
under this natural pairing, i.e.

\Lambda^{*}:=\{f\in\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}^{*}:f(\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L}))\subset\mathbb{Z}\}.

Then for
\mathbf{s}\in\mathrm{Pic}(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{C}}
, where
\mathrm{Re}(\mathbf{s})
in the interior of
\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})
, we can define

\chi_{\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})}(\mathbf{s})=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}e^{-\langle\mathbf{s},y\rangle}dy.

Finally we define
1
1

1

In
[
4
]
,
[-K_{V}]
was used instead of
[\mathcal{L}]
when evaluating
\chi_{\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})}
, which was likely a typo. See also this Math Overflow question
[
28
]
, which shows that using
[\mathcal{L}]
is necessary to make the conjecture compatible between
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k}
.

\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L}):=\chi_{\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})}([\mathcal{L}]).

•

\delta_{V}(\mathcal{L}):=|H^{1}(Gal(\overline{F}/F),Pic(V,\mathcal{L}))|
.

•

\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L}):=w_{V,\mathcal{L}}(\overline{V(F)})
, where
\overline{V(F)}
is the closure of
V(F)
in
V(\mathbb{A}_{F})
.

We are now ready to define

c_{V}(\mathcal{L}):=\frac{\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L})}{\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})(\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})-1)!}\delta_{V}(\mathcal{L})\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L}).

Example 3.24
.

Consider
\mathbb{P}^{1}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
, and consider the line bundle
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with the standard metric.

•

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=2
,
\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
from Example
3.11
.

•

\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
.

–

Since
D
can be taken as 0 in Example
3.18
, we have

Pic(V,\mathcal{L})=Pic(V)\cong\mathbb{Z},

under which
L=O(1)\to 1
. Hence

i:Pic(V,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{R}}\cong\mathbb{R},

where we pick the isomorphism so that
i(O(1))=1
.

–

\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})=\Lambda_{eff}(V)
maps to
[0,\infty)
under
i
. The dual cone
\Lambda^{*}
is still
[0,\infty)
, under the natural pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
via multiplication map

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

–

Hence

\chi_{\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})}(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-sy}dy

and

\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\chi_{\Lambda_{eff}(V,\mathcal{L})}([\mathcal{L}])=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-1\cdot y}dy=1.

•

Pic(V,\mathcal{L})=Pic(V)\cong\mathbb{Z}
with trivial Galois action. Hence

\delta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=|H^{1}(Gal(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}),\mathbb{Z})|=1.

•

Since
\mathbb{P}^{1}
satisfies strong approximation, we have
\overline{V(\mathbb{Q})}=V(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
, hence

\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})=w_{V,\mathcal{L}}(V(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}))=\frac{4}{\zeta(2)}

as calculated in Example
3.21
.

Hence

c_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{2\cdot(1-1)!}\cdot 1\cdot\frac{4}{\zeta(2)}=\frac{2}{\zeta(2)}.

Example 3.25
.

Let
F
be a number field. Consider a morphism
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
over
F
and of degree
k
, and consider
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
on the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

•

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{n+1}{k},\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
from Example
3.12
.

•

\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{k}
, from a similar calculation as in Example
3.24
, and that

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-k\cdot y}dy=\frac{1}{k}.

•

\delta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
as in Example
3.24
.

Hence

c_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{\frac{1}{k}}{\frac{n+1}{k}\cdot(1-1)!}\cdot 1\cdot\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{n+1}\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L}),

where the global Tamagawa measure
\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})
has the shape in Example
3.22
.

Example 3.26
.

Let
F
be a number field. Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

over
F
of degree
k
. This induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on the source
V=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

•

\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{3}{k},\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
from Example
3.13
.

•

\gamma_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{k}
, from a similar calculation as in Example
3.24
, and that

\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-k\cdot y}dy=\frac{1}{k}.

•

\delta_{V}(\mathcal{L})=1
as in Example
3.24
.

Hence

c_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{\frac{1}{k}}{\frac{3}{k}\cdot(1-1)!}\cdot 1\cdot\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{3}\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L}),

where the global Tamagawa measure
\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})
has the shape in Example
3.23
.

3.4.4.
The Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures

Definition 3.27
.

Let
V
be a
\mathcal{L}
-primitive, smooth projective variety over number field
F
, and let
H_{\mathcal{L},F}
be the induced height relative to
F
. For any Zariski open subset
U\subset V
, let

N(U,\mathcal{L},B)=\{x\in U(F):H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq B\}.

We say that
V
is
strongly
\mathcal{L}
-saturated
if
|N(V,\mathcal{L},B)|\to\infty
as
B\to\infty
, and for any Zariski open subset
U
,

\lim_{B\to\infty}\frac{|N(U,\mathcal{L},B)|}{|N(V,\mathcal{L},B)|}=1.

In other words, we don’t have a closed subvariety of
V
where many
F
-rational points accumulates.

Conjecture 3.28
.

[
4
, Section 3.4 Step 4]

Let
F
be a number field. Let
V
be a strongly
\mathcal{L}
-saturated, smooth projective variety over number field
F
. Let
H_{\mathcal{L},F}
be the induced relative height on
V
.

then

N(V,\mathcal{L},B):=\#\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)\leq B\}=c_{V}(\mathcal{L})B^{\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})}(\log B)^{\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})-1}(1+o(1)),

as
B\to\infty
.

Remark
.

Suppose
[F:\mathbb{Q}]=d
. Since the absolute height satisfies

H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)=H_{\mathcal{L},F}(x)^{1/d}

for
x\in V(F)
, we see that

\#\{x\in V(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=c_{V}(\mathcal{L})B^{d\alpha_{V}(\mathcal{L})}(d\log B)^{\beta_{V}(\mathcal{L})-1}(1+o(1)).

3.5.
Two results on counting rational points by height

We document two known results of counting rational points by height: Franke-Manin-Tschinkel
[
24
]
and Néron
[
40
]
. They will be the main tools for our theorems.

3.5.1.
Counting rational points on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider a metrized bundle
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=(O(1),\|\cdot\|)
on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
over
F
(not necessarily the standard metric).

Let
f:\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
be a morphism over
F
of degree
k
, and consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
on the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:V(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) Weil height. Then

\#\{x\in\mathbb{P}^{n}(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\frac{1}{n+1}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(\mathcal{L})B^{d(n+1)/k}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}(B^{(d(n+1)-1)/k}\log B),

as
B\to\infty
.

Proof.

For
O(1)
with the standard metric, Franke-Manin-Tschinkel
[
24
]
proved this theorem with
o(B^{d(n+1)/k})
error, by observing that the associated height zeta function is an Eisenstein series on
GL_{n+1}
; the asymptotics then follows from analytic properties of the Eisenstein series. Peyre
[
42
, Corollary 6.2.16]
verified that the leading constant takes the form
\frac{1}{n+1}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(\mathcal{L})
; in particular, the asymptotics formula is consistent with the predictions of Batyrev-Manin-Peyre (Example
3.25
). The result for general adelic metric follows from the case of standard metric
[
42
, Proposition 5.0.1(c), Corollary 6.2.18]
.

To get an explicit power-saving error term, one can modify Masser-Vaaler’s work
[
36
]
to allow more flexibility at finite places. This leads to an error term of shape
O_{\mathcal{L},F}(B^{(d(n+1)-1)/k}E)
, where

E=\begin{cases}\log B&\text{ when $(d,n)=(1,1)$,}\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Clearly, this error term is
O_{\mathcal{L},F}(B^{(d(n+1)-1)/k}\log B)
.
∎

Remark
.

To the best of author’s knowledge, Widmer
[
51
, Theorem 3.1]
is the closest to the generalization of Masser-Vaaler’s result that we need. Unfortunately his notion of adelic Lipschitz system is too restrictive for our purpose.

More precisely, suppose
f=[F_{0},\cdots,F_{n}]
, where
F_{0},\cdots,F_{n}
are relatively prime homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
, with no common vanishing locus. For each place
v\in M_{F}
, we want to consider the norms

N_{v}([x_{0},\cdots,x_{n}])=\max\{|F_{0}(x_{0},\cdots,x_{n})|_{v},\cdots,|F_{n}(x_{0},\cdots,x_{n})|_{v}\}^{1/k}

These norms satisfy condition (i) - (iii) of adelic Lipschitz system
[
51
, Definition 2.2]
, but may not satisfy condition (iv). Nonetheless, a similar approach can still be used to prove the theorem.

Remark
.

The error term can be strengthened to
O_{\mathcal{L},F}(B^{(d(n+1)-1)/k}E^{\prime})
, where

E^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\log B&\text{ when $(d,n,k)=(1,1,1),$}\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

When
(d,n)\neq(1,1)
, Masser-Vaaler’s argument gives the desired result.

When
d=n=1
but
k>1
, we can use Huxley’s version for principle of Lipschitz
[
30
]
(instead of Masser-Vaaler’s version
[
36
]
), to get the stronger error term.

3.5.2.
Counting rational points on abelian varieties

Theorem 3.30
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
. Let
A
be an abelian variety of dimension
n
over
F
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a metrized line bundle on
A
over
F
, and let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:A(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) height. Let
r
be rank of the Mordell-Weil group
A(F)
.

Assume furthermore that
\mathcal{L}
, considered as an element in
Pic(A)
, is divisible by 2.

(a)

The (absolute, logarithmic) Néron-Tate height
\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}:A(F)\to[0,\infty)
, defined by

\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(x):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log H_{\mathcal{L}}(2^{n}x)}{4^{n}},

descends to a quadratic form on
A(F)/A(F)_{tor}
. By extension of scalars, it extends to a quadratic form on
\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}:A(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
.

(b)

Equip
A(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}\cong\mathbb{R}^{r}
with a measure via the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}
, such that
\mathrm{covol}(A(F))=1
. Then we have the asymptotics formula

\displaystyle\#\{x\in A(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,|A(F)_{tor}|\cdot\mathrm{Vol}\left(\{\mathbf{x}\in A(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathbf{x})\leq 1\}\right)\cdot(\log B)^{r/2}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}((\log B)^{(r-1)/2}).

Proof.

For (a), this follows from the theory of Néron-Tate heights. See
[
32
, Chapter 5, Theorem 3.1]
for a reference.

For (b), this is due to Néron
[
40
, Theorem 6]
. See also
[
32
, Chapter 5, Theorem 7.5]
for a reference.
∎

4.
Examples of main theorems for
8(2,1,1)
over
\mathbb{Q}

In this section, we illustrate Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.7
for the portrait
P=8(2,1,1)
over
\mathbb{Q}
. We will also prove Theorem
1.3
for this portrait with bare hands, to illustrate the ideas of proof in the general case.

Figure 4.
The portrait
8(2,1,1)
.

We first collect some known facts about the dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(P)
and the canonical forgetful map
\pi_{P}
.

Proposition 4.1

(
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
P
be the portrait
8(2,1,1)
.

(a)

X_{1}(P)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
\mathbb{Q}
.

(b)

The forgetful map
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is of degree 4, and has a presentation

\pi_{P}([x,y])=[G_{0}(x,y),G_{1}(x,y)],

where

G_{0}(x,y):=-(3x^{4}+10x^{2}y^{2}+3y^{4}),\,G_{1}(x,y):=4(x^{2}-y^{2})^{2}=4(x-y)^{2}(x+y)^{2}.

Let
H:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute multiplicative Weil height on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

4.1.
Illustration of Theorem
1.3

Consider

S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B):=\#\{c\in\mathbb{Q}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},\mathbb{Q})\cong P,H(c)\leq B\}.

Our goal is an asymptotic formula for
S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)
as
B\to\infty
.

Theorem 4.2

(Special case of Theorem
1.3
for
8(2,1,1)
over
\mathbb{Q}
)
.

Assuming the Morton-Silverman conjecture for
\mathbb{Q}
, we have

S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=\frac{1}{4\zeta(2)}\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4}),

as
B\to\infty
. Here

\mathcal{R}(1):=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\max\{|G_{0}(x,y)|,|G_{1}(x,y)|\}\leq 1\},

and the area is computed under the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The main workhorse is an asymptotic formula for

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B):=\#\{x\in X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q}):H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\},

as
B\to\infty
, provided by the next proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

We have

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4}).

as
B\to\infty
. Here

\mathcal{R}(1):=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\max\{|G_{0}(x,y)|,|G_{1}(x,y)|\}\leq 1\},

and the area is computed under the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

We follow the general strategy of Harron-Snowden
[
26
]
to get the asymptotic formula.

We want to compute

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=\#\{[a,b]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}):H([G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b)])\leq B\}.

This equals

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=\frac{1}{2}\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:gcd(a,b)=1,H([G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b)])\leq B\}.

where the factor of
2
comes from the units
\pm 1
of
\mathbb{Z}
.

Step 1: Local conditions.
Since the calculation of height requires simplification of
[G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b)]
to lowest terms, we first analyze
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b))
for specific values of
a,b
.

Lemma 4.4
.

If
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
satisfies
gcd(a,b)=1
, then

gcd(G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b))=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $a\not\equiv b\mod 2$,}\\
16&\text{ if $a,b$ are both odd.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We repeatedly use the observations that for any integers
m,m^{\prime},n
,

gcd(mm^{\prime},n)\mid gcd(m,n)\gcd(m^{\prime},n)

and

gcd(m+n,n)=gcd(m,n).

In our case,

gcd(G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b))\mid gcd(G_{0}(a,b),4)gcd(G_{0}(a,b),a-b)^{2}gcd(G_{0}(a,b),a+b)^{2}.

When
a\not\equiv b\mod 2
, we now show that
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b))=1
, by showing that each component on in right hand side is 1.

•

If
a,b
have different parity, it is clear that
G_{0}(a,b)
is odd. Hence
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),4)=1
.

•

Since
G_{0}(a,b)=Q(a,b)(a-b)+G_{0}(b,b)
for some
Q(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}[x,y]
, we see that

gcd(G_{0}(a,b),a-b)=gcd(G_{0}(b,b),a-b)=gcd(-16b^{4},a-b).

If
a,b
have different parity,
gcd(16,a-b)=1
. If
a,b
are relatively prime,
gcd(b^{4},a-b)=1
. Therefore
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),a-b)=1
.

By the same argument,
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),a+b)=1
.

When
a,b
are both odd, the same argument implies that
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b))
must be a power of 2. Now note that when
a,b
are odd, we have

G_{0}(a,b)=-(3a^{2}+b^{2})(b^{2}+3a^{2})\equiv 16\mod 32,

G_{1}(a,b)=4(a-b)^{2}(a+b)^{2}\equiv 0\mod 32.

Hence
gcd(G_{0}(a,b),G_{1}(a,b))=16
.
∎

Let

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B):=\frac{1}{2}\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:gcd(a,b)=1,a\not\equiv b\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq B\},

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{2}(P,B):=\frac{1}{2}\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:gcd(a,b)=1,a\equiv b\equiv 1\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq 16B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq 16B\}.

By the lemma, we see that

N_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B)+N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{2}(P,B).

Step 2: Principle of Lipschitz.

We will evaluate
N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B)
and
N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{2}(P,B)
by Huxley’s version of principle of Lipschitz
[
30
]
, which approximates the number of integral points in a compact region by its area.

We first show that the region

\mathcal{R}(B)=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\max\{|G_{0}(x,y)|,|G_{1}(x,y)|\}\leq B\}

is bounded. Since
G_{0}(x,1),G_{1}(x,1)\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
are relatively prime polynomials of the same degree, we have

\max\{|G_{0}(x,1)|,|G_{1}(x,1)|\}\gg_{G_{0},G_{1}}1>0

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. As
G_{0}(x,y),G_{1}(x,y)
are homogeneous of degree 4, we have

B\geq\max\{|G_{0}(x,y)|,|G_{1}(x,y)|\}\gg_{G_{0},G_{1}}|y|^{4}

so
|y|\ll_{G_{0},G_{1}}B^{1/4}
. A symmetric argument shows that
|x|\ll_{G_{0},G_{1}}B^{1/4}
as well.

Being closed and bounded,
\mathcal{R}(B)
is compact. Moreover
\mathcal{R}(B)
has a rectifiable boundary defined by polynomials. By the Principle of Lipschitz (Huxley’s version,
[
30
]
), the number of integral points in the region
\mathcal{R}(B)
is given by its area up to an error proportional to
(1-\delta)
-th power of the length of its boundary for some small
\delta>0
.

Conveniently,
\mathcal{R}(B)
is homogeneous in
B
: since
G_{0},G_{1}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree 4, we have

\mathcal{R}(B)=B^{1/4}\mathcal{R}(1).

Hence by principle of Lipschitz,

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{R}(B)\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}

\displaystyle=area(\mathcal{R}(B))+O(len(boundary(\mathcal{R}(B)))^{1-\delta})

\displaystyle=B^{2/4}area(\mathcal{R}(1))+O_{P}\left(B^{(1-\delta)/4}\right)

for some small
\delta>0
.

One can get similar results for counting integral points with local conditions (via translating and rescaling
\mathcal{R}
, so that the local conditions are gone). In our cases, we have

(4.1)

\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:a\not\equiv b\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq B\}=\frac{1}{2}B^{2/4}area(\mathcal{R}(1))+O_{P}\left(B^{(1-\delta)/4}\right),

(4.2)

\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:a\equiv b\equiv 1\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq B\}=\frac{1}{4}B^{2/4}area(\mathcal{R}(1))+O_{P}\left(B^{(1-\delta)/4}\right).

We now compute
N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B)
. By Möbius inversion,

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:gcd(a,b)=1,a\not\equiv b\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d=1}}{{gcd(d,2)=1}}}^{B^{1/4}}\mu(d)\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:a\not\equiv b\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq d^{-4}B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq d^{-4}B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d=1}}{{gcd(d,2)=1}}}^{B^{1/4}}\mu(d)\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(d^{-4}B\right)^{2/4}area(\mathcal{R}(1))+O_{P}\left(\left(d^{-4}B\right)^{(1-\delta)/4}\right)\right),

by equation
4.1
. Simplifying, we get

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sum_{gcd(d,2)=1}\frac{\mu(d)}{d^{2}}\right)\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-2^{-2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}\right)\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4}).

Similarly we compute
N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{2}(P,B)
.

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{2}(P,B)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:gcd(a,b)=1,a\equiv b\equiv 1\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq 16B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq 16B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d=1}}{{gcd(d,2)=1}}}^{B^{1/4}}\mu(d)\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:a\equiv b\equiv 1\mod 2,|G_{0}(a,b)|\leq 16d^{-4}B,|G_{1}(a,b)|\leq 16d^{-4}B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d=1}}{{gcd(d,2)=1}}}^{B^{1/4}}\mu(d)\left(\frac{1}{4}\left(16d^{-4}B\right)^{2/4}area(\mathcal{R}(1))+O_{P}\left(\left(16d^{-4}B\right)^{(1-\delta)/4}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\sum_{gcd(d,2)=1}\frac{\mu(d)}{d^{2}}\right)\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-2^{-2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}\right)\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)

\displaystyle=N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{1}(P,B)+N_{\mathbb{Q},1}^{2}(P,B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-2^{-2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}\right)\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4}).

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

For any strongly admissible graph
G
, let
\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the forgetful map. The following are equivalent:

•

c\in\mathbb{Q}
satisfies
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},\mathbb{Q})\supset P
,

•

c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q}))
,
c\neq\infty
.

Let
\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the minimal strongly admissible graphs that strictly contains
P
, where
X_{1}(G_{i})
has a
\mathbb{Q}
-rational point. Assuming Morton-Silverman conjecture over
\mathbb{Q}
, this set of graphs is finite; enumerate them as
G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}
.

By inclusion-exclusion principle, we see that

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q})):H(c)\leq B\}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in\pi_{G_{i}}(X_{1}(G_{i})(\mathbb{Q})):H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q})):H(c)\leq B\}

From Proposition
2.5
, among
G_{i}
’s only
G=10(2,1,1)a
or
G=10(2,1,1)b
satisfies
\mathrm{genus}(X_{1}(G))=1
; other dynamical modular curves have genus at least 2. It is also known that these two elliptic curves have rank
0
over
\mathbb{Q}

[
35
, 17.a4, 15.a7]
and hence have finitely many
\mathbb{Q}
-rational points. By Faltings’ theorem, each of the genus
\geq 2
dynamical modular curves has finitely
\mathbb{Q}
-rational points as well.

Hence

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in\pi_{G_{i}}(X_{1}(G_{i})(\mathbb{Q})):H(c)\leq B\}=O_{P}(1)

as
B\to\infty
, and

S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q})):H(c)\leq B\}+O_{P}(1).

Finally, notice that for each
c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q}))
, each fiber
\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)
has size 4 with finitely many exceptions. This is because each fiber has at most size 4 (as
\mathrm{deg}(\pi_{P})=4
), and whenever there is an
[x,y]\in X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q})
in the fiber, so are
[x,-y],[y,x],[y,-x]
; these points are distinct except for finitely many
c
’s. Hence,

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)=

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\mathbb{Q})):H(c)\leq B\}+O_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=
