\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{4\zeta(2)}\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot B^{2/4}+O_{P}(B^{1/4}).

∎

Remark
.

The leading coefficient for
S_{\mathbb{Q},1}(P,B)
matches up with the expectation from Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures calculated in Example
3.25
.

More precisely, the leading coefficient equals

\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L}),

where
\dfrac{1}{4}=\dfrac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}
, and
\dfrac{1}{2}=\dfrac{1}{\dim(\mathbb{P}^{1})+1}
. The global Tamagawa measure

\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}\cdot area(\mathcal{R}(1))\cdot\prod_{p}area(\mathcal{R}_{p}(1)),

can be further broken up into three pieces:

•

\frac{1}{\zeta(2)}
corresponds to the regularization factor
\dfrac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(s)}{\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(2)}
,

•

area(\mathcal{R}(1))
corresponds to local Tamagawa measure of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
at the infinite place.

•

For each finite prime
p
, define

\mathcal{R}_{p}(1):=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{2}:\max\{|G_{0}(x,y)|_{p},|G_{1}(x,y)|_{p}\}\leq 1\}.

Consider the Haar measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}
normalized so that
area\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}\right)=1
. Then
area(\mathcal{R}_{p}(1))
corresponds to the local Tamagawa measure of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
at the finite place
p
.

One can compute the local Tamagawa measure to find

area(\mathcal{R}_{p}(1))=\begin{cases}2&\text{if $p=2$,}\\
1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Hence the local Tamagawa measure at finite primes contribute

\prod_{p}area\left(\mathcal{R}_{p}(1)\right)=2.

4.2.
Illustration of Theorem
1.7

Consider

S_{\mathbb{Q},2}(P,B):=\#\{c\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}:c\in K\text{ where $K/\mathbb{Q}$ is of degree 2},H(c)\leq B,PrePer(f_{c},K)\cong P\}.

This counts all the
c
’s that has a preperiodic portrait isomorphic to
P
, when considered over some quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}
. Our goal is an asymptotic formula for
S_{\mathbb{Q},2}(P,B)
as
B\to\infty
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Using the presentation of
\pi_{P}
in Proposition
4.1
, and the natural isomorphism
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
in Lemma
3.8
, we have a presentation
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
of the form

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}([x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}])=[H_{0}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),H_{1}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),H_{2}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})]

where

H_{0}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}):=16x_{0}^{4}-32x_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+16x_{1}^{4}+64x_{0}^{3}x_{2}-64x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+96x_{0}^{2}x_{2}^{2}-32x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+64x_{0}x_{2}^{3}+16x_{2}^{4}

H_{1}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}):=24x_{0}^{4}+16x_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+24x_{1}^{4}-32x_{0}^{3}x_{2}-96x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}-112x_{0}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+16x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}-32x_{0}x_{2}^{3}+24x_{2}^{4}

H_{2}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}):=9x_{0}^{4}+30x_{0}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+9x_{1}^{4}-60x_{0}^{3}x_{2}-36x_{0}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}+118x_{0}^{2}x_{2}^{2}+30x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}-60x_{0}x_{2}^{3}+9x_{2}^{4}

are relatively prime, homogeneous polynomials of degree 4.

Proof.

This follows from a direct computation in Sage.
∎

We also recall the Mahler measure, which will be used to state the theorem. If
f(x)\in\mathbb{Q}[x]
factorizes as

f(x)=a(x-\alpha_{1})\cdots(x-\alpha_{n})

over
\mathbb{C}
, the Mahler measure is defined as

M_{\infty}(f):=|a|\prod_{i=1}^{n}\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|\}.

(Here
|\cdot|
is the usual Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{C}
.)

Theorem 4.6

(Special case of Theorem
1.7
for
8(2,1,1)
over
\mathbb{Q}
)
.

Assume the Morton-Silverman conjecture for
\mathbb{Q}
with
d=2
. Then we have

S_{\mathbb{Q},2}(P,B)=\frac{1}{8\zeta(3)}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}(1)\right)B^{3/2}+O(B)

as
B\to\infty
. Here

\mathcal{S}(1):=\{(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:M_{\infty}(H_{0}z^{2}-H_{1}z+H_{2})\leq 1\}

and the volume is computed under the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

We will not prove this now, although it should be provable directly in a similar way as Theorem
4.2
.

Remark
.

The leading coefficient of
S_{\mathbb{Q},2}(P,B)
admits a local-global interpretation as in the previous theorem, which matches up with the expectation from Batyrev-Manin-Peyre conjectures calculated in Example
3.26
.

More precisely, the leading coefficient equals

\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{3}\cdot\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L}),

where
\dfrac{1}{4}=\dfrac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}
, and
\frac{1}{3}=\dfrac{1}{\dim(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1})+1}
. The global Tamagawa measure

\tau_{V}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{\zeta(3)}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}(1)\right)\right)\cdot\prod_{p}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}_{p}(1)\right)

can be further broken up into three pieces where

•

\frac{1}{\zeta(3)}
corresponds to the regularization factor
\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(s)}{\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(3)}
,

•

\frac{3}{2}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}(1)\right)
corresponds to local Tamagawa measure of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
at the infinite place. Here the
\frac{3}{2}
factor comes from change of variables: when calculating
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{S}(1))
, we can first integrate in
x_{0}
-axis, then relate the remaining integral to
w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},\infty,g}
in Example
3.20
.

The factor
\frac{3}{2}
should be interpreted as
\dfrac{\dim(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{2})+1}{|\mathbb{Z}^{\times}|}
.

•

Analogously for each finite prime
p
, we can define the
p
-adic Mahler measure
M_{p}
, and define

\mathcal{S}_{p}(1):=\{(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{3}:M_{p}(H_{0}z^{2}-H_{1}z+H_{2})\leq 1\}.

Consider the Haar measure on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{3}
normalized so that
\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{3}\right)=1
. Then
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{S}_{p}(1))
corresponds to the local Tamagawa measure of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
at the finite place
p
. In this specific example, one can compute the local Tamagawa measure to find that
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{S}_{p}(1))=1
for all finite primes.

Again, when calculating
\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{S}_{p}(1))
we can first integrate along the
x_{0}
-axis, then relate the remaining integral to
w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\mathcal{L},p,g}
in Example
3.20
. There will be a factor
\frac{1-p^{-3}}{1-p^{-1}}
analogous to
\frac{3}{2}
, but this factor is cancelled out when we regularize the divergent infinite product (Example
3.23
). Hence there is no extra factor at finite places.

Perhaps the leading coefficient of
S_{\mathbb{Q},2}(P,B)
can be interpreted more naturally as follows: it equals

\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\zeta(3)}\cdot\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}(1)\right)\cdot\prod_{p}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}_{p}(1)\right),

where

•

\frac{1}{4}=\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}
,

•

\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{Z}^{\times}|}
,

•

\frac{1}{\zeta(3)}
corresponds to the regularization factor
\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(s)}{\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(3)}
,

•

\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}(1)\right)
and
\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{S}_{p}(1)\right)
are the volumes of analogously defined
\mathcal{S}(1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\mathcal{S}_{p}(1)\subset\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{3}
,

\mathcal{S}(1):=\{(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:M_{\infty}(H_{0}z^{2}-H_{1}z+H_{2})\leq 1\},

\mathcal{S}_{p}(1):=\{(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{3}:M_{p}(H_{0}z^{2}-H_{1}z+H_{2})\leq 1\},

under Haar measure.

5.
Proof of Theorem
1.3

5.1.
Precise statement of Theorem
1.3

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and let
H:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute multiplicative Weil height.

Let
\mathscr{P}_{1}=\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}
be a set of portraits, where

\Gamma_{0}:=\{\emptyset,4(1,1),4(2),6(1,1),6(2),6(3),8(2,1,1)\},

\Gamma_{1}:=\{8(1,1)a,8(1,1)b,8(2)a,8(2)b,10(2,1,1)a,10(2,1,1)b\}.

(See Appendix
A
for pictures of these graphs.) By Proposition
2.5
,
P\in\Gamma_{g}
is equivalent to the dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(P)
having genus
g
.

By Theorem
1.2
, if there are infinitely many
c\in F
’s such that
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\cong P
, then
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
.

Definition 5.1

(Constants
a_{F,1},b_{F,1},c_{F,1}
)
.

Let
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
be a portrait. Let
X_{1}(P)
be the dynamical modular curve associated to
P
, and let
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the natural forgetful map of degree
k
.

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is rational over
F
.

We define
a_{F,1}(P)=\frac{2d}{k}
;
b_{F,1}(P)=0
. For
c_{F,1}
, suppose
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
has a presentation

\pi_{P}(x)=\frac{g_{0}(x)}{g_{1}(x)}

for relatively prime
g_{0}(x),g_{1}(x)\in F[x]
(see examples in Appendix
A
, Table
1
), then we define

c_{F,1}(P)=\frac{1}{2|Aut(\pi_{P})|}\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}{\zeta_{F}(2)}\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\frac{w_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))}{\lambda_{v}},

where

–

M_{F}
is the set of places of
F
.

–

\zeta_{F}(s)
is the Dedekind zeta function of
F
.

–

For each place
v
,
w_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}
is a local Tamagawa measure of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v})
,

w_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))=\int_{F_{v}}\max\{|g_{0}(u)|_{v},|g_{1}(u)|_{v}\}^{-2/k}du.

–

For each place
v
,
\lambda_{v}
is a regularization factor to make sure the infinite product converges,

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}1+\frac{1}{N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})}&\text{ if $v<\infty$ corresponds to prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$,}\\
1&\text{if $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{cases}

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{1}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is an elliptic curve over
F
.

We define
a_{F,1}(P)=0
;
b_{F,1}(P)=\frac{r}{2}
, where
r
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil Group
X_{1}(P)(F)
; and

c_{F,1}(P)=\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}\cdot\frac{|X_{1}(P)(F)_{tor}|V_{r}}{k^{r/2}Reg(X_{1}(P)/F)^{1/2}}

where
V_{r}:=\frac{\pi^{r/2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{r}{2}+1\right)}
is the volume of unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and
Reg(X_{1}(P)/F)
is the regulator of
X_{1}(P)
over
F
.

With
a_{F,1},b_{F,1},c_{F,1}
defined, we now restate Theorem
1.3
with error terms.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
. For each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
, define

S_{F,1}(P,B):=\#\{c\in F:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\cong P,H(c)\leq B\}.

Assume the Morton-Silverman conjecture for the number field
F
. Then for each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
, We have the asymptotic formula

S_{F,1}(P,B)=c_{F,1}(P)B^{a_{F,1}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,1}(P)}+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,1}(B))

as
B\to\infty
. Here

Error_{P,F,1}(B)=\begin{cases}B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{0}$,}\\
(\log B)^{(r-1)/2}+1&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{1}$.}\end{cases}

5.2.
Asymptotic formula for
N_{F,1}(P,B)

The main input to Theorem
1.3
/Theorem
5.2
is an asymptotic formula for

N_{F,1}(P,B):=\#\{x\in X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\},

as
B\to\infty
. It is our goal to obtain such formula in this section (Theorem
5.5
).

Proposition 5.3

(Counting rational points on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
)
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider
\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
with the standard metric.

Let
f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism over
F
of degree
k
, and consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
on the source
V=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:V(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) Weil height. Then

\#\{x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})B^{2d/k}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}(B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B),

as
B\to\infty
. Here
\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})
is the volume of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with respect to a global Tamagawa measure, defined in Section
3.4.3
and calculated in Example
3.19
,
3.22
and
3.25
.

Proof.

This is special case of Theorem
3.29
when
n=1
.
∎

Proposition 5.4

(Counting rational points on elliptic curve)
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider
\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
with the standard metric.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
F
. Let
f:E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism over
F
of degree
k
such that
f(x)=f(-x)
; in particular,
k
is even.

Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
on
E
, and let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:E(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) height. Then

\#\{x\in E(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\frac{|E(F)_{tor}|V_{r}}{k^{r/2}Reg(E/F)^{1/2}}(\log B)^{r/2}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}((\log B)^{(r-1)/2})

as
B\to\infty
. Here

•

r=r(E/F)
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil group
E(F)
,

•

V_{r}:=\frac{\pi^{r/2}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{r}{2}+1\right)}
is the volume of unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,

•

Reg(E/F)
is the regulator of
E
over
F
.

Proof.

From Theorem
3.30
, we see that

\displaystyle\#\{x\in E(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,|E(F)_{tor}|\cdot\mathrm{Vol}\left(\{\mathbf{x}\in E(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathbf{x})\leq 1\}\right)\cdot(\log B)^{r/2}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}((\log B)^{(r-1)/2}).

Here
r=r(E/F)
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil group
E(F)
, and the volume of
E(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}
is induced from Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
, normalized such that
\mathrm{covol}(E(F))=1
. We fix this measure by taking an integral basis
P_{1},\cdots,P_{r}
of
E(F)/E(F)_{tor}
, and identify
\mathbb{R}^{r}\cong E(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}
via

(a_{1},\cdots,a_{r})\to a_{1}P_{1}+\cdots+a_{r}P_{r}.

Let
\widehat{h_{E}}
be the canonical height of
E/F
. For
x\in E(F)
,

\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log H(f(2^{n}x))}{4^{n}}=k\cdot\widehat{h_{E}}(x)

by the defining property of canonical height
[
48
, Proposition VIII.9.1]
. The discriminant of the quadratic form
\widehat{h_{E}}
,

\mathrm{disc}(\widehat{h_{E}})=\mathrm{det}\,\left(\langle P_{i},P_{j}\rangle\right)_{i,j},

is exactly the regulator
Reg(E/F)
by definition. Hence

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}\left(\{\mathbf{x}\in E(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\widehat{h_{\mathcal{L}}}(\mathbf{x})\leq 1\}\right)=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Vol}\left(\left\{\mathbf{x}\in E(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\widehat{h_{E}}(\mathbf{x})\leq\frac{1}{k}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{k^{r/2}}\mathrm{Vol}\left(\left\{\mathbf{x}\in E(F)\otimes\mathbb{R}:\widehat{h_{E}}(\mathbf{x})\leq 1\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{V_{r}}{k^{r/2}Reg(E/F)^{1/2}},

where
V_{r}
is the volume of unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
∎

With the last two results at hand, we can compute the asymptotics for
N_{F,1}(P,B)
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
P
be a portrait. Let
X_{1}(P)
be the dynamical modular curve associated to
P
, and let
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the natural forgetful map of degree
k
.

Let
H:\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute, multiplicative Weil height. For a fixed number field
F/\mathbb{Q}
of degree
d
, denote

N_{F,1}(P,B):=\#\{x\in X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\},

(a)

Suppose
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is rational over
F
. We have

N_{F,1}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,1}(P)B^{2d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B)

as
B\to\infty
.

(b)

Suppose
P\in\Gamma_{1}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is an elliptic curve over
F
. We have

N_{F,1}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,1}(P)(\log B)^{r/2}+O_{P,F}((\log B)^{(r-1)/2})

as
B\to\infty
. Here
r=r(X_{1}(P)/F)
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil Group
X_{1}(P)(F)
.

(c)

Suppose
P\notin\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is a curve of genus
\geq 2
, and

N_{F,1}(P,B)=O_{P,F}(1).

Proof.

For part (a), we use Proposition
5.3
. The constant
\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})
was calculated in Example
3.19
,
3.22
and
3.25
. We defined
c_{F,1}(P)
such that

\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,1}(P),

so part (a) follows from Proposition
5.3
directly.

For part (b), we use Proposition
5.4
. We defined
c_{F,1}(P)
such that

\frac{|X_{1}(P)(F)_{tor}|V_{r}}{k^{r/2}Reg(X_{1}(P)/F)^{1/2}}=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,1}(P),

so part (b) follows from Proposition
5.4
directly.

Part (c) follows from Faltings’ theorem.
∎

5.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
/Theorem
5.2

Lemma 5.6
.

With the notations in Theorem
5.5
, we have

\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F)):H(c)\leq B\}=\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}N_{F,1}(P,B)+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,1}(B)).

Here

Error_{P,F,1}(B)=\begin{cases}B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{0}$,}\\
(\log B)^{(r-1)/2}+1&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{1}$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Suppose
c=\pi_{P}(x)
for some
x\in X_{1}(P)(F)
. We want to show that for generic
c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F))
,

\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap X_{1}(P)(F)=\{\sigma(x):\sigma\in Aut(\pi_{P})\}.

By the definition of
Aut(\pi_{P})
, we see that right hand side is a subset of the left.

First, note that right hand side has size
|Aut(\pi_{P})|
with
O_{P}(1)
exceptions of
c
. This is because if right hand side is smaller than expected, it must mean that an unexpected coincidence

\sigma(x)=\sigma^{\prime}(x)

occured for some
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}\in Aut(\pi_{P})
. Since
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
is a proper Zariski-closed subset of the curve
X_{1}(P)
, it is of dimension 0; so there are finitely many exceptional
x
’s, hence finitely many exceptional
c
’s as well. To recap, with
O_{P}(1)
exceptions of
c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F))
, we have

|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap X_{1}(P)(F)|\geq|Aut(\pi_{P})|.

Finally, we need to show that

|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap X_{1}(P)(F)|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|

happens rarely. Suppose we have such an exceptional
c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F))
, where there exists
x,z\in X_{1}(P)(F)
not in the same
Aut(\pi_{P})
-orbit, such that

\pi_{P}(x)=\pi_{P}(z)=c.

Then
(x,z)
is a
F
-rational point on some irreducible component
C
of

X_{1}(P)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{1}(P):=\{(x,z)\in X_{1}(P)\times X_{1}(P):\pi_{P}(x)=\pi_{P}(z)\},

which is not the graph of any
\sigma\in Aut(\pi_{P})
; we call such curve
C
exceptional. By computing these exceptional curves for each portrait
P\in\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}
in Sage (see Appendix
A
), we find that

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, any exceptional
C
is smooth and has genus
\geq 1
.

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{1}
, any exceptional
C
is smooth and has genus
\geq 4
.

The number of
F
-rational points on these exceptional curves are few; by Theorem
5.5
applied to genus 1 and genus
\geq 2
curves, we see that

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F)):|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap X_{1}(P)(F)|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|,H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle\ll_{P}

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{(x,z)\in(X_{1}(P)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{1}(P))(F):\begin{aligned} &(x,z)\text{ lies in an exceptional curve }\\
&\text{of $X_{1}(P)$, $H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B$}\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P}(Error_{P,F,1}(B))

This shows the rarity of
c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F))
satisfying

|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap X_{1}(P)(F)|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|,

and finish the proof of lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
/Theorem
5.2
.

Recall the definition of
S_{F,1}(P,B)
: for each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{1}
,

S_{F,1}(P,B):=\#\{c\in F:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\cong P,H(c)\leq B\}.

Our goal is to get an asymptotic formula for
S_{F,1}(P,B)
as
B\to\infty
.

By definition of
X_{1}(P)
, the following are equivalent:

•

c\in F
satisfies
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\supset P
,

•

c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F))
,
c\neq\infty
.

Let
\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the minimal strongly admissible graphs that strictly contains
P
, where
X_{1}(G_{i})
has a
F
-rational point. Assuming Morton-Silverman conjecture over
F
, this set of graphs is finite; enumerate them as
G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}
.

By inclusion-exclusion principle, we see that

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in F:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\supset P,H(c)\leq B\}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in F:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\supset G_{i},H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,S_{F,1}(P,B)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in F:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},F)\supset P,H(c)\leq B\}.

Using the above equivalence, we have

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F)):H(c)\leq B\}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in\pi_{G_{i}}(X_{1}(G_{i})(F)):H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,S_{F,1}(P,B)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F)):H(c)\leq B\}.

Now note that

\displaystyle\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F)):H(c)\leq B\}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}N_{F,1}(P,B)+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,1}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,c_{F,1}(P)B^{a_{F,1}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,1}(P)}+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,1}(B))

by Theorem
5.5
and Lemma
5.6
.

Finally, we show that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in\pi_{G_{i}}(X_{1}(G_{i})(F)):H(c)\leq B\}

only contributes to the error term. For each
G_{i}\supset P
,

•

If
a_{F,1}(P)>0
, by Proposition
2.3
(b),
\mathrm{deg}\pi_{G_{i}}\geq 2\mathrm{deg}\pi_{P}
, so either
X_{1}(G_{i})
has genus 0 and

a_{F,1}(G_{i})=\frac{2d}{\mathrm{deg}\pi_{G_{i}}}\leq\frac{d}{\mathrm{deg}\pi_{P}}\leq\frac{2d-1}{\mathrm{deg}\pi_{P}},

or
X_{1}(G_{i})
has genus
\geq 1
and
a_{F,1}(G_{i})=0
. In either case,

\#\{c\in\pi_{G_{i}}(X_{1}(G_{i})(F)):H(c)\leq B\}=O\left(B^{(2d-1)/\mathrm{deg}\pi_{P}}\right)=O(Error_{P,F,1}(B)).

•

If
a_{F,1}(P)=0
, then
X_{1}(P)
has genus 1. Since the 6 such possible
P
’s (Table
2
, Appendix
A
) has no containment relationship, we see that
X_{1}(G_{i})
has genus
\geq 2
. Faltings’ theorem thus implies that

\#\{c\in\pi_{G_{i}}(X_{1}(G_{i})(F)):H(c)\leq B\}=O_{G_{i},F}(1)=O(Error_{P,F,1}(B)).

Hence

\displaystyle S_{F,1}(P,B)=

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(F)):H(c)\leq B\}+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,1}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,c_{F,1}(P)B^{a_{F,1}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,1}(P)}+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,1}(B)).

∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.7

6.1.
Precise statement of Theorem
1.7

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and let
H:\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute multiplicative Weil height.

Let
\mathscr{P}_{2}=\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
be a set of portraits, where

\Gamma_{0}:=\{\emptyset,4(1,1),4(2),6(1,1),6(2),6(3),8(2,1,1)\},

\Gamma_{1}:=\{8(1,1)a,8(1,1)b,8(2)a,8(2)b,10(2,1,1)a,10(2,1,1)b\},

\Gamma_{2}:=\{8(3),8(4),10(3,1,1),10(3,2)\}.

(See Appendix
A
for pictures of these graphs.) By Proposition
2.5
,
P\in\Gamma_{g}
is equivalent to the dynamical modular curve
X_{1}(P)
having genus
g
.

By Theorem
1.6
, if there are infinitely many
c\in\overline{F}
such that
[F(c):F]\leq 2
and
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P
for some
K/F
of degree 2, then
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
.

Definition 6.1

(Constants
a_{F,2},b_{F,2},c_{F,2}
)
.

Let
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
be a portrait. Let
X_{1}(P)
be the dynamical modular curve associated to
P
, and let
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the natural forgetful map of degree
k
.

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is rational over
F
.

We define
a_{F,2}(P)=\frac{6d}{k}
;
b_{F,2}(P)=0
; and

c_{F,2}(P)=\frac{2}{3|Aut(\pi_{P})|}\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}{\zeta_{F}(3)}\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\frac{w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))}{\lambda_{v}},

where

–

M_{F}
is the set of places of
F
.

–

\zeta_{F}(s)
is the Dedekind zeta function of
F
.

–

For each place
v
,
w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}
is a local Tamagawa measure of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v})
. Suppose
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}
takes the form

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}([x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}])=[F_{0}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),F_{1}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}),F_{2}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})]

where
F_{0},F_{1},F_{2}\in O_{F_{v}}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]
are relatively prime, homogeneous polynomials of degree
k
. Then

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{F_{v}^{2}}|F_{0}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|^{-3/k}\prod_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\in\overline{F_{v}}}}{{a^{2}-x_{1}a+x_{2}=0}}}\max\{1,|\pi_{P}(a)|_{v}\}^{-3/k}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|.

By Gauss lemma
[
5
, Lemma 1.6.3]
, if
v
is non-archimedean, this is the same as

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}O(1),v}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{F_{v}^{2}}\max\{|F_{0}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|,|F_{1}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|,|F_{2}(1,x_{1},x_{2})|\}^{-3/k}|dx_{1}dx_{2}|.

–

For each place
v
,
\lambda_{v}
is a regularization factor to make sure the infinite product converges,

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}\frac{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-3}}{1-N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})^{-1}}&\text{ if $v<\infty$ corresponds to prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$,}\\
1&\text{if $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{cases}

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{1}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is an elliptic curve over
F
.

We define
a_{F,2}(P)=\frac{4d}{k}
;
b_{F,2}(P)=0
. We will show the existence of the constant
c_{F,2}>0
, but it does not have a nice form; see the remark after Proposition
6.4
for its value in a special case, where
c_{F,2}=\frac{2}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}C_{E,f,F}
with the constant
C_{E,f,F}
defined in the remark.

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{2}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is a genus 2 curve over
F
.

We define
a_{F,2}(P)=\frac{4d}{k}
;
b_{F,2}(P)=0
. For
c_{F,2}
, suppose
X_{1}(P)
has a model
y^{2}=h(x)
for some polynomial
h(x)\in F[x]
, and
\pi_{P}
has a presentation

\pi_{P}(x)=\frac{g_{0}(x)}{g_{1}(x)}

for relatively prime
g_{0}(x),g_{1}(x)\in F[x]
(see examples in Appendix
A
,Table
1
). In particular,
\pi_{P}
is invariant under the hyperelliptic involution. Then we define

c_{F,2}(P)=\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}\frac{\mathrm{res}_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}{\zeta_{F}(2)}\prod_{v\in M_{F}}\frac{w_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}\mathbf{O(1)},v}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))}{\lambda_{v}},

where

–

M_{F}
is the set of places of
F
.

–

\zeta_{F}(s)
is the Dedekind zeta function of
F
.

–

For each place
v
,
w_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}\mathbf{O(1)},v}
is a local Tamagawa measure of
\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v})
, and

w_{\mathbb{P}^{1},\pi_{P}^{*}\mathbf{O(1)},v}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(F_{v}))=\int_{F_{v}}\max\{|g_{0}(u)|_{v},|g_{1}(u)|_{v}\}^{-4/k}du.

–

For each place
v
,
\lambda_{v}
is a regularization factor to make sure the infinite product converges,

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}1+\frac{1}{N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\mathfrak{p})}&\text{ if $v<\infty$ corresponds to prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$,}\\
1&\text{if $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{cases}

With
a_{F,2},b_{F,2},c_{F,2}
defined, we now restate Theorem
1.7
with error terms.

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
. For each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
, define

S_{F,2}(P,B):=\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}.

Assume the Morton-Silverman conjecture for quadratic extensions of
F
. Then for each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
, we have the asymptotic formula

S_{F,2}(P,B)=c_{F,2}(P)B^{a_{F,2}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,2}(P)}+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,2}(B))

as
B\to\infty
. Here

Error_{P,F,2}(B)=\begin{cases}B^{2(3d-1)/k}\log B&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{0}$,}\\
B^{2(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{1}$,}\\
B^{2(2d-1)/k}\log B&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{2}$.}\\
\end{cases}

Here when
P\in\Gamma_{1}
,
X_{1}(P)
is an elliptic curve over
F
, and
r=r(X_{1}(P)/F)
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil group
X_{1}(P)(F)
.

6.2.
Counting rational points on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
. Consider a morphism

f:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

over
F
of degree
k
. This induces a map

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on the source
V=\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) Weil height. Then

\#\{x\in\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\frac{1}{3}\tau_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})B^{3d/k}+O_{f,F}(B^{(3d-1)/k}),

as
B\to\infty
. Here
\tau_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})
is the volume of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with respect to a global Tamagawa measure, defined in Section
3.4.3
and calculated in Example
3.20
,
3.23
and
3.26
.

Proof.

This is special case of Theorem
3.29
when
n=2
, for the product metric on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The savings of
\log B
in error term comes from
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
having dimension
>1
.
∎

6.3.
Counting rational points on symmetric square of elliptic curves

Proposition 6.4

(Counting rational points on symmetric square of elliptic curve)
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
with the standard metric.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
F
. Let
f:E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism over
F
of degree
k
such that
f(x)=f(-x)
; in particular,
k
is even. This induces

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
.

Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on the source
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
, and let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) height. Then

\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=C_{f,E,F}B^{2d/k}+O_{f,E,F}(B^{(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}),

as
B\to\infty
, for some constant
C_{f,E,F}>0
. Here
r=r(E/F)
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil group
E(F)
.

Remark
.

This calculation was done in Arakelov without details
[
1
, p. 408, Constant T]
, where the celebrated Faltings-Riemann-Roch theorem was also conjectured (and later proved in Faltings
[
22
]
).

As pointed out in Faltings
[
22
, p. 406, Remark]
, Arakelov’s normalization for volume does not lead to a Riemann-Roch theorem, so modifications are probably needed for Arakelov’s result to hold. See also
[
4
, Section 4.3]
, where Bost pointed out that Arakelov’s calculations may have some
O(1)
error.

Since we are unable to find another reference for this calculation, we would sketch it here, and carry it out in details in Appendix
B
. Comparing to Faltings’ calculation
[
22
, Section 8]
, we compute the leading constant of main term explicitly (which was not calculated there), and we prove an explicit zero-free region of width
\frac{1}{k}-\epsilon
for the associated height zeta function.

Remark
.

The constant
C_{f,E,F}
does not have a nice form. To illustrate how the constant may look, we compute
C_{f,E,F}
in Appendix
B
with the following assumptions:

•

E
is semi-stable over
F
,

•

If
X/\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is the minimal regular model of
E/F
, and
v
is a non-archimedean place, we assume that the vertical fiber
X_{v}:=X\times_{\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})}\mathrm{Spec}(k(v))
is irreducible.

In this case, the constant
C_{f,E,F}
takes the form

\displaystyle C_{f,E,F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\cdot\frac{|Cl(F)|Reg_{F}}{|\mu_{F}|\zeta_{F}(2)}\cdot\frac{1}{|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{exp}\left(d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E)\right)}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|^{1/4}H(f(O))^{2d/k}}

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in E(F)}\left(\frac{H_{NT}(R)}{H(f(R))^{2/k}}\right)^{d}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\left(\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right)\right).

Here,

•

r_{1},r_{2}
are the number of real embeddings/pairs of complex embeddings of
F
.

•

|Cl(F)|
,
Reg_{F}
,
|\mu_{F}|
,
disc(O_{F})
,
\zeta_{F}(s)
are the class number, regulator, number of roots of unity, discriminant and Dedekind zeta function of
F
.

•

h_{Faltings}(E)
is the (logarithmic) Faltings height of
E
(defined at Remark
Remark
).

•

\Delta_{E/F}
is the minimal discriminant of
E/F
.

•

E(F)
are the
F
-rational points of the elliptic curve
E
.
O
is the identity of the elliptic curve
E/F
.

•

X\to Spec(O_{F})
is the minimal regular model of
E/F
.

•

D_{R}
is the closure of the
F
-rational point
R
in
X
. It is an irreducible horizontal Weil divisor.

•

O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})
is the line bundle on
X
corresponding to the horizontal Weil divisor
D_{R}+D_{O}
.

•

H_{NT}
is the (multiplicative) Néron-Tate height on
E
.

•

For each archimedean place
v
, let

d\mu_{v}:=\frac{1}{k}c_{1}(f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})

be the first Chern form of the metrized line bundle
f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
, normalized by
1/k
. The
1/k
factor ensures that

\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}d\mu_{v}=1.

•

The space
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}
has a Haar measure, called the Faltings volume
[
22
]
, and is denoted by
\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}
(see Definition
B.10
).

It is worth remarking that

2^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\cdot\frac{|Cl(F)|Reg_{F}}{|\mu_{F}|\zeta_{F}(2)}\cdot\frac{1}{|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|}

is basically Schanuel’s constant for
n=2
(up to a constant factor), and is closely related to

\frac{res_{s=1}\zeta_{F}(s)}{\zeta_{F}(2)}

via class number formula.

Proof.

We will sketch the proof here, while leaving the details in Appendix
B
.

The addition map
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E\to E
gives
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle structure over
E
. We can then count rational points on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
fiber by fiber. We summarize the key points in this argument:

•

If
x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F)
lies in the fiber over
R\in E(F)
such that
H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B
, we will show that

H(R)\ll_{E,f,F}B^{2}.

By Theorem
3.30
, there are
\asymp_{E,f,F}(\log B)^{r/2}
such
R\in E(F)
, so we only need to handle
\asymp_{E,f,F}(\log B)^{r/2}
fibers.

•

Let
R\in E(F)
. We will count the number of rational points
x
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fiber over
R
satisfying
H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B
. The height function on the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fiber comes from the pullback of
\mathbf{O(1)}
via the map

\mathbb{P}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

and is a metrized line bundle of degree
k
. By Proposition
5.3
, we hence expect the number of rational points on each
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fiber to have main term
\asymp B^{2d/k}
, and error term
O\left(B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B\right)
. However, a priori the big
O
constant of the error term depends on the fiber; equivalently it depends on
R\in E(F)
.

We will show that:

–

The sum over
R\in E(F)
of the leading constants in the main term converges.

–

The big-
O
constant in the error term can be taken uniformly over
R\in E(F)
.

(As in Masser-Vaaler
[
36
]
, power savings in error term comes from the Lipschitz parametrizability of certain regions, which vary with
R\in E(F)
. We will show that the number of Lipschitz parametrizations/Lipschitz constants of these regions are uniform over
R\in E(F)
(Proposition
B.44
), from which the uniformity of big-O constant follows.)

Hence after summing over all fibers, the main term would still have size
\asymp B^{2d/k}
, and the error term would have size
O\left(B^{(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}\right)
.

We would need to work explicitly with the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle structure of
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
and height on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
restricted to fibers over
E
. This naturally brings us to Arakelov geometry, and is why we ran the argument in the Arakelov setup.
∎

6.4.
Counting rational points on symmetric square of genus 2 curves

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
with the standard metric.

Let
C
be a genus 2 curve over
F
with a
F
-rational point
Q_{0}
, and let
-:C\to C
be the hyperelliptic involution. Let
x:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the unique even degree 2 map such that

x(Q_{0})=x(-Q_{0})=\infty.

Consider an even morphism
f:C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
of degree
k
, factorized as
f=G\circ x
for some
G:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
of degree
\frac{k}{2}
. This induces

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
. Consider the metrized line bundle

\mathcal{L}:=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}

on the source
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C
. Let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C)(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) Weil height. Then

\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})B^{2d/k}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}\left(B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B\right),

as
B\to\infty
. Here
\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})
is the volume of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with respect to a global Tamagawa measure, defined in Section
3.4.3
and calculated in Example
3.19
,
3.22
and
3.25
.

Proof.

Consider the birational map

p_{Q_{0}}:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C\to Jac(C)

over
F
, defined by sending
\{Q,Q^{\prime}\}\to cl\left([Q]+[Q^{\prime}]-2[Q_{0}]\right)
. This is the blow up of
Jac(C)
at one point, with exceptional divisor of the form

\nabla=\{\{Q,-Q\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}.

Consider the theta divisor on
Jac(C),

\Theta_{Q_{0}}:=\{[Q]-[Q_{0}]:Q\in C(\overline{F})\},

with respect to
Q_{0}
. Similarly, consider
p_{-Q_{0}}:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C\to Jac(C)
and the theta divisor
\Theta_{-Q_{0}}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

In
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C)
,

[\mathcal{L}]=\frac{k}{2}[p_{Q_{0}}^{*}\Theta_{Q_{0}}]+\frac{k}{2}[p_{-Q_{0}}^{*}\Theta_{-Q_{0}}]-k\nabla.

Proof of lemma.

In
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1})
,
O(1,1)
corresponds to the hyperplane class

[D]=\{\{R,\infty\}:R\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{F})\}.

Pulling back, we see that in
\mathrm{Pic}(Sym^{2}C)
,

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{f(P)=\infty}}}\{\{Q,P\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P/\{\pm 1\}}}{{f(P)=\infty}}}\left(\{\{Q,P\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}+\{\{Q,-P\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}\right)

For any
P\in C(\overline{F})
, consider the morphism
prod\circ\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(x-x(P)):\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, defined by

prod\circ\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(x-x(P))(\{Q,Q^{\prime}\})=(x(Q)-x(P))(x(Q^{\prime})-x(P)).

Note that the associated principal divisor is

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\left(prod\circ\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(x-x(P))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\{\{Q,P\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}+\{\{Q,-P\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}

\displaystyle\,-\{\{Q,Q_{0}\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}-\{\{Q,-Q_{0}\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}.

Hence in
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C)
,

[\mathcal{L}]=\frac{k}{2}\left(\{\{Q,Q_{0}\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}+\{\{Q,-Q_{0}\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}\right)

By
[
27
, Proposition V.3.6]
,

p_{Q_{0}}^{*}\Theta_{Q_{0}}=\{\{Q,Q_{0}\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}+\nabla

and

p_{-Q_{0}}^{*}\Theta_{-Q_{0}}=\{\{Q,-Q_{0}\}:Q\in C(\overline{F})\}+\nabla

Hence

[\mathcal{L}]=\frac{k}{2}[p_{Q_{0}}^{*}\Theta_{Q_{0}}]+\frac{k}{2}[p_{-Q_{0}}^{*}\Theta_{-Q_{0}}]-k\nabla

as desired.
∎

We now count
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C(F)
in two cases: those on the exceptional divisor
\nabla
, and those not on
\nabla
.

Case 1: Counting
\nabla(F)
.

Note that
\nabla
is a line; it is parametrized by

x^{-1}:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\nabla\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C

defined over
F
. Moreover, if
R\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)
and
x(Q)=x(-Q)=R
, we see that

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{L}}(x^{-1}(R))

\displaystyle=H(f(Q))H(f(-Q))

\displaystyle=H(f(Q))^{2}

\displaystyle=H(G(R))^{2}

Hence by Proposition
5.3
,

\displaystyle\,\#\{R\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x^{-1}(R))\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\#\{R\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(F):H(G(R))\leq B^{1/2}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})B^{2d/k}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}\left(B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B\right),

since
\mathrm{deg}G=\frac{k}{2}
.

Case 2: Counting
(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C-\nabla)(F)
.

Note that
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C-\nabla
is isomorphic to
Jac(C)-(point)
, and that the divisor
\mathcal{L}|_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C-\nabla}
comes from pullback of a degree
k
effective divisor on
Jac(C)
. Hence by Theorem
3.30
applied to
Jac(C)
, we see that

\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C-\nabla)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=O_{\mathcal{L},F}((\log B)^{r/2}),

where
r
is the rank of Mordell-Weil group
Jac(C)(F)
. In particular, this is smaller than the error term in Case 1.

Finally, putting both cases together we see that

\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})B^{2d/k}+O_{\mathcal{L},F}\left(B^{(2d-1)/k}\log B\right),

as desired.
∎

6.5.
Asymptotics formula of
N_{F,2}(P,B)

Putting the last three propositions together, we can now compute the asymptotics for
N_{F,2}(P,B)
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
P
be a portrait. Let
X_{1}(P)
be the dynamical modular curve associated to
P
, and let
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the natural forgetful map of degree
k
.

Let
H:\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\to[1,\infty)
be the absolute, multiplicative Weil height. For a fixed number field
F/\mathbb{Q}
of degree
d
, define

N_{F,2}(P,B):=\#\{x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(x):F]\leq 2,H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\},

(a)

Suppose
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is rational over
F
.
We have

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{6d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(3d-1)/k}\log B)

as
B\to\infty
.

(b)

Suppose
P\in\Gamma_{1}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is an elliptic curve over
F
. We have

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1})

as
B\to\infty
.

(c)

Suppose
P\in\Gamma_{2}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is a genus 2 curve over
F
. We have

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}\log B)

as
B\to\infty
.

(d)

Suppose
P\not\in\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
, then
X_{1}(P)
is a curve of genus
\geq 3
, and

N_{F,2}(P,B)=O_{P,F}(1).

Proof.

N_{F,2}(P,B)
is the cardinality of the set

S:=\{x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(x):F]\leq 2,H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\}

Note that
S
can be partitioned into
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
, where
S_{1}
is the degree 1 points,

S_{1}:=\{x\in X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\},

and
S_{2}
is the degree 2 points,

S_{2}:=\{x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(x):F]=2,H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\}.

Also consider

T:=\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))H(\pi_{P}(Q_{2}))\leq B^{2}\}.

which can be partitioned into
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
. Here
T_{1}
is

T_{1}:=\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)(F):[F(Q_{1},Q_{2}):F]=1,H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))H(\pi_{P}(Q_{2}))\leq B^{2}\}.

Since
[F(Q_{1},Q_{2}):F]=1
forces
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in X_{1}(P)(F)
,
T_{1}
tracks unordered pairs of points in
X_{1}(P)(F)^{2}
with bounded height,

T_{1}=\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in X_{1}(P)(F)^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}):H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))H(\pi_{P}(Q_{2}))\leq B^{2}\}.

On the other hand,

T_{2}:=\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)(F):[F(Q_{1},Q_{2}):F]=2,H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))H(\pi_{P}(Q_{2}))\leq B^{2}\}.

We have the obvious lemma.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\overline{x}\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})
be the Galois conjugate of
x
, when
[F(x):F]=2
.
Then the map
x\to\{x,\overline{x}\}
is a 2-to-1 map from
S_{2}
to
T_{2}
.

Hence
|S_{2}|=2|T_{2}|=2(|T|-|T_{1}|)
, and

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|S_{1}|+|S_{2}|=|S_{1}|+2(|T|-|T_{1}|).

We now use the last three propositions, and previous results on
N_{F,1}(P,B)
to calculate
N_{F,2}(P,B)
. The main term would come from
|T|
, while the
|S_{1}|,|T_{1}|
would contribute to error terms only.

Case (a):
P\in\Gamma_{0}
.

•

By Theorem
5.5
,
|S_{1}|=O_{P,F}(B^{2d/k})
.

•

By Proposition
6.3
,

|T|=\frac{1}{3}\tau_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})B^{6d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(3d-1)/k}).

The constant
\tau_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})
was calculated in Example
3.20
,
3.23
and
3.26
. We defined
c_{F,2}(P)
such that

2\cdot\frac{1}{3}\tau_{\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{L})=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P),

hence

2|T|=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{6d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(3d-1)/k}).

•

For
|T_{1}|
, focus on
H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))
, and partition
[0,B^{2}]
into
\asymp\log B
dyadic intervals. By Theorem
5.5
, for each dyadic interval
[U,2U]
we have

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in X_{1}(P)(F)^{2}:H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))\asymp U,H(\pi_{P}(Q_{2}))\ll\frac{B^{2}}{U}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P,F}\left(U^{2d/k}\cdot\left(\frac{B^{2}}{U}\right)^{2d/k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P,F}(B^{4d/k}).

Summing over all the intervals and since
d\geq 1
, we see that

|T_{1}|=O_{P,F}(B^{4d/k}\log B)=O_{P,F}(B^{2(3d-1)/k}\log B).

•

Hence

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{6d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(3d-1)/k}\log B).

Case (b):
P\in\Gamma_{1}
.
Let
r
be the rank of Mordell-Weil group
X_{1}(P)(F)
.

•

By Theorem
5.5
,
|S_{1}|=O_{P,F}\left((\log B)^{r/2}\right)
.

•

By Proposition
6.4
,

|T|=C_{\pi_{P},E,F}B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}).

We defined
c_{F,2}(P)
such that

2\cdot C_{\pi_{P},E,F}=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P),

hence

2|T|=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}).

•

For
|T_{1}|
, note that

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in X_{1}(P)(F)^{2}:H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))H(\pi_{P}(Q_{2}))\leq B^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{Q_{1}\in X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))\leq B^{2}\right\}^{2}.

By Theorem
5.5
,

\#\left\{Q_{1}\in X_{1}(P)(F):H(\pi_{P}(Q_{1}))\leq B^{2}\right\}=O_{P,F}\left((\log B)^{r/2}\right).

Therefore
|T_{1}|=O_{P,F}\left((\log B)^{r}\right)
.

•

Hence

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}).

Case (c):
P\in\Gamma_{2}
.

•

By Faltings’ theorem,
|S_{1}|=O_{P,F}(1)
.

•

By Proposition
6.5
,

|T|=\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}{G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}\log B).

The constant
\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}{G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}
was calculated in Example
3.19
,
3.22
and
3.25
. We defined
c_{F,2}(P)
such that

2\cdot\frac{1}{2}\tau_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}{G^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P),

hence

2|T|=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}\log B).

•

By Faltings’ theorem,
|T_{1}|=O_{P,F}(1)
.

•

Hence

N_{F,2}(P,B)=|Aut(\pi_{P})|\cdot c_{F,2}(P)B^{4d/k}+O_{P,F}(B^{2(2d-1)/k}\log B).

Case (d):
P\notin\Gamma_{0}\cup\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
.

•

By Faltings’ theorem,
|S_{1}|=O_{P,F}(1)
.

•

By Theorem
1.6
and the remark that follows,
|T|=O_{P,F}(1)
.

•

By Faltings’ theorem,
|T_{1}|=O_{P,F}(1)
.

•

Hence

N_{F,2}(P,B)=O_{P,F}(1).

∎

6.6.
Proof of Theorem
1.7
/Theorem
6.2

Lemma 6.9
.

With the notations in Theorem
6.7
, we have

\displaystyle\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset P\text{ for some $K/\mathbb{Q}$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},[F(c):F]\leq 2,H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}N_{F,2}(P,B)+Error_{P,F,2}(B).

Here

Error_{P,F,2}(B)=\begin{cases}B^{2(3d-1)/k}\log B&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{0}$,}\\
B^{2(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{1}$,}\\
B^{2(2d-1)/k}\log B&\text{when $P\in\Gamma_{2}$.}\\
\end{cases}

Proof.

The forgetful map
\pi_{P}:X_{1}(P)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
induces

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}.

Note that the following are equivalent:

•

c\in\overline{F}
satisfies
[F(c):F]\leq 2
and
\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset P
for some
K/F
of degree 2,

•

c=\pi_{P}(x)
for some
x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})
such that
[F(x):F]\leq 2
, and
c\neq\infty
.

Hence

\displaystyle\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset P\text{ for some $K/\mathbb{Q}$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},[F(c):F]\leq 2,H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\#\{c\in\overline{F}:c=\pi_{P}(x)\text{ for some $x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})$, with }[F(x):F]\leq 2\}+O_{P}(1).

Suppose
c=\pi_{P}(x)
for some
x\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})
with
[F(x):F]\leq 2
. We want to show that for generic such
c
,

\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap\{z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(z):F]\leq 2\}=\{\sigma(x):\sigma\in Aut(\pi_{P})\},

which would prove the lemma. By the definition of
Aut(\pi_{P})
, we see that right hand side is a subset of the left.

First, note that right hand side has size
|Aut(\pi_{P})|
with
O_{P}(1)
exceptions of
c
. This is because if right hand side is smaller than expected, it must mean that an unexpected coincidence

\sigma(x)=\sigma^{\prime}(x)

occured for some
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}\in Aut(\pi_{P})
. Since
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
is a proper Zariski-closed subset of the curve
X_{1}(P)
, it is of dimension 0; so there are finitely many exceptional
x
’s, hence finitely many exceptional
c
’s as well. To recap, with
O_{P}(1)
exceptions of
c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}))
, we have

\left|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap\{z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(z):F]\leq 2\}\right|\geq|Aut(\pi_{P})|.

Finally, we need to show that

\left|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap\{z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(z):F]\leq 2\}\right|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|

happens rarely. Suppose we have such an exceptional
c
, where there exists
x,z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})
not in the same
Aut(\pi_{P})
-orbit, with
[F(x):F]\leq 2
and
[F(z):F]\leq 2
, such that

\pi_{P}(x)=\pi_{P}(z)=c.

•

If
[F(c):F]=2
, then
F(x)=F(c)
, since
F(c)\subset F(x)
, and

2=[F(c):F]\leq[F(x):F]\leq 2.

Similarly,
F(z)=F(c)=F(x)
. Hence
(x,z)
is a quadratic point on some irreducible component
C
of

X_{1}(P)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{1}(P):=\{(x,z)\in X_{1}(P)\times X_{1}(P):\pi_{P}(x)=\pi_{P}(z)\},

which is not the graph of any
\sigma\in Aut(\pi_{P})
; we call such curve exceptional. We find that

–

If
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, then any exceptional
C
is smooth and has genus
\geq 1
(Explicit computation in Sage, see Appendix
A
).

–

If
P\in\Gamma_{1}
, then any exceptional
C
is smooth and has genus
\geq 4
(Explicit computation in Sage, see Appendix
A
).

–

If
P\in\Gamma_{2}
, then any exceptional
C
has geometric genus
\geq 3
.

Reason:
Consider the projection map
\pi:C\to X_{1}(P)
to first coordinate

C\hookrightarrow X_{1}(P)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{1}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}X_{1}(P),

and extend it to the smooth projective model
\widetilde{\pi}:\widetilde{C}\to X_{1}(P)
. Note that
\widetilde{\pi}
has degree
>1
; otherwise
C
is the graph of automorphism
\sigma:=\pi_{2}\circ\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}\in Aut(\pi_{P})
, contradicting the assumption that
C
is exceptional.

Since
X_{1}(P)
has genus 2, by applying Riemann-Hurwitz theorem to
\widetilde{\pi}:\widetilde{C}\to X_{1}(P)
we see that
genus(\widetilde{C})\geq 3
.

The number of quadratic points on these exceptional curves are few due to the lower bound on genus: by Theorem
6.7
, we see that

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})):\begin{aligned} &\left|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap\{z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(z):F]\leq 2\}\right|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|,\\
&[F(c):F]=2,H(c)\leq B\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle\ll_{P}

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{(x,z)\in(X_{1}(P)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{1}(P))(\overline{F}):\begin{aligned} &(x,z)\text{ lies in an exceptional curve $C$}\\
&\text{of $X_{1}(P)$, $[F(x,z):F]=2$,}\\
&\pi_{P}(x)=\pi_{P}(z),H(\pi_{P}(x))\leq B\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle\ll_{P}

\displaystyle\,\sum_{\text{exceptional curves $C$}}\#\left\{Q\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}C)(F):H(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}(Q))\leq B^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P}(Error_{P,F,2}(B))

•

Now suppose
c\in F
, and suppose there exists
x,z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})
not in the same
Aut(\pi_{P})
-orbit, with
[F(x):F]\leq 2
and
[F(z):F]\leq 2
, such that

\pi_{P}(x)=\pi_{P}(z)=c.

If
x\in X_{1}(P)(F)
, then
(x,z)
is a quadratic point on
X_{1}(P)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}X_{1}(P)
. The number of such
c
’s can be handled the same way as in the last case.

•

Hence suppose
c\in F
and
[F(x):F]=2
as well. Let
\overline{x}
be the Galois conjugate of
x
. Consider the diagonal closed subset
\Delta:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. That
c\in F
implies that
\{c,c\}\in\Delta(F)\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}(F)
, so

\{x,\overline{x}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P})^{-1}(\Delta)(F)\subset\mathrm{Sym}^{2}X_{1}(P)(F).

The number of rational points on the curve
(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P})^{-1}(\Delta)
is few: the degree of the restriction

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}:(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P})^{-1}(\Delta)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

is still
k
, there are
O_{P}(1)
irreducible components in
(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P})^{-1}(\Delta)
, and each irreducible component
C
has at most
O\left(B^{2d/k}\right)
rational points
Q\in C(F)
with
H(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}(Q))\leq B
(Theorem
5.5
), which is small compared to
Error_{P,F,2}(B)
.

In other words,

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})):\begin{aligned} &\left|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap\{z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(z):F]\leq 2\}\right|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|,\\
&c\in F,c=\pi_{P}(x)\text{ for $[F(x):F]=2$},H(c)\leq B\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{c\in\pi_{P}(X_{1}(P)(\overline{F})):c\in F,c=\pi_{P}(x)\text{ for $[F(x):F]=2$},H(c)\leq B\right\}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\,\#\left\{Q\in\left(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}\right)^{-1}(\Delta)(F):H(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\pi_{P}(Q))\leq B\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P}\left(B^{2d/k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P}(Error_{P,F,2}(B)).

This shows the rarity of
c
satisfying

\left|\pi_{P}^{-1}(c)\cap\{z\in X_{1}(P)(\overline{F}):[F(z):F]\leq 2\}\right|>|Aut(\pi_{P})|,

and finish the proof of lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
/Theorem
6.2
.

Recall the definition of
S_{F,2}(P,B)
: for each
P\in\mathscr{P}_{2}
,

S_{F,2}(P,B):=\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\cong P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}.

Our goal is to get an asymptotic formula for
S_{F,2}(P,B)
as
B\to\infty
.

Let
\{G_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the minimal strongly admissible graphs that strictly contains
P
, where
X_{1}(G_{i})
has a quadratic point over
F
. Assuming Morton-Silverman conjecture for quadratic extensions of
F
, this set of graphs is finite; enumerate them as
G_{1},\cdots,G_{m}
.

By inclusion-exclusion principle, we see that

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle\,\,-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset G_{i}\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,S_{F,2}(P,B)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}.

The main term would be

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset P\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{|Aut(\pi_{P})|}N_{F,2}(P,B)+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,2}(B)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,c_{F,2}(P)B^{a_{F,2}(P)}(\log B)^{b_{F,2}(P)}+O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,2}(B))

by Lemma
6.9
and Theorem
6.7
.

It now suffices to show that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset G_{i}\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

only contributes to the error term.

For each
G_{i}\supset P
,

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{0}
, by Proposition
2.3
(b),
\mathrm{deg}\pi_{G_{i}}\geq 2\cdot\mathrm{deg}\pi_{P}=2k
, so either
X_{1}(G_{i})
has genus
\leq 2
and

a_{F,1}(G_{i})\leq\frac{6d}{\mathrm{deg}\pi_{G_{i}}}\leq\frac{3d}{k}<\frac{2(3d-1)}{k},

or
X_{1}(G_{i})
has genus
\geq 3
and
a_{F,1}(G_{i})=0
. In either case, Theorem
6.7
implies that

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset G_{i}\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P,F}\left(B^{2(3d-1)/k}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,2}(B)).

•

If
P\in\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
, then
X_{1}(P)
has genus 1 or 2. Since the 10 such possible
P
’s (Table
2
and Table
3
, Appendix
A
) has no containment relationship, we see that
X_{1}(G_{i})
has genus
\geq 3
. Theorem
6.7
(d) implies that

\displaystyle\,\#\{c\in\overline{F}:\mathrm{Preper}(f_{c},K)\supset G_{i}\text{ for some $K/F$ of degree $2$ containing $c$},H(c)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P,F}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,O_{P,F}(Error_{P,F,2}(B)).

The result hence follows.
∎

Appendix A
Explicit presentation of
\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

For strongly admissible graphs
G
, we document a model of
X_{1}(G)
, and a presentation of the forgetful map
\pi_{G}:X_{1}(G)\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

when genus of
X_{1}(G)
is 0, 1 or 2.

Model and presentation of
\pi_{G}
were already known. For genus 0, it was calculated in
[
45
]
but scattered across several theorems there; we summarize it in the table below. For genus 1 and 2, it was calculated in
[
45
]

[
38
]
and was summarized nicely in
[
17
, Appendix C, Table 8]
. We replicate this table here, and add the Cremona labels
[
11
]
/LMFDB labels
[
35
]
whenever appropriate, for reader’s convenience.

We also document the automorphism group
Aut(\pi_{G})
for each
G
. When genus of
X_{1}(G)
is 0 or 1, we also record some information about exceptional curves associated to
G
, used in Lemma
5.6
and Lemma
6.9
. These were calculated manually in Sage
[
49
]
.

All pictures from below are taken from
[
17
, Appendix C, Table 8]
.

Appendix B
Proof of Proposition
6.4

This appendix proves Proposition
6.4
, restated here.

Proposition
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
with the standard metric.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
F
. Let
f:E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism over
F
of degree
k
such that
f(x)=f(-x)
; in particular,
k
is even. This induces

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
.

Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on the source
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
, and let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) height. Then

\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}=C_{f,E,F}B^{2d/k}+O_{f,E,F}(B^{(2d-1)/k}(\log B)^{r/2+1}),

as
B\to\infty
, for some constant
C_{f,E,F}>0
. Here
r=r(E/F)
is the rank of the Mordell-Weil group
E(F)
.

Assumption B.1
.

For ease of presentation, we will assume in the following that:

•

Class number of
F
is 1.

•

E
is semi-stable over
F
.

•

If
X/Spec(O_{F})
is the minimal regular model of
E/F
, and
v
is a non-archimedean place, we assume that the vertical fiber
X_{v}:=X\times_{\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})}\mathrm{Spec}(k(v))
is irreducible.

Our basic strategy is to utilize the addition map
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E\to E
, which gives
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle structure. We will count the rational points fiber by fiber.

In Section 1-5, we review some Arakelov geometry and calculus on arithmetic surfaces. Section 6-8 recasts Proposition
6.4
into a lattice point counting problem in a region; both the lattice and the region vary with
R\in E(F)
. The geometry of numbers set up is very similar to Masser-Vaaler
[
36
]
. In Section 9-10, we develop basic properties of the first successive minimum and the covolume of the involved lattices, and we track the dependence on
R\in E(F)
carefully. In Section 11-12, we prove the Lipschitz parametrizability of boundary of the regions in which we count lattice points. These results come together in Section 13 and gives us an asymptotic count of rational point on each fiber. Finally in section 14, we finish the proof of Proposition
6.4
, and discuss modifications needed wihout Assumption
B.1
.

B.1.
First Chern form of a Hermitian metric

For this subsection, let
M
be a compact connected Riemann surface, and
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
be a line bundle
L
on
M
equipped with a continuous Hermitian metric
\|\cdot\|
.

Let
s
be a nonzero, meromorphic section of
\mathcal{L}
. The first Chern form of
\mathcal{L}
is a distribution on the space of smooth functions
C^{\infty}(M)
defined by

c_{1}(\mathcal{L}):=\frac{\partial\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\log\|s\|+\delta_{\mathrm{div}(s)}.

This is independent of the choice of section
s
.

Remark
.

When the metric is smooth, the first Chern form can be represented by a
(1,1)
-form on
M
.

Proposition B.2

(Poincare-Lelong formula,
[
15
, Theorem 13.4]
)
.

\int_{M}c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{deg}(L).

Proposition B.3
.

Let
\omega
be a positive measure on
M
with
\omega(M)=1
. Suppose
\omega
is a scalar multiple of the first Chern form of some smoothly (resp. continuously) metrized line bundle.

Then for any line bundle
L
on
M
with a nonzero meromorphic section
s
, there is a unique smooth (resp. continuous) Hermitian metric
\|\cdot\|
on
L
such that the first Chern form

c_{1}(\|\cdot\|,L)=\mathrm{deg}(L)\cdot\omega,

and

\int_{M}\log\|s\|\omega=0.

Proof.

Suppose
\omega=a\cdot c_{1}(L^{\prime})
for some line bundle
L^{\prime}
with smooth (resp. continuous) metric. By integrating over
M
and Proposition
B.2
, we must have
a=\frac{1}{\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})}
.

Take an arbitrary smooth metric
\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}}
on
L^{\prime}
. Note that
\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})\omega
and
c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}},L^{\prime})
are both Chern forms of
L^{\prime}
with possibly different metrics. By definition, one sees that their difference is
\partial\overline{\partial}
-exact. In particular,

\omega-\frac{1}{\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})}\cdot c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}},L^{\prime})=\frac{\partial\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\varphi^{\prime}

for some smooth (resp. continuous) function
\varphi^{\prime}
on
M
.

Consider a line bundle
L
on
M
with a nonzero meromorphic section
s
. Take an arbitrary smooth metric
\|\cdot\|_{L}
on
L
. Then

\frac{\mathrm{deg}(L)}{\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})}c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}},L^{\prime})-c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L},L)

is a
(1,1)
-form with integral 0 over
M
, hence
d
-exact (from de Rham’s theorem), and hence
\partial\overline{\partial}
-exact
[
15
, Lemma VI.8.6]
. Hence

\frac{\mathrm{deg}(L)}{\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})}c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}},L^{\prime})-c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L},L)=\frac{\partial\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\varphi

for some smooth function
\varphi
on
M
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}(L)\cdot\omega

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{deg}(L)\left(\omega-\frac{1}{\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})}\cdot c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}},L^{\prime})\right)+\left(\frac{\mathrm{deg}(L)}{\mathrm{deg}(L^{\prime})}c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L^{\prime}},L^{\prime})-c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L},L)\right)+c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L},L)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\left(\mathrm{deg}(L)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}+\varphi\right)+c_{1}(\|\cdot\|_{L},L).

So the metric

\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{L}\cdot\mathrm{exp}\left(\mathrm{deg}(L)\cdot\varphi^{\prime}+\varphi\right)

is smooth (resp. continuous), and satisfies
c_{1}(\|\cdot\|,L)=\mathrm{deg}(L)\cdot w
. By taking a unique scalar multiple of this metric, we can make sure that

\int_{M}\log\|s\|\omega=0.

∎

For this paper, all metrics we consider would be in class (S), as defined below. These metrics were considered in
[
52
]
.

Definition B.4
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on compact, connected Riemann surface
M
. A metric
\|\cdot\|
on
L
is of class (S) if

•

The first Chern form
c_{1}(\|\cdot\|,L)
can always be extended to a continuous functional on the space of continuous functions
C(M)
with supremum norm.

•

There is a sequence of smooth metrics
\|\cdot\|_{n}
on
M
such that

–

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|\cdot\|_{n}}{\|\cdot\|}=1
uniformly on
M
.

–

For continuous function
g
on
M
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{M}gc_{1}(L,\|\cdot\|_{n})=\int_{M}gc_{1}(L,\|\cdot\|).

Proposition
B.3
also works for metrics of class (S).

Example B.5
.

Consider
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
with coordinates
[x_{0},x_{1}]
, and consider the line bundle
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
with the standard metric. The standard metric is of class (S), since it is well-approximated by the
L^{p}
-metrics on
O(1)
as
p\to\infty
.

One computes that the first Chern form
c_{1}(\mathbf{O(1)})
to be the functional: for continuous function
g
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
,

\int_{\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})}gc_{1}(\mathbf{O(1)}):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}g([1,e^{i\theta}])d\theta.

(See
[
52
, Section 6]
for a reference.)

Example B.6
.

Let
E(\mathbb{C})
be a complex elliptic curve. Let
f:E(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
be a morphism of degree
k
. Consider
\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
with the standard metric, and consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}=f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}
on
E(\mathbb{C})
.

For
x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})
, let
A_{f}(g)
be the average of
g
over fiber of
f
counted with multiplicity, i.e.

A_{f}(g)(x):=\sum_{y\in f^{-1}(x)}e_{f}(y)g(y),

with
e_{f}(y)
the ramification index of
f
at
y\in E(\mathbb{C})
.

Similar to the last example, one can then compute the first Chern form
c_{1}(f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})
to be the functional: for continuous function
g
on
E(\mathbb{C})
,

\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}gc_{1}(f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}A_{f}(g)([1,e^{i\theta}])d\theta.

Since
\mathrm{deg}(f^{*}O(1))=k
, Poincare-Lelong formula implies that

\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}c_{1}(f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})=k,

so
\frac{1}{k}c_{1}(f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})
is a probability measure on
E(\mathbb{C})
.

More generally, let
F
be a number field. Let
E/F
be an elliptic curve and
f:E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism of degree
k
over
F
. For each archimedean place
v
, let
\overline{f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}_{v}}
be the metrized line bundle on
E(\overline{F_{v}})
via base change. As before, we can define the probability measure

d\mu_{v}:=\frac{1}{k}c_{1}(\overline{f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}_{v}})

on
E(\overline{F_{v}})
for each
v
.

B.2.
Admissible metrics and Faltings volume on elliptic curves

Following Faltings
[
22
]
, we define the admissible metrics on line bundles of elliptic curves, and will use them to define the Faltings volume on global sections of a line bundle. These notions will be used when we discuss the Faltings-Riemann-Roch theorem in later sections. Finally, we will prove an upper bound on Faltings volume.

Let
E(\mathbb{C})
be a complex elliptic curve.

Definition B.7

(Canonical volume form)
.

Take a global holomorphic 1-form
\omega\in H^{0}(E,\Omega_{E}^{1})
such that its norm

\frac{i}{2}\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\omega\wedge\overline{\omega}=1.

The form
\nu_{can}:=\frac{i}{2}\omega\wedge\overline{\omega}
is called the canonical volume form on
E(\mathbb{C})
.

Definition B.8

(Admissible metric)
.

Let
L
be a line bundle on
E(\mathbb{C})
. By
[
15
, Theorem V.13.9(b)]
and Proposition
B.3
, there exists a smooth metric
\|\cdot\|
on
L
such that

c_{1}(\|\cdot\|,L)=\mathrm{deg}(L)\cdot\nu_{can}.

We will call this an
admissible metric
on
L
. When
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
is metrized with an admissible metric, we will call
\mathcal{L}
an admissible line bundle.

There is a unique metric on
L
that further satisfies

\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\log\|s\|\cdot\nu_{can}=0.

This metric will be called the
canonical admissible metric
on
L
.

The canonical admissible metric can be expressed in terms of the Arakelov-Green function (normalized as in Faltings
[
22
, p.393]
). Recall that the Arakelov-Green function
G:E(\mathbb{C})\times E(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is the unique function such that the following three properties hold:

•

G(P,Q)^{2}
is smooth on
E(\mathbb{C})\times E(\mathbb{C})
, and
G(P,Q)
vanishes only on the diagonal with multiplicity 1. In particular,
G(P,Q)
is bounded above on
E(\mathbb{C})\times E(\mathbb{C})
.

•

For any
P\neq Q\in E(\mathbb{C})
, we have

\frac{\partial_{Q}\overline{\partial_{Q}}}{\pi i}\log G(P,Q)=\nu_{can}(Q).

•

For any
P\in E(\mathbb{C})
,

\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\log G(P,Q)\nu_{can}(Q)=0.

For
Q\in E(\mathbb{C})
, consider the line bundle
O_{E}(Q)
with the canonical holomorphic section
1_{Q}
that vanishes at
Q
. The canonical admissible metric on
O_{E}(Q)
then satisfies

\|1_{Q}\|(P)=G(P,Q),

for
P\neq Q\in E(\mathbb{C})
.

We record two properties (
[
22
, p.394]
) of admissible metrics and Arakelov-Green’s function as follows:

•

Tensor product/inverse of admissible line bundles, with the natural metric, is admissible. Hence for a Weil divisor
D=n_{1}Q_{1}+\cdots+n_{r}Q_{r}
, with corresponding line bundle
O_{E}(D)
and canonical section
1_{D}
, we have

\|1_{D}\|(P)=G(P,Q_{1})^{n_{1}}\cdots G(P,Q_{r})^{n_{r}}

for
P\neq Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{r}\in E(\mathbb{C})
.

•

Arakelov-Green function is symmetric, i.e.
G(P,Q)=G(Q,P)
.

We next work towards the Faltings metric on determinant of cohomology. For a complex vector space
V
, define

\mathrm{det}\,(V):=\wedge^{top}V

to be the top exterior product of
V
. For a line bundle
L
on
E(\mathbb{C})
, define the determinant of its cohomology to be

\mathrm{det}\,H(E,L):=\mathrm{det}\,H^{0}(E,L)\otimes\mathrm{det}\,H^{1}(E,L)^{-1}.

Note that when
H^{1}(E,L)=0
, we have
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,L)=\mathrm{det}\,H^{0}(E,L)
.

Theorem B.9

(Faltings metric,
[
22
, Theorem 1]
)
.

For each admissible line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
E(\mathbb{C})
, one can define a unique metric on
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\mathcal{L})
such that the following properties hold:

•

Isometric isomorphism of line bundles
\mathcal{L}_{1}\cong\mathcal{L}_{2}
induces an isometry on

\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\mathcal{L}_{1})\cong\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\mathcal{L}_{2}).

•

If the metric on
\mathcal{L}
is changed by a factor
\alpha>0
, then the metric on
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\mathcal{L})
is changed by
\alpha^{\mathrm{deg}\mathcal{L}}
.

•

For a Weil divisor
D
on
E(\mathbb{C})
and a point
P\in E(\mathbb{C})
, let
D_{1}=D-P
, and consider the line bundles
O_{E}(D),O_{E}(D_{1})
with canonical admissible metrics. The fiber of
O_{E}(D)
over
P
, denoted
O_{E}(D)[P]
, has a metric by restriction. The natural isomorphism

\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(D))\cong\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(D_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}O_{E}(D)[P]

is an isometry.

•

For
\mathcal{L}=\Omega_{E}^{1}
, we require
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\Omega_{E}^{1})\cong H^{0}(E,\Omega_{E}^{1})
to be metrized by the metric:

\langle\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\rangle:=\frac{i}{2}\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\omega_{1}\wedge\overline{\omega_{2}}.

This unique metric is called the Faltings metric on
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\mathcal{L})
.

Definition B.10

(Faltings volume)
.

If
H^{1}(E,\mathcal{L})=0
, then
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,\mathcal{L})=\mathrm{det}\,H^{0}(E,\mathcal{L})
. In that case, the Faltings metric is a volume form on
H^{0}(E,\mathcal{L})
, called the Faltings volume on
H^{0}(E,\mathcal{L})
.

Let
O\in E(\mathbb{C})
be the identity, and
R\neq O\in E(\mathbb{C})
. Note that
H^{1}(E,O_{E}(R+O))=0
by Riemann-Roch, so we have Faltings volume on
H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
.

On the other hand, equip
O_{E}(R+O)
with the canonical admissible metric. This induces an
L^{2}
-norm on
g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
, defined as

\|g\|_{2}^{2}:=\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\|g\|^{2}\nu_{can}.

Proposition B.11
.

The Faltings volume of the unit ball

\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O)):\|g\|_{2}\leq 1\right\}

is bounded above, uniformly over
R\in E(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We follow the approach of Faltings
[
22
, Theorem 2]
.

Start with a fixed
R\in E(\mathbb{C})
. For
P,Q\in E(\mathbb{C})
, the lines
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(R+O-P-Q))
varies nicely with
P,Q
: they are the fibers of a line bundle
\mathcal{N}
on
E(\mathbb{C})^{2}
(see
[
10
, p.297]
for a construction of
\mathcal{N}
). By fixing a polarization
\mathrm{Pic}^{0}(E)\cong E
via
O_{E}(R-S)\to S
, we see that the map
\varphi:E(\mathbb{C})^{2}\to\mathrm{Pic}^{0}(E)\cong E
,

\varphi(P,Q)=O_{E}(R+O-P-Q)\cong O_{E}(R-(P+Q))\cong P+Q

is the addition map. Moreover, from the proof of Faltings
[
22
, Theorem 1]
,

i:\mathcal{N}\cong\varphi^{*}O_{E}(-R),

where the Faltings metric on
\mathcal{N}
is the pullback of some admissible metric on
O_{E}(-R)
. Hence the Faltings metric is a constant multiple of the canonical admissible metric on
O_{E}(-R)
, with the constant
c_{E}>0
depending only on
E
.

For general points
P,Q\in E(\mathbb{C})
where
P+Q\neq R
, the line bundle
O_{E}(R+O-P-Q)
is of degree 0 and has no global sections. So
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(R+O-P-Q))\cong\mathbb{C}
. Moreover at such points, under the isomorphism
i
, the image of
1
in
\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(R+O-P-Q))
corresponds to the section
1_{-R}
of
O_{E}(-R)
at
P+Q
. This correspondence implies that

\|1\|^{2}_{Fal}=c_{E}^{2}\|1_{-R}(P+Q)\|^{2}=\frac{c_{E}^{2}}{G(P+Q,R)^{2}}.

The restriction map at general points
P,Q\in E(\mathbb{C})
yields an exact sequence

0\to O_{E}(R+O-P-Q)\to O_{E}(R+O)\to O_{E}(R+O)[P]\oplus O_{E}(R+O)[Q]\to 0

which yields a natural isomorphism

\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(R+O))\cong\mathrm{det}\,H(E,O_{E}(R+O-P-Q))\otimes O_{E}(R+O)[P]\otimes O_{E}(R+O)[Q].

One computes the norm of this map to be
G(P,Q)
.

Let
1_{R+O}
be the canonical section for
O_{E}(R+O)
. Let
f_{1}1_{R+O},f_{2}1_{R+O}
be an orthonormal basis of
H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
, under the
L^{2}
-norm induced from admissible metric; here
f_{i}
are meromorphic functions on
E
with at worst a pole at
R
and
O
. Under the isomorphism,

f_{1}1_{R+O}\wedge f_{2}1_{R+O}\to(f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q))1\otimes 1_{R+O}(P)\otimes 1_{R+O}(Q).

We hence have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O)):\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\|g\|^{2}\nu_{can}\leq 1\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\|f_{1}1_{R+O}\wedge f_{2}1_{R+O}\|_{Fal}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\frac{1}{G(P,Q)^{2}}\cdot\left|f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q)\right|^{2}\|1\|_{Fal}^{2}\|1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(Q)\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi^{2}c_{E}^{2}}{2}\frac{1}{G(P+Q,R)^{2}G(P,Q)^{2}}\left|f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q)\right|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(Q)\|^{2}

We integrate over

S_{\epsilon}=\{(P,Q)\in E(\mathbb{C})^{2}:G(P,Q)>\epsilon,\,G(P+Q,R)>\epsilon\}.

for a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, such that
\mathrm{Vol}(S_{\epsilon})>0
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O)):\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\|g\|^{2}\nu_{can}\leq 1\}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}c_{E}^{2}}{2\mathrm{Vol}(S_{\epsilon})}\int_{(P,Q)\in S_{\epsilon}}\frac{\left|f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q)\right|^{2}}{G(P+Q,R)^{2}G(P,Q)^{2}}\|1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(Q)\|^{2}\nu_{can}^{2}

\displaystyle\ll_{E,\epsilon}\int_{(P,Q)\in S_{\epsilon}}\left|f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q)\right|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(Q)\|^{2}\nu_{can}^{2}.

Since
f_{1}1_{R+O},f_{2}1_{R+O}\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
are orthonormal, we have

\displaystyle\,\int_{(P,Q)\in S_{\epsilon}}\left|f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q)\right|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(Q)\|^{2}\nu_{can}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\int_{E(\mathbb{C})^{2}}\left|f_{1}(P)f_{2}(Q)-f_{2}(P)f_{1}(Q)\right|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\|1_{R+O}(Q)\|^{2}\nu_{can}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2\left(\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\|f_{1}(P)1_{R+O}(P)\|^{2}\nu_{can}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,2.

Thus we have

\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O)):\int_{E(\mathbb{C})}\|g\|^{2}\nu_{can}\leq 1\right\}\right)\ll_{E,\epsilon}1,

uniformly over
R\in E(\mathbb{C})
as desired.
∎

B.3.
Regular model of
E

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
F
.

Definition B.12

(Regular model of
E
)
.

A regular model
X\to\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is an integral, regular, proper, flat scheme of dimension 2, whose generic fiber is isomorphic to
E\to\mathrm{Spec}(F)
.

For elliptic curves, there is a particularly nice regular model: the minimal regular model
[
34
, Definition 9.3.14]
. Minimal regular model exists and is unique for elliptic curves
[
34
, Theorem 9.3.21]
. We will eventually work with minimal regular models so that Proposition
B.18
applies, but will stay at the level of regular models for now.

B.4.
Weil divisors on regular model of
E

Let
X
be a regular model of
E
. There are two types of irreducible Weil divisors on
X
:

•

The horizontal divisors: those whose image under
X\to\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is the entire
\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
. For
Q\in E(\overline{F})
, we will denote its closure in
X
by
D_{Q}
; this is a horizontal divisor, and all irreducible horizontal divisor on
X
arises this way.

•

The vertical divisors: those whose image under
X\to\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is a closed point.

For a non-archimedean place
v
, let
k(v)
be the residue field, and
X_{v}
be the fiber of
X
over
\mathrm{Spec}(k(v))
. By Assumption
B.1
,
X_{v}
is irreducible.

Since
X
is regular and
X_{v}
is irreducible of codimension 1, each
X_{v}
corresponds to a discrete valuation
\mathrm{val}_{X_{v}}
on
K(X)
, or equivalently a discrete absolute value
|\cdot|_{X_{v}}
, normalized such that for
a\in F
,

|a|_{X_{v}}=|a|_{v}.

We will also consider principal divisors. A rational function
g
on
E
uniquely extends to a rational function
g_{X}
on
X
. We note that if

\mathrm{div}(g)=\sum_{i}a_{i}Q_{i}

for
Q_{i}\in E(\overline{F})
, then

\mathrm{div}(g_{X})=\sum_{i}a_{i}D_{Q_{i}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\mathrm{val}_{X_{v}}(g_{X})X_{v}.

B.5.
Intersection theory on regular model of
E

Let
E/F
be an elliptic curve, and
X/\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
be a regular model of
E
. For two smoothly metrized line bundles
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
on
X
, Deligne
[
14
]
defined an intersection number
\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}\rangle
which is bilinear and includes contributions from archimedean places. The same definition works for metrized line bundle of class (S) as well (
[
52
, Section 1]
). From here, we can define

•

The intersection number between a metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}
of class (S), and a Weil divisor
D
.

This can be done by first equipping
O_{X}(D)
with the metric whose first Chern form equals that of
\mathcal{L}
, via Proposition
B.3
; we then take the intersection number of
\mathcal{L}
and
O_{X}(D)
under this metric.

For horizontal Weil divisor, this definition is equivalent to the degree of metrized line bundle pulled back to
\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})

[
37
, Proposition 4.20]
.

•

The intersection number between two Weil divisors
D_{1},D_{2}
.

By Proposition
B.3
, we can equip
O_{X}(D_{1})
and
O_{X}(D_{2})
with metric that has the same first Chern form, then take the intersection number of such metrized line bundles. This definition is compatible with Arakelov
[
1
]
, where the canonical admissible metric is always used.

We will use
\langle-,-\rangle
to denote this intersection pairing. The precise definition would not matter to us; we will only use the formal properties of intersection number in the following propositions.

The intersection number is important for us because height can be expressed via this intersection number.

Proposition B.13

(
[
37
, Proposition 4.20]
)
.

Let
X
be a regular model of
E/F
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a metrized line bundle on
X
, and
H_{\mathcal{L}}
be the absolute, multiplicative Weil height on
X
induced by
\mathcal{L}
.

If
Q\in E(\overline{F})
and
D_{Q}
is its closure in
X
, then

\log H_{\mathcal{L}}(Q)=\frac{1}{[F(Q):\mathbb{Q}]}\langle\mathcal{L},D_{Q}\rangle

We document other properties of the intersection number as follows.

Proposition B.14

(Intersection with a vertical divisor,
[
37
, Lemma 4.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a regular model of
E/F
. Let
\mathcal{L}
be a metrized line bundle on
X
.

If
v
is a non-archimedean place of
F
, then

\langle\mathcal{L},X_{v}\rangle=\mathrm{deg}(\mathcal{L})\log|k(v)|.

Proposition B.15

(Intersection with a principal divisor,
[
37
, Proposition 4.17(2)]
)
.

Let
X
be a regular model of
E/F
, and
\mathcal{L}
a metrized line bundle on
X
.

For each archimedean place
v
, let
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}}
be the metrized line bundle over
X(\overline{F_{v}})
, induced from
\mathcal{L}
; let
c_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}})
be the first Chern form of
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}}
.

For a rational function
g_{X}
on
X
, we have

\langle\mathcal{L},\mathrm{div}(g_{X})\rangle=\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}c_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}}).

Proposition B.16

(Adjunction formula,
[
37
, Lemma 4.26]
)
.

Let
X/\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
be a regular model of
E/F
. The relative dualizing sheaf
\omega_{X}
exists
[
34
, Theorem 6.4.32]
. For each archimedean place, we metrize
\omega_{X}
with the canonical admissible metric (Section
B.2
).

Let
Q\in E(\overline{F})
, and
D_{Q}
be the irreducible horizontal divisor on
X
. Metrize
O_{X}(D_{Q})
with the canonical admissible metric. Then

\langle O_{X}(D_{Q}),O_{X}(D_{Q})+\omega_{X}\rangle=0.

Proposition B.17

(Faltings-Hriljac theorem)
.

Let
X
be a regular model of
E/F
.

Let
O\in E(F)
be the identity, and
Q\in E(F)
. Metrize
O_{X}(D_{Q}-D_{O})
with the canonical admissible metric. If
H_{NT}
is the (multiplicative) Néron-Tate height of
E
, then
