\langle O_{X}(D_{Q}-D_{O}),O_{X}(D_{Q}-D_{O})\rangle=-2d\log H_{NT}(Q)

Proof.

We assumed that all vertical fibers are irreducible (Assumption
B.1
). By Proposition
B.14
,
D_{Q}-D_{O}
is perpendicular to all vertical divisors. Faltings-Hriljac theorem
[
22
, Theorem 4]
thus applies to get the result.
∎

The next result is the reason we work with minimal regular model, instead of any regular model.

Proposition B.18

(
[
13
, Theorem 6.1, Proposition 6.2]
)
.

Let
X
be the minimal regular model of
E/F
.

Let
O\in E(F)
be the identity, and
Q\in E(F)
. Then,

\langle O_{X}(D_{Q}),O_{X}(D_{Q})\rangle=\langle O_{X}(D_{O}),O_{X}(D_{O})\rangle.

If furthermore
E
is semi-stable over
F
, then

\langle O_{X}(D_{O}),O_{X}(D_{O})\rangle=-\frac{1}{12}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|,

where
\Delta_{E/F}
is the minimal discriminant of the elliptic curve
E/F
.

B.6.
Reinterpreting counting points on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
as counting rational functions on
E

We first recall the set up of Proposition
6.4
.

Let
F/\mathbb{Q}
be a number field of degree
d
, and consider
\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
F
with the standard metric.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve over
F
, and let
X
over
\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
be the minimal regular model. Let
f:E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a morphism over
F
of degree
k
such that
f(x)=f(-x)
; in particular,
k
is even. This induces

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f:\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E\to\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\mathbb{P}^{1},

also of degree
k
.

Consider the metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}=(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}f)^{*}\mathbf{O(1,1)}
on the source
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
, and let
H_{\mathcal{L}}:(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F)\to[1,\infty)
be the induced (absolute, multiplicative) height.

Our eventual goal is to prove Proposition
6.4
, i.e. getting an asymptotic formula for

\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

as
B\to\infty
. As a first step, we want to reinterpret this point counting problem in terms of counting rational functions on
E
instead.

If
D
is a Weil divisor on
E
, we let
O_{E}(D)
be the corresponding line bundle, and
H^{0}(E,O_{E}(D))
be its global sections; this can also be identified with the rational functions
g
on
E
satisfying
\mathrm{div}(g)+D\geq 0
. Any such
g
extends uniquely to a rational function
g_{X}
on
X
.

Proposition B.19
.

\displaystyle\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in E(F)}\#\left\{g_{X}\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}:\,\right.

\displaystyle\left.\,\,\,\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq\frac{d}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)\right\}

Proof.

Our goal is to count

\displaystyle\,\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\#\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H(f(Q_{1}))H(f(Q_{2}))\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\#\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))\leq\log B\}

We can first fix the sum
Q_{1}+Q_{2}=R\in E(F)
, then count the points in each fiber
(Sym^{2}E)(F)\to E(F)
over
R
. This gives

\displaystyle\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in E(F)}\#\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):Q_{1}+Q_{2}=R,\,\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))\leq\log B\}

For each
R\in E(F)
, we next try to rewrite the inner sum

(B.1)

\#\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):Q_{1}+Q_{2}=R,\,\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))\leq\log B\}.

By Riemann-Roch theorem, there is a bijection between

(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}\leftrightarrow\{\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):Q_{1}+Q_{2}=R\}

as follows: for
g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0
, its divisor is of the shape

\mathrm{div}(g)=\frac{1}{[F(Q_{1}):F]}\left(Q_{1}+Q_{2}\right)-R-O.

We can then send
g\to\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
.

Hence to count the pair
\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F)
, we can count
g\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}
instead. We need to express the condition

\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))\leq\log B

in terms of
g
, which we do next.

For each
g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
, the unique extension
g_{X}
on
X
has divisor

\mathrm{div}(g_{X})=\frac{1}{[F(Q_{1}):F]}\left(D_{Q_{1}}+D_{Q_{2}}\right)-D_{R}-D_{O}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\mathrm{val}_{X_{v}}(g_{X})X_{v}.

By Proposition
B.13
,

\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))=\frac{1}{[F(Q_{1}):\mathbb{Q}]}\langle f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)},D_{Q_{1}}+D_{Q_{2}}\rangle.

Expressing
D_{Q_{1}}+D_{Q_{2}}
in terms of
\mathrm{div}(g_{X})
, we get

\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))=\frac{1}{d}\langle f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)},D_{R}+D_{O}-\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\mathrm{val}_{X_{v}}(g_{X})X_{v}+\mathrm{div}(g_{X})\rangle.

Using bilinearity of intersection number, and simplifying each term using Proposition
B.13
, Proposition
B.14
and Proposition
B.15
, we get

\displaystyle\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))+\frac{1}{d}\left(-\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\mathrm{val}_{X_{v}}(g_{X})\cdot k\log|k(v)|+\right.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\left.\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}c_{1}\left(\overline{f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}_{v}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))+\frac{k}{d}\left(\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)

The last equation comes from how we normalize
|\cdot|_{X_{v}}
(Section
B.4
), and how
d\mu_{v}
was defined (Example
B.6
).

Therefore the condition

\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))\leq\log B

is equivalent to

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq\frac{d}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)

and the result follows.
∎

B.7.
Finding good representative of
(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}

We next try to pin down a specific representative for each equivalence class of
(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}
, so that the
F^{\times}
-action can be removed.

Fix a
g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0
, and let
g_{X}
be its unique extension to
X
.

Pinning down the finite places.

For finite places
v
, since
|\cdot|_{X_{v}}
extends the valuation on
F_{v}
,

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}

equals the valuation of some fractional ideal of
F
. By Assumption
B.1
,
F
has class number 1, so there exists some
a_{g}\in F^{\times}/O_{F}^{\times}
such that

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|a_{g}|_{v}.

In other words,

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|a_{g}^{-1}g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0.

We can hence impose the condition

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,

while we still have freedom to change
g_{X}
by some factor in
O_{F}^{\times}
.

Pinning down the infinite places.

For infinite places
v
, we first introduce some notations as in Masser-Vaaler
[
36
]
. They would be used in later geometry of numbers arguments as well.

•

Let
r_{1},r_{2}
be the number of real/complex embeddings of
F
, so that
r_{1}+2r_{2}=d
.

•

Denote by
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
the plane

\Sigma:=\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{r_{1}+r_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}:x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r_{1}+r_{2}}=0\}.

Also let

\delta=(1,\cdots,1,2,\cdots,2)\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}},

where the first
r_{1}
entries are 1, and the last
r_{2}
entries are 2.

•

By Dirichlet’s unit theorem, the log embedding
O_{F}^{\times}\to\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
has image a lattice in
\Sigma
, and has kernel the roots of unity
\mu_{F}
of
F
.

Let
\Omega
be a fundamental domain of this lattice in
\Sigma
. For real
T>0
, we denote the set
\Omega(T)\subset\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
to be the vector sum

\Omega(T):=\Omega+(-\infty,\log T]\delta.

For a place
v
, define the map
\mathrm{ev}_{v}:H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}-0\to\mathbb{R}
by

\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{v})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\log|g_{v}|_{X_{v}}&\text{if $v$ is non-archimedean,}\\
\displaystyle\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}&\text{if $v$ is archimedean.}\end{cases}

Note that for
a\in O_{F}^{\times}
, we have

\mathrm{ev}_{v}(ag_{X})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{X})&\text{ if $v$ is non-archimedean,}\\
\log|a|_{v}+\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{X})&\text{ if $v$ is archimedean.}\end{cases}

Hence the condition

g_{X}\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/O_{F}^{\times}\text{ and }\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{X})\leq d\log T,

is equivalent to requiring

g_{X}\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/\mu_{F}\text{ and }(\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{X}))_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in\Omega\left(T\right),

via multiplying
g_{X}
by a suitable
a\in O_{F}^{\times}
. Since
\mathrm{ev}_{v}
is
\mu_{F}
-invariant for any place
v
, we can remove the
\mu_{F}
-action directly.

We summarize how we pin down a good representative as follows.

Proposition B.20
.

There is a bijection between

\#\{g_{X}\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq\log T\}

and

\frac{1}{|\mu_{F}|}\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,(\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{X}))_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in\Omega\left(T^{1/d}\right)\right\}.

Definition B.21
.

For each
R\in E(F)
, define

S_{\Omega,R}(T)\subset\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}

as the subset

S_{\Omega,R}(T):=\left\{(g_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}:(\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{v}))\in\Omega(T)\right\}.

Note that
S_{\Omega,R}(T)=TS_{\Omega,R}(1)
. Hence we can restate Proposition
B.20
as

Proposition B.22
.

There is a bijection between

\#\{g_{X}\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0)/F^{\times}:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq\log T\}

and

\frac{1}{|\mu_{F}|}\#\left\{g_{X}\in(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0):\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in T^{1/d}S_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}.

Here
g_{X}\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
is embedded diagonally into
\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v})
.

Applying this proposition with

T=\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{d}{k}(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O)))\right),

together with Proposition
B.19
, we get

Corollary B.23
.

\displaystyle\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mu_{F}|}\sum_{R\in E(F)}\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,\,\right.

\displaystyle\left.\,\,\,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)\right)S_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}.

Here
g_{X}\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
is embedded diagonally into
\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}(H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v})
.

B.8.
Reinterpreting counting points on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
in terms of
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
.

We can repackage the constraint on finite places

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0

using
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
and Möbius inversion. The end result will be recorded in Corollary
B.26
.

Proposition B.24
.

There is a bijection

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\cong\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O)):\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0\text{ for all non-arch place $v$}\right\}.

Proof.

Since cohomology commutes with flat base change, we have

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F\cong H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O)).

Since
X\to\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is flat, so
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
is torsion-free, thus the natural map

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\to H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F\cong H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))

is injective. For any non-archimedean place
v
, by definition
g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
has no poles along
X_{v}
. This is equivalent to
\mathrm{val}_{X_{v}}(g_{X})\geq 0
, or equivalently,
\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0
, as desired.
∎

Proposition B.25
.

\displaystyle\#\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(a)\subset O_{F}}\mu((a))\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}\left(\frac{T}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|^{1/d}}\right)\right\}.

Here
\mu(I)
is the Möbius function on a fractional ideal
I
of
F
.

Proof.

By Möbius inversion, counting

\#\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0\}

is equivalent to

\sum_{I\subset O_{F}}\mu(I)\#\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq\log|I|_{v}\text{ for all non-arch place $v$}\}.

Here
I
is an integral ideal of
O_{F}
, and
\mu(I)
is the Möbius function on fractional ideals of
F
. The absolute value
|\cdot|_{v}
on
F
extends naturally to fractional ideals of
F
, hence we can make sense of
|I|_{v}
.

By Assumption
B.1
,
F
has class number 1, so all ideals are principal. Therefore,

\displaystyle\#\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(a)\subset O_{F}}\mu((a))\#\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\right.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\left.\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq\log|a|_{v}\text{ for all non-arch place $v$},(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)\right\}

Note that

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{ev}_{v}(g_{X})\leq d\log T,

is equivalent to

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{ev}_{v}(a^{-1}g_{X})\leq d\log T-\sum_{v\in M_{\infty}}\log|a|_{v}=d\log\left(\frac{T}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|^{1/d}}\right).

So

(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)\Leftrightarrow(a^{-1}g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}\left(\frac{T}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|^{1/d}}\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle\#\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}=0,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(a)\subset O_{F}}\mu((a))\#\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\right.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\left.\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|a^{-1}g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0,\,(a^{-1}g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}\left(\frac{T}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|^{1/d}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(a)\subset O_{F}}\mu((a))\#\left\{g\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0:\right.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\left.\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0,\,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}\left(\frac{T}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|^{1/d}}\right)\right\}

The result now follows by repackaging the constraint on finite place via Proposition
B.24
.
∎

Combining the last proposition with Corollary
B.23
, we have

Corollary B.26
.

\displaystyle\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mu_{F}|}\sum_{R\in E(F)}\sum_{(a)\subset O_{F}}\mu((a))\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:\right.

\displaystyle\left.\,\,\,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)S_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}

We have now transformed the counting problem on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E
to counting points of the lattice

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v},

lying inside the homogeneous region

\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)S_{\Omega,R}(1),

as
B\to\infty
, for each
R\in E(F)
. This can be approached by geometry of numbers.

In the next few sections, we will develop the basic properties for this lattice, such as the first successive minimum and the covolume.

B.9.
The first successive minimum of the lattice
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))

For each
R\in E(F)
, we seek to understand the lattice

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}.

In this section, we first define a metric on the ambient space
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
, then prove a lower bound of the first successive minimum of the lattice.

The line bundle
O_{E}(R+O)
has a canonical section
1_{R+O}
. For an archimedean place
v
, by Proposition
B.3
there is a unique metric
\|\cdot\|_{v}
on
O_{E(\overline{F_{v}})}(R+O)
such that the first Chern form of the metric equals
d\mu_{v}
, and

\int_{E(\overline{F_{v}})}\log\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}d\mu_{v}=0.

Definition B.27

(Metric on
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
)
.

For
g_{v}\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}
, we define the
L^{2}
-norm

\|g_{v}\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{E(\overline{F_{v}})}|g_{v}|^{2}\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}^{2}d\mu_{v}.

The
L^{2}
-norm on
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}
can now be defined by

\|(g_{v})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\|_{2}^{2}:=\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\|g_{v}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{E(\overline{F_{v}})}|g_{v}|^{2}\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}^{2}d\mu_{v}.

Recall that the first successive minimum
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
of a lattice
\Lambda
is defined to be the length of shortest nonzero vector of the lattice.

Proposition B.28
.

The first successive minimum of
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
satisfies

\lambda_{1}(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))\gg_{E,f,F}\frac{1}{(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{1/k}}.

Proof.

By Proposition
B.24
, we can identify
g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0
as
g_{X}\neq 0\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0
with

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0.

Our goal is to find a lower bound for

\|g_{X}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}|g_{X}|_{v}^{2}\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}^{2}d\mu_{v}.

Restricting
g_{X}
to the generic fiber
E
, suppose that

\mathrm{div}(g)=\frac{1}{[F(Q_{1}):F]}(Q_{1}+Q_{2})-R-O.

As in the proof of Proposition
B.19
, we have

\displaystyle\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))+\frac{k}{d}\left(\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right).

Since

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0,

we see that

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq\frac{d}{k}\left(\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right).

Recall that the logarithmic Néron-Tate height
h_{NT}
is a quadratic form on
E(F)
, and satisfies

\log H(f(Q))=h_{NT}(Q)+O_{E,f,F}(1)

for any
Q\in E(F)
, with the error
O_{E,f,F}(1)
independent of
Q
.

Switching to the Néron-Tate height, we see that

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq\frac{d}{k}\left(h_{NT}(Q_{1})+h_{NT}(Q_{2})-h_{NT}(R)-h_{NT}(O)\right)+O_{E,f,F}(1).

Since
h_{NT}(O)=0
, and

\displaystyle h_{NT}(Q_{1})+h_{NT}(Q_{2})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(h_{NT}(Q_{1}+Q_{2})+h_{NT}(Q_{1}-Q_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(h_{NT}(R)+h_{NT}(Q_{1}-Q_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}h_{NT}(R),

we have

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq-\frac{d}{2k}h_{NT}(R)+O_{E,f,F}(1).

Switching back to the height
H(f(Q))
, we have

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq-\frac{d}{2k}\left(\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))\right)+O_{E,f,F}(1),

independent of
R\in E(F)
.

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Jensen’s inequality (
\log
is concave), we have for each
v\in M_{F,\infty}
,

\displaystyle\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}|g_{X}|_{v}^{2}\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}^{2}d\mu_{v}\geq

\displaystyle\,\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}|g_{X}|_{v}\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{exp}\left(2\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\left(\log|g_{X}|_{v}-\log\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}\right)d\mu_{v}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{exp}\left(2\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle\|g_{X}\|_{2}^{2}=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}|g_{X}|_{v}^{2}\|1_{R+O}\|_{v}^{2}d\mu_{v}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{exp}\left(2\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,(r_{1}+r_{2})\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{2}{r_{1}+r_{2}}\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\text{ (by AM-GM inequality)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,(r_{1}+r_{2})\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{d}{k(r_{1}+r_{2})}\left(\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))\right)+O_{E,f,F}(1)\right)

Since
d=r_{1}+2r_{2}\leq 2(r_{1}+r_{2})
, we have

\|g_{X}\|_{2}^{2}\gg_{E,f,F}\mathrm{exp}\left(-\frac{2}{k}(\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O)))\right)=\frac{1}{(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{2/k}}.

Taking square root on both sides, we get the desired result.
∎

Proposition B.29
.

If
T\ll_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/(2k)}
with a sufficiently small big-
O
constant, then

\#\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)\}=0.

Proof.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,D_{R}+D_{O})-0
exists. By Proposition
B.24
, we can identify
g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0
as
g_{X}\neq 0\in H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))-0
with

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0.

As a rational function on
E
, suppose

\mathrm{div}(g)=\frac{1}{[F(Q_{1}):F]}(Q_{1}+Q_{2})-R-O.

As in the proof of Proposition
B.19
, we have

\displaystyle\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))+\frac{k}{d}\left(\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}+\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)

Since

\sum_{v\in M_{F,finite}}\log|g_{X}|_{X_{v}}\leq 0,

we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{d}{k}\left(\log H(f(Q_{1}))+\log H(f(Q_{2}))-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)

Recall that the logarithmic Néron-Tate height
h_{NT}
is a quadratic form on
E(F)
, and satisfies

\log H(f(Q))=h_{NT}(Q)+O_{E,f,F}(1)

for any
Q\in E(F)
, with the error
O_{E,f,F}(1)
independent of
Q
.

Switching to the Néron-Tate height, we see that

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq\frac{d}{k}\left(h_{NT}(Q_{1})+h_{NT}(Q_{2})-h_{NT}(R)-h_{NT}(O)\right)+O_{E,f,F}(1).

Since
h_{NT}(O)=0
, and

\displaystyle h_{NT}(Q_{1})+h_{NT}(Q_{2})=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(h_{NT}(Q_{1}+Q_{2})+h_{NT}(Q_{1}-Q_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\left(h_{NT}(R)+h_{NT}(Q_{1}-Q_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}h_{NT}(R),

we have

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq-\frac{d}{2k}h_{NT}(R)+O_{E,f,F}(1).

Switching back to the height
H(f(Q))
, we have

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\geq-\frac{d}{2k}\left(\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))\right)+O_{E,f,F}(1),

independent of
R\in E(F)
.

By definition,
(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in S_{\Omega,R}(T)
implies

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{X}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq d\log T.

Hence the existence of
g_{X}
implies that

d\log T\geq-\frac{d}{2k}\left(\log H(f(R))+\log H(f(O))\right)+O_{E,f,F}(1).

which is equivalent to

T\gg_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/(2k)}

Hence if
T\ll_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/(2k)}
with a sufficiently small big-
O
constant, we get a contradiction if
g_{X}
exists.
∎

B.10.
Covolume of the lattice
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))

We defined a
L^{2}
-norm on
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
in the last section, which induces a Haar measure
\mathrm{Vol}_{2}
on
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
. To apply geometry of numbers, we want to compute the covolume of the lattice

\mathrm{covol}_{2}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right):=\mathrm{Vol}_{2}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}/H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)

It would be easier to calculate the covolume using another Haar measure on
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
: the Faltings volume
\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}
. This measure allows the Faltings-Riemann-Roch theorem to apply. For lattice point counting, we only care about quotient of measures of two sets, so it does not matter which Haar measure we use.

We will work towards stating Faltings-Riemann-Roch theorem (following Faltings
[
22
]
), and then calculate the Faltings covolume of
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
.

Definition B.30

(Euler characteristic of finitely generated
O_{F}
-module)
.

For a finitely generated
O_{F}
-module
M
, together with a Haar measure
\mathrm{Vol}
on
M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
, we can define the Euler characteristic of
M
by

\chi(M)=-\log\left(\mathrm{Vol}\left(M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}/M\right)/|M_{tor}|\right)+rank_{O_{F}}(M)\cdot\log\mathrm{Vol}\left(O_{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}/O_{F}\right).

Remark
.

By definition of discriminant of number fields,

\mathrm{Vol}\left(O_{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}/O_{F}\right)=|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|^{1/2}.

The term
\mathrm{rank}_{O_{F}}(M)\cdot\log\mathrm{Vol}\left(O_{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}/O_{F}\right)
is a normalization term to make sure that
\chi(O_{F})=0
.

For each archimedean place
v
,
X(\overline{F_{v}})
is an elliptic curve. In Section
B.2
, we defined admissible metrics on line bundles over
X(\overline{F_{v}})
and Faltings metrics on the determinant of cohomology for admissible line bundles.

Definition B.31
.

An metrized line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
X
is called admissible, if for each archimedean place
v
the base change
\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}}
on
X(\overline{F_{v}})
is equipped with an admissible metric.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an admissible line bundle on
X
. For each archimedean place
v
, the Faltings metric gives a Haar measure on the formal difference

H^{0}(X(\overline{F_{v}}),\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}})-H^{1}(X(\overline{F_{v}}),\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}}).

We therefore obtain a Haar measure on the formal difference

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(X(\overline{F_{v}}),\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}})-\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{1}(X(\overline{F_{v}}),\overline{\mathcal{L}_{v}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,H^{0}(X,\mathcal{L})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}-H^{1}(X,\mathcal{L})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}.

This allows us to define

\chi(\mathcal{L}):=\chi(H^{0}(X,\mathcal{L}))-\chi(H^{1}(X,\mathcal{L})).

Example B.32
.

Suppose
R\in E(F)
, and let
\mathcal{L}=O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})
, metrized with the canonical admissible metric at each archimedean place. We want to compute
\chi(\mathcal{L})
.

We first calculate
\chi(H^{0}(X,\mathcal{L}))
. By definition,

\displaystyle\chi(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)+\log|H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))_{tor}|

\displaystyle\,+\mathrm{rank}_{O_{F}}(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))\cdot\log\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(O_{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}/O_{F}\right).

Recall that

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))_{tor}=0

since
X\to\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is flat. Moreover, by Riemann-Roch theorem for curves,

\mathrm{rank}_{O_{F}}(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))=\mathrm{rank}_{F}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))=2.

Hence,

\displaystyle\chi(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)+2\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)+\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|.

For
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{L})
, again by Riemann-Roch theorem for curves,

\mathrm{rank}_{O_{F}}(H^{1}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))=\mathrm{rank}_{F}H^{1}(E,O_{E}(R+O))=0,

so
H^{1}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
is torsion. Since
X\to\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
is flat, each fiber has arithmetic genus 1, so the same argument actually shows that
H^{1}(X_{v},O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})|_{X_{v}})=0
for any non-archimedean place
v\in\mathrm{Spec}(O_{F})
. Grauert’s theorem
[
27
, Corollary III.12.9]
then applies to show that
H^{1}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
is torsion-free. Hence
H^{1}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))=0
, and

\chi(H^{1}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))=

\displaystyle\,\chi(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))-\chi(H^{1}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\log\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)+\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|.

Theorem B.33

(Faltings-Riemann-Roch Theorem,
[
22
, Theorem 3]
)
.

Metrize
O_{X}
and the relative dualizing sheaf
\omega_{X}
by admissible metrics. Then for any admissible line bundle
\mathcal{L}
, we have

\chi(\mathcal{L})=\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}-\omega_{X}\rangle+\chi(O_{X}).

Remark
.

Applying Faltings-Riemann-Roch theorem to
\omega_{X}
, we see that
\chi(\omega_{X})=\chi(O_{X})
. Normalizing, we arrived at the (unstable) Faltings height of an elliptic curve,

h_{Fal}(E):=\frac{1}{d}\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

Proposition B.34
.

Metrize
O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})
with the canonical admissible metric at each archimedean place. Then

\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)=\frac{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|^{1/4}\cdot|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|}{\mathrm{exp}\left(d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E)\right)}\cdot\frac{1}{H_{NT}(R)^{d}}.

Here
\Delta_{E/F}
is the minimal discrminant of
E/F
,
H_{NT}(R)
is the (multiplicative) Néron-Tate height of
R
and
h_{Faltings}(E)
is the (logarithmic) Faltings height of
E
.

Proof.

From Example
B.32
, we see that

\log\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)=-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))+\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|.

By Faltings-Riemann-Roch theorem, we have

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})-\omega_{X}\rangle+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}),\omega_{X}\rangle+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

By adjunction formula (Proposition
B.16
),

\langle O_{X}(D_{R}),O_{X}(D_{R})+\omega_{X}\rangle=0,\,\,\,\langle O_{X}(D_{O}),O_{X}(D_{O})+\omega_{X}\rangle=0.

Hence by linearity of intersection pairing,

\displaystyle\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R})\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O})\rangle+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{3}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R})\rangle+\frac{3}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O})\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}-D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}-D_{O})\rangle+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}).

By Proposition
B.17
,

\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}-D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}-D_{O})\rangle=-2d\log H_{NT}(R),

where
H_{NT}
is the multiplicative Néron-Tate height.

By Assumption
B.1
,
E
is semi-stable over
F
. Proposition
B.18
then implies that for any
R\in E(F)
,

\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R})\rangle=\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O})\rangle=-\frac{1}{12}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|,

where
\Delta_{E/F}
is the minimal discriminant of the elliptic curve
E/F
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,3\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O}),\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{O})\rangle+d\log H_{NT}(R)+\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1}{4}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|+d\log H_{NT}(R)+d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E),

by the definition of Faltings height. Hence,

\displaystyle\log\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))+\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|-d\log H_{NT}(R)-d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E)+\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|

and

\displaystyle\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}\left(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{4}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|-d\log H_{NT}(R)-d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E)+\log|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|^{1/4}\cdot|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|}{\mathrm{exp}\left(d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E)\right)}\cdot\frac{1}{H_{NT}(R)^{d}}.

∎

B.11.
Some lemmas on
\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}

Recall that after Corollary
B.26
, we want to count points of the lattice

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v},

lying inside the homogeneous region

\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)S_{\Omega,R}(1),

as
B\to\infty
, for each
R\in E(F)
. We will use geometry of numbers to do this counting in Section 13. To control the error term in the geometry of numbers argument, we will show that the boundary
\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)
can be parametrized by finitely many Lipschitz maps with controlled Lipschitz constants in Section 12. In this section, we develop properties of
\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}
, to be used in the next section.

Let
v\in M_{F,\infty}
be an archimedean place.

B.11.1.
Interpretation of
\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}
as Mahler measure

Let
C(\overline{F_{v}})
be a smooth projective curve, and
\mathcal{L}=(L,\|\cdot\|)
a metrized line bundle of class (S) on
C(\overline{F_{v}})
.

Definition B.35

(Mahler measure)
.

Consider a rational function
G:C(\overline{F_{v}})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{F_{v}})
, with

\mathrm{div}(G)=\sum_{\gamma}m_{\gamma}\gamma.

Consider a section
s\in H^{0}(C(\overline{F_{v}}),L)
, with
\mathrm{div}(s)=\sum_{\eta}n_{\eta}\eta
disjoint from
\mathrm{div}(G)
.

We define the Mahler measure of
G
with respect to
\mathcal{L}
to be

M_{v,\mathcal{L},s}(G):=\prod_{\gamma\in\mathrm{Supp}(G)}\|s(\gamma)\|^{-m_{\gamma}}\prod_{\eta\in\mathrm{Supp}(s)}|G(\eta)|_{v}^{n_{\eta}}.

Proposition B.36
.

The Mahler measure has the following properties:

(a)

M_{v,\mathcal{L},s}(G)
does not depend on the section
s
. In fact,

\log M_{v,\mathcal{L},s}(G)=\int_{C(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|G|_{v}c_{1}(\mathcal{L}),

for any section
s
where
\mathrm{div}(s)
and
\mathrm{div}(G)
are disjoint. We may hence denote the Mahler measure as
M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G)
.

(b)

If
G_{1},G_{2}
are two rational functions on
C(\overline{F_{v}})
and
G_{1}G_{2}
is their pointwise product, then

M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G_{1}G_{2})=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G_{1})M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G_{2}).

(c)

(Pushforward) If
\phi:C^{\prime}(\overline{F_{v}})\to C(\overline{F_{v}})
is a morphism, and
G^{\prime}
is a rational function on
C^{\prime}(\overline{F_{v}})
, then

M_{v,\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}}(G^{\prime})=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(\phi_{*}(G^{\prime})).

Here
\phi_{*}:=N_{\mathbb{C}(C(\overline{F_{v}}))/\mathbb{C}(\phi^{*}C^{\prime}(\overline{F_{v}}))}
is the norm map on function fields.

(d)

(Pullback) If
\phi:C^{\prime}(\overline{F_{v}})\to C(\overline{F_{v}})
is a morphism, and
G
is a rational function on
C(\overline{F_{v}})
, then

M_{v,\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}}(\phi^{*}(G))=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G)^{deg(\phi)}.

Here
\phi^{*}(G):=G\circ\phi
is the usual pullback.

Proof.

For part (a), this is
[
44
, Proposition B.2]
; for completeness we sketch the proof here. Let
s
be a nonzero, meromorphic section of
\mathcal{L}
, where
\mathrm{div}(s)
and
\mathrm{div}(G)
are disjoint. Away from
\mathrm{div}(s)
,

c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=\frac{\partial\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\log\|s\|.

For
x\in\mathrm{div}(G)\cup\mathrm{div}(s)
, let
D_{x,\epsilon}
be a small disk around
x
of radius
\epsilon>0
and
C_{x,\epsilon}
be a positively oriented circle around
x
of radius
\epsilon>0
. We take
\epsilon
small enough so that these disks are disjoint. Let

Y_{\epsilon}=C(\overline{F_{v}})-\bigcup_{x\in\mathrm{div}(G)\cup\mathrm{div}(s)}D_{x,\epsilon},

and let

\omega=\log|G|\cdot\frac{\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\log\|s\|+\log\|s\|\cdot\frac{\partial}{\pi i}\log|G|

be a 1-form on
Y_{\epsilon}
. As
G
is meromorphic,
\log|G|
is harmonic on
Y_{\epsilon}
, hence

\displaystyle d\omega=

\displaystyle\log|G|\cdot\frac{\partial\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\log\|s\|+\log\|s\|\cdot\frac{\overline{\partial}\partial}{\pi i}\log|G|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log|G|\cdot c_{1}(\mathcal{L})

on
Y_{\epsilon}
. By Stokes’ theorem,

\int_{Y_{\epsilon}}\log|G|_{v}c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=-\sum_{x\in\mathrm{div}(G)\cup\mathrm{div}(s)}\int_{C_{x,\epsilon}}\omega=-\sum_{\gamma\in\mathrm{div}(G)}\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\omega-\sum_{\eta\in\mathrm{div}(s)}\int_{C_{\eta,\epsilon}}\omega

(the minus sign comes from the fact that applying Stokes’ theorem to the outside of
C_{x,\epsilon}
, so that the orientation is reversed). For
\gamma\in\mathrm{div}(G)
,

\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\log|G|\cdot\frac{\overline{\partial}}{\pi i}\log\|s\|=O(\epsilon\log\epsilon),

and

\displaystyle\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\log\|s\|\cdot\frac{\partial}{\pi i}\log|G|=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\log\|s\|\partial\log(G\overline{G})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\log\|s\|\frac{G^{\prime}(z)}{G(z)}dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,m_{\gamma}\log\|s(\gamma)\|

by residue theorem. Hence

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\omega=m_{\gamma}\log\|s(\gamma)\|.

Similarly for
\eta\in\mathrm{div}(s)
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{C_{\eta,\epsilon}}\omega=-n_{\eta}\log|G(\eta)|.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{C(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|G|c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=

\displaystyle\,\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{Y_{\epsilon}}\log|G|c_{1}(\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(-\sum_{\gamma\in\mathrm{div}(G)}\int_{C_{\gamma,\epsilon}}\omega-\sum_{\eta\in\mathrm{div}(s)}\int_{C_{\eta,\epsilon}}\omega\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\sum_{\gamma\in\mathrm{div}(G)}m_{\gamma}\log\|s(\gamma)\|+\sum_{\eta\in\mathrm{div}(s)}n_{\eta}\log|G(\eta)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\log M_{v,\mathcal{L},s}(G)

as desired.

Part (b) follows by taking a section
s
with
\mathrm{div}(s)
disjoint from both
\mathrm{div}(G_{1})
and
\mathrm{div}(G_{2})
, and the definition of Mahler measure.

For part (c), take a section
s
of
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathrm{div}(s)
disjoint from
\mathrm{div}(\phi_{*}(G^{\prime}))
. Then
\phi^{*}s
is a section of
\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}
and
\mathrm{div}(\phi^{*}s)=\phi^{*}\mathrm{div}(s)
has disjoint support from
\mathrm{div}(G^{\prime})
. By definition,

M_{v,\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}}(G^{\prime})=\prod_{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Supp}(G^{\prime})}\|s(\phi(\gamma^{\prime}))\|^{-m_{\gamma^{\prime}}}\prod_{\eta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Supp}(\phi^{*}s)}|G^{\prime}(\eta^{\prime}))|^{-n_{\eta^{\prime}}}.

Note that
\mathrm{div}(\phi_{*}(G^{\prime}))=\phi_{*}\mathrm{div}(G^{\prime})
, and for
\eta\in C(\overline{F_{v}})
with
\phi^{*}([\eta])=\sum_{\eta^{\prime}}n_{\eta^{\prime}}\eta^{\prime}
, we have
[
48
, Exercise 2.10]

\phi_{*}(G^{\prime})(\eta)=\prod_{\phi(\eta^{\prime})=\eta}G^{\prime}(\eta^{\prime})^{n_{\eta^{\prime}}}.

Hence,

M_{v,\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}}(G^{\prime})=\prod_{\gamma\in\mathrm{Supp}(\phi_{*}(G^{\prime}))}\|s(\gamma)\|^{-m_{\gamma}}\prod_{\eta\in\mathrm{Supp}(s)}|(\phi_{*}G^{\prime})(\eta))|^{-n_{\eta}}=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}\left(\phi_{*}(G^{\prime})\right)

as desired.

For part (d), we apply
G^{\prime}=\phi^{*}G
to part (c). Then

M_{v,\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}}(\phi^{*}G)=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(\phi_{*}\phi^{*}G)=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G^{\mathrm{deg}(\phi)}).

Using property (b), we see that
M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G^{\mathrm{deg}(\phi)})=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G)^{\mathrm{deg}(\phi)}
. Hence,

M_{v,\phi^{*}\mathcal{L}}(\phi^{*}G)=M_{v,\mathcal{L}}(G)^{\mathrm{deg}(\phi)}

as desired.
∎

Example B.37
.

Consider
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{O(1)}
on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with standard metric. If
G:\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a polynomial, i.e.

G([x,y])=[a(x-\alpha_{1}y)\cdots(x-\alpha_{k}y),y^{k}]

where
a\neq 0
, we claim that

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(G)=|a|_{v}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|_{v}\}

recovers the classical Mahler measure of a polynomial.

The reasoning is as follows. Note that
G
has a pole at
[1,0]
of order
k
. For small
\epsilon>0
, one consider the section
s_{\epsilon}
, where

s_{\epsilon}([x_{0},x_{1}])=\frac{\epsilon x_{0}+x_{1}}{x_{i}}

on the open set
x_{i}\neq 0
; this is a section with a simple zero at
[1,-\epsilon]
and no poles. For most
\epsilon
,
\mathrm{div}(s_{\epsilon})
and
\mathrm{div}(G)
are disjoint. Moreover, under the standard norm on
\mathbf{O(1)}
,

\|s_{\epsilon}([x_{0},x_{1}])\|=\frac{|\epsilon x_{0}+x_{1}|_{v}}{\max\{|x_{0}|_{v},|x_{1}|_{v}\}}

By definition, we have

\displaystyle M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)},s_{\epsilon}}(G)=

\displaystyle\,\|s_{\epsilon}([1,0])\|^{k}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\|s_{\epsilon}([\alpha_{i},1])\|^{-1}\cdot|G([1,-\epsilon])|_{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,|\epsilon^{k}G([1,-\epsilon])|_{v}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|\}}{|1+\epsilon\alpha_{i}|_{v}}

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\,|a|_{v}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|_{v}\}

as
\epsilon\to 0
. Since
M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(G)
does not depend on the choice of section
s_{\epsilon}
, we have

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(G)=|a|_{v}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|_{v}\}

as desired.

Corollary B.38
.

Back to our set up, where
X(\overline{F_{v}})
is an elliptic curve, and
f:X(\overline{F_{v}})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{F_{v}})
is a morphism of degree
k
. Then

\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|d\mu_{v}=\frac{1}{k}\log M_{v,f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}(g_{v}).

If we define the rational function
G_{v}:\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{F_{v}})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{F_{v}})
via the norm map

G_{v}\circ f=N_{\mathbb{C}(X(\overline{F_{v}}))/\mathbb{C}(f)}(g_{v}),

then

\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|d\mu_{v}=\frac{1}{k}\log M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(G_{v}).

Proof.

By definition,
d\mu_{v}=\frac{1}{k}c_{1}(f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)})
. Hence, by Proposition
B.36
(a),

\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|d\mu_{v}=\frac{1}{k}\log M_{v,f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}(g_{v}).

The second equality follows from Proposition
B.36
(c).
∎

B.11.2.
Continuity of
\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}
and consequences

The vector space
H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))
vary nicely as
R
varies in
E
; they are the fibers of a vector bundle
\mathcal{N}
over
E
, defined over
F
. (Rigorously, this follows from the existence of Poincare bundle on
E\times\mathrm{Pic}^{2}(E)
.) Base change to
\overline{F_{v}}
gives us the vector bundle
\mathcal{N}\times_{F}\overline{F_{v}}
over
E(\overline{F_{v}})
, whose fiber over
R\in E(\overline{F})
is
H^{0}(E(\overline{F_{v}}),O_{E}(R+O))
.

Each fiber
H^{0}(E(\overline{F_{v}}),O_{E}(R+O))
is equipped with the
L^{2}
-metric
\|\cdot\|_{R,2}
, defined using the Arakelov-Green function (see Definition
B.27
; when the point
R
is clear, we may denote the metric as
\|\cdot\|_{2}
). The smoothness of Arakelov-Green function implies that this
L^{2}
-metric glues to a metric on
\mathcal{N}
. We can thus form the unit sphere bundle
M
over
E(\overline{F_{v}})
. As both the base
E(\overline{F_{v}})
and the fiber are compact,
M
is compact.

Lemma B.39
.

For
g_{v,R}\neq 0\in H^{0}(E(\overline{F_{v}}),O_{E}(R+O))
, we have an error bound

\left|\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}-\log\|g_{v,R}\|_{2}\right|=O_{E,v}(1),

uniform over
g_{v,R}
, and
R\in E(\overline{F_{v}})
.

Proof.

For
g_{v,R}\in H^{0}(E(\overline{F_{v}}),O_{E}(R+O))
with
\|g_{v,R}\|_{2}=1
, note that the map

g_{v,R}\to\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}

is continuous on the compact space
M
(This is clear using the expression as Mahler measure). Hence
\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}
is bounded uniformly over
R\in E(\overline{F_{v}})
and
\|g_{v,R}\|_{2}=1
.

For arbitrary
g_{v,R}\neq 0\in H^{0}(E(\overline{F_{v}}),O_{E}(R+O))
, by normalizing
g_{v,R}
to have norm 1 we have

\left|\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}-\log\|g_{v,R}\|_{2}\right|=\left|\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log\frac{|g_{v,R}|_{v}}{\|g_{v,R}\|_{2}}d\mu_{v}\right|=O_{E,v}(1),

as desired.
∎

B.12.
Lipschitz parametrizability of
\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)

In this section, we will show that the
\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)
is Lipschitz parametrizable uniformly over
R\in E(F)
(Proposition
B.44
). This will be used to control the error term in geometry of numbers argument in next section.

We start with the definition of Lipschitz parametrizability, and its basic properties.

Definition B.40

(Lipschitz parametrizability)
.

A set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension
k
, if there are
M
maps
\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{M}:[0,1]^{n-k}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying the Lipschitz condition

\|\phi_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\phi_{i}(\mathbf{y})\|_{2}\leq L\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|_{2}

for each
i=1,\cdots,M
, such that
S
is covered by the image of
\phi_{i}
.

Definition B.41

(Uniform Lipschitz parametrizability)
.

Let
D
be a set, and suppose we have a family of sets
S_{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
parametrized by
R\in D
.

We say that the family is
uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension
k
over
D
, if each
S_{R}
is Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension
k
, and the constants
M_{R}
(number of parametrizations),
L_{R}
(Lipschitz constant) for each
S_{R}
can be chosen independent of
R
.

Our main tool of showing (uniform) Lipschitz parametrizability is Gromov’s algebraic lemma
[
25
]
; we will use the version stated in
[
8
]
. This lemma was first used in
[
2
]
in similar problems.

Recall that a semi-algebraic set of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a finite union of sets defined by polynomial equalities or inequalities. The degree of a semi-algebraic set is the smallest sum of degree of polynomials appearing in a complete description of the set.

Theorem B.42

(Gromov’s algebraic lemma,
[
8
, Theorem 1]
)
.

For all integers
r\geq 1
,
m\geq 0
,
E\geq 0
, there exists
M(m,r,E)<\infty
with the following properties.

For any compact semialgebraic subset
A\subset[0,1]^{m}
, of dimension
n
, and degree at most
E
, there exists an integer
N\leq M(m,r,E)
and
C^{r}
-maps
\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}:[0,1]^{n}\to[0,1]^{m}
such that

•

\bigcup_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{i}([0,1]^{n})=A
, and

•

\|\phi_{i}\|_{r}:=\max_{\beta:|\beta|\leq r}\|\partial^{\beta}\phi_{i}\|\leq 1
.

Corollary B.43
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. Suppose
A_{R}\subset\mathbb{F}^{m}
is a family of compact semialgebraic subsets parametrized by
R\in D
, such that

•

Each
A_{R}
lies inside a ball of diameter
l
, independent of
R\in D
.

•

Each
A_{R}
is of dimension
n
, with degree at most
E
, both of which independent of
R\in D
.

Then
A_{R}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable over
R\in D
.

Proof.

Translating if necessary, suppose
A_{R}\subset[0,l]^{d}
. Apply Gromov’s algebraic lemma with
r=1
to
\frac{1}{l}A_{R}\subset[0,1]^{d}
, we find
\phi_{1},\cdots,\phi_{N}:[0,1]^{n}\to\frac{1}{l}A_{R}
with first derivative bounded by 1; hence
\phi_{i}
are Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant
\sqrt{d}
. With our assumptions,
N
does not depend on
R\in D
.

Consider the maps
l\phi_{1},\cdots,l\phi_{N}:[0,1]^{n}\to A_{R}
. Then there are
N
Lipschitz maps of Lipschitz constants
l\sqrt{d}
that covers
A_{R}
for each
R\in D
. Moreover, the number of maps/Lipschitz constants are independent of
R\in D
. Hence we proved the desired uniform Lipschitz parametrizability.
∎

Recall Definition
B.21
, where for
R\in E(F)
we defined

\displaystyle S_{\Omega,R}(1)=

\displaystyle\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}:\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right\}

The main result in this section is as follows.

Proposition B.44
.

For each
R\in E(F)
,
\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)
can be covered by
W
maps
\phi:[0,1]^{2d-1}\to\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O))
satisfying

\|\phi(\mathbf{x_{1}})-\phi(\mathbf{x_{2}})\|_{2}\leq L\|\mathbf{x_{1}}-\mathbf{x_{2}}\|_{2}.

Moreover, we can take
W,L=O_{E,f,F}(1)
independent of
R
. In other words,
\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 1 over
R\in E(F)
.

The rest of the section is devoted to proving this result.

Remark
.

Strictly speaking, we need to fix an isomorphism of vector spaces

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O))\cong\mathbb{R}^{2d}

before discussing Lipschitz parametrizability. We will fix such an isomorphism in the course of proof.

By definition,

\displaystyle\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\partial\left(\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega\cup\left(\partial\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega\right\}

\displaystyle\bigcup\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\partial\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right\}

Let

T_{R,1}:=\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega\right\},

T_{R,2}:=\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\partial\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right\}.

We will focus on proving the Lipschitz parametrizability of
T_{R,1}
. The second set can be handled similarly, and we will sketch the proof at the end of this section.

We will now fix an isomorphism of vector spaces

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O))\cong\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

For each
v\in M_{F,\infty}
, locally around
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
we consider a basis
g_{v,R_{v},1},g_{v,R_{v},2}
of
H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R_{v}+O))
that varies smoothly with
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
(Section
B.11.2
). For
g_{v,R_{v}}\in H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R_{v}+O))
, we may then write

g_{v,R_{v}}=a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}

for some
a_{v},b_{v}\in F_{v}
. This gives us an isomorphism of vector spaces

H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R_{v}+O))\cong F_{v}^{2}

defined by
g_{v,R_{v}}\to(a_{v},b_{v})
. Since
E(F_{v})
is compact, we only need to consider a finite open cover of
E(F_{v})
, each of which has coordinates
g_{v,R_{v},1},g_{v,R_{v},2}
for
H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R_{v}+O))
locally.

In the following, we use
|\cdot|
to represent the Euclidean norm on
F_{v}
, and
|\cdot|_{v}
to represent the normalized absolute value on
F_{v}
. In particular when
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{C}
,
|\cdot|
would mean the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{C}
, while
|\cdot|_{v}
would mean Euclidean norm squared.

Proposition B.45
.

To show that
T_{R,1}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 1 over
R\in E(F)
, it suffices to show that for
\delta>0
,

T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}:=\left\{((a_{v},b_{v}))\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}F_{v}^{2}:\begin{array}[]{l}\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right)=1,\\
\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\text{ for each $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{array}\right\}

is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 1 over
(R_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
. Here
N_{v,f}:=N_{\mathbb{C}(E(\overline{F_{v}}))/f^{*}\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\overline{F_{v}}))}
is the norm map on function fields, induced by
f:E\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Using
(a_{v},b_{v})
coordinates, the Lipschitz parametrizability of
T_{R,1}
is equivalent to that of

\left\{((a_{v},b_{v}))\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}F_{v}^{2}:\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|a_{v}g_{v,R,1}+b_{v}g_{v,R,2}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega\right\}

for
R\in E(F)
, embedded diagonally inside
\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
. We will show more generally that

(B.2)

\left\{((a_{v},b_{v}))\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}F_{v}^{2}:\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\Omega\right\}

is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable over
(R_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
.

Hence consider
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
, and let

g_{v,R_{v}}=a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}

for some
a_{v},b_{v}\in F_{v}
. One has

\frac{\|a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}\|_{2}}{(|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2})^{1/2}}\asymp_{R_{v}}1

for each
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
, since norms on Euclidean space are equivalent. Using compactness of
E(F_{v})
and Lemma
B.39
, we have

(B.3)

\left|\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}|_{v}d\mu_{v}-\frac{1}{2}\log(|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2})\right|=O_{E,v}(1)

independent of
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
.

Note that

•

By Equation
B.3
and the compactness of
\Omega
, we see that
((a_{v},b_{v}))
lies in the set
B.2
would imply that

\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},

for some
\delta>0
, uniformly over
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
.

•

By definition,
\Omega
has sum of coordinates equal 0. So
((a_{v},b_{v}))
lies in the set
B.2
would imply that

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}|_{v}d\mu_{v}=0.

By property (a) and (c) of Mahler measure (Proposition
B.36
),

\displaystyle\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}|_{v}d\mu_{v}=

\displaystyle\,\log M_{v,f^{*}\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\log M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right).

Hence

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}|_{v}d\mu_{v}=0

is equivalent to

\sum_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\log M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right)=0.

In other words,

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right)=1.

Therefore the set
B.2
is a subset of
T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}
, and thus the Lipschitz parametrizability of
T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}
would imply that of the set
B.2
, hence that of
T_{R,1}
.
∎

Let
G_{v,R_{v}}
be the rational function on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
defined by the norm map of function fields

G_{v,R_{v}}\circ f=N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2}).

Since
g_{v,R_{v},1},g_{v,R_{v},2}
is of degree
\leq 2
and has at worst a pole at
R_{v}
and
O
, we see that
G_{v,R_{v}}
is also degree
\leq 2
, with at worst a pole at
f(R_{v})
and
f(O)
. Moreover, as
f
has degree
k
, we can write

G_{v,R_{v}}=a_{v}^{k}G_{v,R_{v},0}+a_{v}^{k-1}b_{v}G_{v,R_{v},1}+\cdots+b_{v}^{k}G_{v,R_{v},k}

where
G_{v,R_{v},0},\cdots,G_{v,R_{v},k}
are degree
\leq 2
rational functions on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, with at worst a pole at
f(R_{v})
and
f(O)
, that varies holomorphically with
R_{v}
. We can thus write

G_{v,R_{v}}=\frac{E_{v,R_{v}}}{(z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))}

for some polynomials
E_{v,R_{v}}
of degree
\leq 2
, where

E_{v,R_{v}}(z)=H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})+H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})z+H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})z^{2}

has coefficients
H_{v,R_{v},0},H_{v,R_{v},1},H_{v,R_{v},2}
, each of which a homogeneous degree
k
polynomial in
F_{v}[a_{v},b_{v}]
, whose coefficient varies smoothly in
R_{v}
. Shrinking the neighborhood for local basis if needed, we can assume that coefficients of
H_{v,R_{v},0},H_{v,R_{v},1},H_{v,R_{v},2}\ll 1
uniformly over
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
, since
E(F_{v})
is compact.

With this framing,

\displaystyle T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,\left\{((a_{v},b_{v}))\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}F_{v}^{2}:\begin{array}[]{l}\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(G_{v,R_{v}})\right)=1,\\
\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\text{ for each $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\left\{((a_{v},b_{v}))\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}F_{v}^{2}:\begin{array}[]{l}\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(E_{v,R_{v}})\right)\\
=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left((z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))\right),\\
\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\text{ for each $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{array}\right\},

by the multiplicativity of Mahler measure (Proposition
B.36
(b)).

Lemma B.46
.

If
((a_{v},b_{v}))\in T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}
, then for each
v\in M_{F,\infty}
we have

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\asymp_{k,\delta}1,

independent of
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
.

Proof.

Suppose

E_{v,R_{v}}(z)=H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})+H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})z+H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})z^{2}

has two roots
\alpha
and
\beta
. By definition of Mahler measure, we always have

\displaystyle M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=

\displaystyle\,|H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\max\{1,|\alpha|_{v}\}\max\{1,|\beta|_{v}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,|H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}|\alpha|_{v}|\beta|_{v}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}.

Since
H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})
is homogeneous of degree
k
,
|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\ll_{\delta}1
, and the coefficients of
H_{v,R_{v},0}
are
\ll 1
, we have

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\leq|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\ll_{k,\delta}1,

uniformly over
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
.

For any given place
v\in M_{F,\infty}
, apply the forementioned upper bound of
M_{w,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{w,R_{v}})
for all
w\neq v
to the condition

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right)=1,

we see that the other inequality
M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\gg_{k,\delta}1
is also true.
∎

Proposition B.47
.

For each archimedean place
v\in M_{F,\infty}
, the set

T_{v}:=\left\{(a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\asymp_{k,\delta}1\right\}

can be covered by the image of
\ll_{k,\delta}1
maps
\phi
, which satisfies:

•

Domain of
\phi
is a compact semi-algebraic set with degree
\ll_{k,\delta}1
, lying inside a ball of diameter
\ll_{k,\delta}1
, with ambient space having dimension over
\mathbb{R}

\ll 1
.

•

The map
\phi
is a polynomial map, which is Lipschitz on the domain with Lipschitz constant
\ll 1
.

•

Each point
(a_{v},b_{v})
lies in the image of some map
\phi
, say
\phi(\vec{x})=(a_{v},b_{v})
, such that

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\circ\phi(\vec{x})=|M_{\phi}(\vec{x})|

for a polynomial map
M_{\phi}
of degree
\ll_{k}1
.

Moreover, all the constants above are independent of
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
.

Proof.

We will stratify

\{(a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\}

for each
R_{v}\in E(F_{v})
, based on the degree of
E_{v,R_{v}}
, and whether the two roots of
E_{v,R_{v}}(a_{v},b_{v})
has norm less than or greater than 1.

Case 1:
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=0
.
In this case,
E_{v,R_{v}}(z)\equiv H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})
, and
M_{v}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}
. This can be covered by
\phi\equiv id
on the domain

\{a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1\}.

We verify this map satisfies the conditions we need.

•

Recall that
H_{v,R_{v},0}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
; varying
R_{v}
affects the coefficients but not the degree. Squaring if necessary, the condition
|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1
can be defined by two inequalities of a polynomial of degree
2k
. Hence the domain of
\phi
is semi-algebraic with degree
\ll_{k}1
.

•

The domain lies inside
\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}
, so it lies inside a ball of diameter
\ll_{\delta}1
.

•

The domain lies inside
F_{v}^{2}
, with dimension over
\mathbb{R}
at most 4.

•

\phi=id
is clearly a polynomial map, and is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant 1.

•

(a_{v},b_{v})
covered in this case satisfies

M_{v}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v},

so we may take
M_{\phi}=H_{v,R_{v},0}
(when
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{R}
), or
H_{v,R_{v},0}^{2}
(when
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{C}
). In either case,
M_{\phi}
has degree
\ll_{k}1
.

Case 2:
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=1
.
In this case,

E_{v,R_{v}}(z)=H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})+H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})z

has a unique root
\alpha=-\frac{H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})}{H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})}
. In this case, Mahler measure equals

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\max\{1,|\alpha|_{v}\}.

Case 2(a):
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=1
, and the root
\alpha
has norm
\leq 1
.
In this case,

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}.

As in case 1, this can be covered by
\phi\equiv id
on the domain

\{a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1\}.

Case 2(b):
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=1
, and the root
\alpha
has norm
\geq 1
.
In this case,

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}.

As in case 1, this can be covered by
\phi\equiv id
on the domain

\{a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1\}.

Case 3:
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=2
.
In that case,

E_{v,R_{v}}(z)=H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})+H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})z+H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})z^{2}

has two roots
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
. In this case, Mahler measure equals

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\max\{1,|\alpha_{1}|_{v}\}\max\{1,|\alpha_{2}|_{v}\}.

Case 3(a):
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=2
, and both
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
has norm
\leq 1
.
In this case,

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}.

As in case 1, this can be covered by
\phi\equiv id
on the domain

\{a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1\}.

Case 3(b):
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=2
, and both
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
has norm
\geq 1
.
In this case,

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}.

As in case 1, this can be covered by
\phi\equiv id
on the domain

\{a_{v},b_{v})\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1\}.

Case 3(c):
\mathrm{deg}(E_{v,R_{v}})=2
,
|\alpha_{1}|_{v}\geq 1\geq|\alpha_{2}|_{v}
.

In this case,

M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})\alpha_{1}|_{v}.

Let
u=H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})\alpha_{1}
. Then

|u|_{v}=M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\asymp_{k,\delta}1,

and as
\alpha_{1}
is a root of
E_{v,R_{v}}
,
u
satisfies the quadratic equation

Q_{R_{v}}(a_{v},b_{v},u):=H_{v,R_{v},0}(a_{v},b_{v})H_{v,R_{v},2}(a_{v},b_{v})+H_{v,R_{v},1}(a_{v},b_{v})u+u^{2}=0.

Define
\phi(a_{v},b_{v},u):=(a_{v},b_{v})
from the domain

\left\{(a_{v},b_{v},u)\in F_{v}^{2}:\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|u|_{v}\asymp_{k,\delta}1,Q_{R_{v}}(a_{v},b_{v},u)=0\right\}.

We verify this map satisfies the conditions we need.

•

Recall that
Q_{R_{v}}
is a polynomial of degree
2k
; varying
R_{v}
affects the coefficients but not the degree. So the domain of
\phi
is semi-algebraic with degree
\ll_{k}1
.

•

The domain lies inside
\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1},|u|\asymp_{k,\delta}1
, so it lies inside a ball of diameter
\ll_{k,\delta}1
.

•

The domain lies inside
F_{v}^{3}
, with dimension over
\mathbb{R}
at most 6.

•

\phi(a_{v},b_{v},u)=(a_{v},b_{v})
is clearly a polynomial map, and is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant 1.

•

(a_{v},b_{v})
covered in this case satisfies

M_{v}(E_{v,R_{v}})=|u|_{v},

so we may take
M_{\phi}=u
(when
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{R})
or
u^{2}
(when
F_{v}\cong\mathbb{C}
). In either case,
M_{\phi}
has degree
\ll_{k}1
.

∎

Proposition B.48
.

T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 1 over
(R_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
. Hence by Proposition
B.45
,
T_{R,1}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable over
R\in E(F)
.

Proof.

Recall from equation
B.12
that

T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}=\left\{((a_{v},b_{v}))\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}F_{v}^{2}:\begin{array}[]{l}\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(E_{v,R_{v}})\right)\\
=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left((z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))\right),\\
\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\text{ for each $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{array}\right\}.

By Lemma
B.46
and Proposition
B.47
, one can cover
T_{(R_{v}),\delta,1}^{\prime}
with
\ll_{k,\delta,F}1
maps of the shape
\Phi:=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\phi_{v}
, that satisfies:

•

Domain of
\Phi
is a compact semi-algebraic set with degree
\ll_{k,\delta,F}1
, lying inside a ball of diameter
\ll_{k,\delta,F}1
, with ambient space having dimension over
\mathbb{R}\ll_{F}1
.

•

The map
\phi
is a polynomial map, and is Lipschitz with Lipschitz constant
\ll_{F}1
.

•

Each point
((a_{v},b_{v}))
lies in the image of some map
\Phi
, say
\Phi(\vec{x})=((a_{v},b_{v}))
, such that

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}(E_{v,R_{v}})\circ\Phi(\vec{x})=\left|\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{\phi_{v}}(\vec{x})\right|

for a polynomial map
\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{\phi_{v}}
of degree
\ll_{k,F}1
.

•

Moreover, the constants here are all independent of
(R_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
.

This is a set of one dimension higher than what we want, because so far we only utilized the constraint
M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(E_{v,R_{v}})\right)\asymp_{k,\delta}1
. We now try to carve out the condition

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(E_{v,R_{v}})\right)=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left((z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))\right)

as well. For a parametrization
\Phi=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\phi_{v}
, the condition becomes

\left|\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{\phi_{v}}(\vec{x})\right|=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left((z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))\right).

It now suffices to show that

\left\{\vec{x}\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}Domain(\phi_{v}):\begin{array}[]{l}\left|\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{\phi_{v}}(\vec{x})\right|=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left((z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))\right),\\
\delta\leq|\phi_{v}(\vec{x})|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\text{ for each $v\in M_{F,\infty}$.}\end{array}\right\}

is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 1 over
(R_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
. Since

\left|\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{\phi_{v}}(\vec{x})\right|=\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left((z-f(R_{v}))(z-f(O))\right)

is an algebraic condition on
\vec{x}
(after squaring), and that
R_{v}
only ever affects the coefficients (but not the degree) of the involved polynomial, the uniform Lipschitz parametrizability follows from Corollary
B.43
to Gromov’s algebraic lemma.
∎

Proof of Proposition
B.44
.

Recall the discussion right after Proposition
B.44
, where we defined
T_{R,1}
and
T_{R,2}
and showed that

\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)\subset T_{R,1}\cup T_{R,2}.

To show uniform Lipschitz parametrizability of
\partial S_{\Omega,R}(1)
, it suffices to show the that for
T_{R,1}
and
T_{R,2}
.

We just showed the uniform Lipschitz parametrizability of
T_{R,1}
in Proposition
B.48
. One can show that
T_{R,2}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable in a similar way; we sketch the argument as follows.

Recall that

T_{R,2}:=\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\partial\Omega+(-\infty,0]\delta\right\}.

If there is only one infinite place (i.e.
r_{1}+r_{2}=1
), then
\Omega
is a point, and
T_{R,2}
is empty; the Lipschitz parametrizability of
T_{R,2}
is then vacuously true. Henceforth we will assume there are at least two infinite places.

Let

T^{\prime}_{R,2}=\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in\partial\Omega\right\}.

We first show that
T^{\prime}_{R,2}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 2 over
R\in E(F)
.

•

Since
\Omega
is a parallelepiped in
\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}
, we see that
\partial\Omega
is the union of
2(r_{1}+r_{2}-1)\ll_{F}1
parallelepiped. It suffices to show that for each parallelepiped
P
, the set

T^{\prime}_{R,2,P}=\left\{(g_{v,R})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in P\right\}

is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable over
R\in E(F)
.

As in Proposition
B.45
we strive to prove instead the larger set

T^{\prime}_{(R_{v}),2,P}=\left\{(g_{v,R_{v}})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E(F_{v}),O_{E}(R_{v}+O)):\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R_{v}}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in P\right\}

is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable over
(R_{v})\in\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}E(F_{v})
.

•

For each parallelepiped
P
, similar to Proposition
B.45
, the condition

\left(\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v,R_{v}}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\right)\in P

is covered by

\delta\leq|a_{v}|^{2}+|b_{v}|^{2}\leq\delta^{-1}\text{ for each $v\in M_{F,\infty}$},

and two conditions on Mahler measures:

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right)=1

and

\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}M_{v,\mathbf{O(1)}}\left(N_{v,f}(a_{v}g_{v,R_{v},1}+b_{v}g_{v,R_{v},2})\right)^{d_{v}}=1

for some
(d_{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
linearly independent with
(1,\cdots,1)
.

•

Proposition
B.47
still applies. Using those parametrizations there, we need to show uniform Lipschitz parametrizability of a codimension 2 set, similar to Proposition
B.48
.

As before, varying
(R_{v})
only affects the coefficients of polynomials defining the semi-algebraic set, but not the degrees. Hence uniform Lipschitz parametrizability follows from Corollary
B.43
to Gromov’s algebraic lemma.

Finally, if
\psi:[0,1]^{r_{1}+r_{2}-2}\to T^{\prime}_{R,2}
is a Lipschitz map, then the map
\widetilde{\psi}:[0,1]^{r_{1}+r_{2}-2}\times(0,1]\to T_{R,2}

defined by

\widetilde{\psi}(\mathbf{x},u)=u\cdot\psi(\mathbf{x})

is clearly Lipschitz. By Lipschitz continuity, one can extend
\widetilde{\psi}
to
[0,1]^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}
. The image of
\psi
’s covers
T_{R,2}
, and the number of maps/Lipschitz constant are still uniform over
R\in E(F)
. This shows that
T_{R,2}
is uniformly Lipschitz parametrizable of codimension 1 over
R\in E(F)
, and wraps up the proof of our main result Proposition
B.44
.
∎

B.13.
Point counting the lattice
H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))

Recall that after Corollary
B.26
, we want to count points of the lattice

H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\cong\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v},

lying inside the homogeneous region

\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)S_{\Omega,R}(1),

as
B\to\infty
, for each
R\in E(F)
. The main goal of this section is Corollary
B.52
, which provides an asymptotic formula for this point count.

We start with a version of Lipschitz principle, as stated in Masser-Vaaler
[
36
]
.

Lemma B.49

(
[
36
, Lemma 2]
)
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{D}
be a bounded set whose boundary
\partial S
can be covered by the image of at most
W
maps from
[0,1]^{D-1}
to
\mathbb{R}^{D}
satisfying Lipschitz conditions

\|\phi(x_{1})-\phi(x_{2})\|_{2}\leq L\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|_{2}.

Then
S
is Lebesgue measurable. Further, let
\Lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
be a lattice with first successive minimum
\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)
. Then the number
Z
of points in
S\cap\Lambda
satisfies

\left|Z-\frac{\mathrm{Vol}(S)}{\mathrm{covol}(\Lambda)}\right|\leq cW\left(\frac{L}{\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)}+1\right)^{D-1}

for some
c=c(D)
depending only on
D
.

Proposition B.50
.

For each
R\in E(F)
, if
T\ll_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/2k}
with a sufficiently small big-
O
constant, then

\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in TS_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}=0.

On the other hand, if
T\gg_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/k}
, then

\displaystyle\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in TS_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))}{\mathrm{covol}_{Fal}(H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O})))}T^{2d}+O_{E,f,F}\left(\left(H(f(R))H(f(O))\right)^{(2d-1)/k}T^{2d-1}\right).

as
T\to\infty
.

Proof.

The first part follows from Proposition
B.29
.

For the second part, we apply Lemma
B.49
to
S=S(T)=TS_{\Omega,R}(1)
,
\Lambda=H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))
,
D=2d
. As
T
varies, note that

S(T)=TS(1),\,\mathrm{Vol}(S(T))=T^{2d}\mathrm{Vol}(S(1)),\,W(T)=W(1),\,L(T)=TL(1).

Moreover, Proposition
B.28
gives us a lower bound

\lambda_{1}(\Lambda)\gg_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/k},

hence the error term in Lemma
B.49
contributes

O_{E,f,F}\left(W\left(LT(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{1/k}+1\right)^{2d-1}\right).

Here we used the uniformity of
W
and
L
from Proposition
B.44
, so that
W,L
are independent of
R\in E(F)
.

The condition
T\gg_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/k}
guarantees that

LT(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{1/k}\gg_{E,f,F}1.

Hence the error term contributes

O_{E,f,F}\left(W\left(LT(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{1/k}\right)^{2d-1}\right)=O_{E,f,F}\left(\left(H(f(R))H(f(O))\right)^{(2d-1)/k}T^{2d-1}\right).

Finally, note that any Haar measure on
\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}H^{0}(E,O_{E}(R+O))\otimes_{F}F_{v}
gives the same main term; in particular, we can use Faltings volume as desired.
∎

We next evaluate
\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))
that appears in the numerator of main term.

Lemma B.51
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}Reg_{F}\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\left\{g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right\}\right).

Proof.

The same argument in
[
36
, Lemma 4]
gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}\frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{Lebesgue}(\Omega)}{(r_{1}+r_{2})^{1/2}}\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\left\{g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\right.\right.

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\,\left.\left.\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right\}\right).

Here recall that
\Omega\subset\{x_{1}+\cdots+x_{r_{1}+r_{2}}=0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
is a fundamental domain for the log embedding for
O_{F}^{\times}
.

Finally, note that the regulator of
F
equals

Reg_{F}=\frac{\mathrm{Vol}_{Lebesgue}(\Omega)}{(r_{1}+r_{2})^{1/2}}.

This is because the regulator is the absolute value of any maximal subdeterminant of the matrix with
r_{1}+r_{2}-1
rows and
r_{1}+r_{2}
columns for the log embedding of units, while the volume
\mathrm{Vol}(\Omega)
is the square root of the sum of squares of these same subdeterminants. Since all such subdeterminants are the same, the formula for regulator of
F
follows, and this completes the proof.
∎

Substituting the calculated volumes (Lemma
B.51
, Proposition
B.34
) into Proposition
B.50
, we get

Corollary B.52
.

For each
R\in E(F)
, if
T\ll_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/2k}
with a sufficiently small big-
O
constant, then

\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in TS_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}=0.

On the other hand, if
T\gg_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/k}
, then

\displaystyle\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in TS_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T^{2d}\cdot\frac{2^{r_{1}+r_{2}-1}Reg_{F}}{|\mathrm{disc}(O_{F})|}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{exp}\left(d\cdot h_{Faltings}(E)\right)}{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\Delta_{E/F})|^{1/4}}\cdot H_{NT}(R)^{d}\cdot

\displaystyle\,\,\,\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{F}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right)

\displaystyle\,\,\,+O_{E,f,F}\left(T^{2d-1}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{(2d-1)/k}\right),

as
T\to\infty
.

For later purpose, we prove an upper bound of
\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))
, uniform over
R\in E(F)
.

Proposition B.53
.

We have a uniform bound over
R\in E(F)
,

\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))\ll_{E,F}1.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.51
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(S_{\Omega,R}(1))

\displaystyle\ll_{F}

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right)

Now for each archimedean place
v
, by Lemma
B.39
, the set

\left\{g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right\}

lies in a ball
B_{R,v,r}\subset H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}
, with radius
r\ll_{E,v}1
, bounded uniformly over
R\in E(F)
. Hence

\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\left\{g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right\}\right)\ll_{E,v}\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(B_{R,v,1}).

But by Proposition
B.11
, we saw that the volume of unit ball
\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}(B_{R,v,1})
is bounded above, uniformly over
R\in E(F)
. Thus for each non-archimedean place
v
, we have

\mathrm{Vol}_{Fal}\left(\left\{g_{v}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))\otimes_{O_{F}}F_{v}:\int_{X(\overline{F_{v}})}\log|g_{v}|_{v}d\mu_{v}\leq 0\right\}\right)\ll_{E,v}1.

Multiplying the bound over all archimedean places
v
, we get the desired result.
∎

B.14.
Proof of Proposition
6.4

We now have all the ingredients to prove Proposition
6.4
.

Proof.

By Proposition
B.19
, Corollary
B.23
and Corollary
B.26
, we reframed the problem of counting points on
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E(F)
to counting rational functions on
X
with prescribed poles as follows.

\displaystyle\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mu_{F}|}\sum_{R\in E(F)}\sum_{(a)\subset O_{F}}\mu((a))\#\left\{g_{X}\in H^{0}(X,O_{X}(D_{R}+D_{O}))-0:\right.

\displaystyle\left.\,\,\,(g_{X})_{v\in M_{F,\infty}}\in\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)S_{\Omega,R}(1)\right\}

By Corollary
B.52
, this is really a finite sum, where we only count
a
’s satisfying

\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)\gg_{E,f,F}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{-1/(2k)}.

This is equivalent to

|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|^{1/d}(H(f(R))H(f(O)))^{1/(2k)}\ll_{E,f,F}B^{1/k}.

In particular, these implies that
|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\ll_{E,f,F}B^{d/k}
, and
H(f(R))\ll_{E,f,F}B^{2}
.

For the
a
’s that show up in the sum, Corollary
B.52
applied to

T=\mathrm{exp}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(\log B-\log H(f(R))-\log H(f(O))\right)-\frac{1}{d}\log|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\right)

shows that

\displaystyle\#\{x\in(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}E)(F):H_{\mathcal{L}}(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mu_{F}|}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\in E(F)}}{{H(f(R))\ll B^{2}}}}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)\subset O_{F}}}{{|N_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(a)|\ll B^{d/k}}}}\mu((a))
